Bauru International Craniofacial Symposium: Comprehensive Care, Policy and Advocacy Proceedings
Published: 2026

Abstract

In March 2024, a two-day international symposium was held in São Paulo, Brazil, to address regional health priorities and gaps in surgical care for rare craniofacial disorders, with a focus on syndromic craniosynostosis, particularly Apert syndrome. The event was co-organized by a patient’s family, São Paulo University, Boston Children’s Hospital, and the Brazilian Team of the Program in Global Surgery and Social Change at Harvard Medical School, with support from Necker Hospital (Paris), the Brazilian Ministry of Health, and the former Vice President of Ecuador.

The discussions took a broad and practical approach to improving care for individuals with craniofacial disorders, covering surgical techniques, case presentations, multidisciplinary management, and systems-level strategies. Topics included policy and advocacy contributions from healthcare professionals and patient partners, patient-centered care, and access barriers across public and private hospitals in Brazil, the United States, Ecuador, and France. Speakers represented diverse specialties, including neurosurgery, neurology, anesthesiology, dentistry, orthopedics, plastic and general surgery, primary care, pediatrics, genetics, and obstetrics, with participation from several regions across Brazil. The symposium highlighted the need for coordinated care pathways, robust data systems, and greater inclusion of patient experiences to guide national and regional planning for rare surgical conditions.

This proceedings document captures the range of perspectives and insights shared during the event. Given the limited global attention to rare craniofacial diseases, particularly in low-resource settings, we believe it is essential to make this knowledge accessible. The content reflects a rare convergence of technical, clinical, policy, and lived-experience viewpoints, offering a uniquely holistic foundation for ongoing collaboration and progress in this field.

Keywords

Craniosynostosis, Rare Diseases, Apert, Crouzon, Global Health Strategies, Multidisciplinary care, Health Equity, Policy Making, Stakeholder Participation, Surgical Procedures, Universal Health Care, Global Surgery

Beardmore Division of Pediatric Surgery, The Montreal Children’s Hospital, McGill University Health Centre, Montreal, Quebec, Canada; 10Postgraduate Program in Rehabilitation Sciences, Hospital de Reabilitação de Anomalias Craniofaciais, Universidade de São Paulo, Bauru, SP, Brazil; 11Centro Universitário São Camilo, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 12Instituto de Educação Médica (IDOMED/Estácio), Vista Carioca Campus, Rio de Janeiro, RJ, Brazil; 13Kursk State Medical University, Kursk, Kurskaya Oblast, Russia; 14Universidade São Francisco, Bragança Paulista, SP, Brazil; 15Department of Neurosurgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):1

A 2-day symposium addressing craniofacial disorders created momentum to discuss this crucial yet neglected health issue. The event featured morning sessions with a clinical focus, particularly syndromic craniosynostosis, including Apert syndrome. These sessions explored and compared different treatment protocols from multiple centers in Brazil, Boston Children’s Hospital, and Necker Hospital in Paris, offering a comprehensive look at various approaches to care. Additionally, there were targeted discussions on sub-specialty care, addressing the full spectrum of needs for patients with complex syndromic craniofacial conditions. The primary aim of these morning gatherings was to facilitate the sharing of clinical experiences and, more critically, to establish a foundation for future clinical and research collaborations.

In the afternoons, the symposium pivoted towards policy and advocacy, examining Brazil’s current approach to managing craniofacial disorders, including challenges and barriers to care. Discussions covered a range of issues, from workforce and infrastructure to funding, policy, and governance, focusing on identifying gaps in the public and private management of these conditions. The sessions also looked at ways Brazil could learn from global surgical successes to strengthen its surgical systems and move towards providing universal access to safe, affordable care for patients with craniofacial disorders. Experts in healthcare policy, delivery, and governance, alongside patient and family advocacy organizations and NGOs, were invited to contribute to these discussions. The objective for the afternoon sessions was to identify current care challenges and to engage in a dialogue that could lead to meaningful policy-making and governance reforms in Brazil, ensuring safe, affordable, and high-quality care for all individuals and families affected by craniofacial conditions.

2 General aspects of syndromic craniosynostosis

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):2

Moderators: Dr. John G. Meara and Cristiano Tonello

2.1 Overview of syndromic craniosynostosis

Nivaldo Alonso1,2

1Department of Surgery, Faculty of Medicine, São Paulo University, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 2Craniofacial Surgery Division. Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):2.1

Craniosynostosis is a congenital condition marked by the premature fusion of one or more cranial sutures, resulting in impairment of brain development and abnormally shaped skull.1,2,3 The incidence of craniosynostosis is approximately 1 in 2,500 live births, with variations influenced by genetic predispositions and environmental factors.2 This condition can present as either non-syndromic or syndromic.3 Syndromic craniosynostosis accounts for 9% to 40% of cases and is often associated with a spectrum of other developmental anomalies, including limb abnormalities, cardiac defects, and airway issues. 3

Management strategies for craniosynostosis involve early surgical intervention to correct the cranial deformity, prevent intracranial pressure and respiratory complications, and optimize cosmetic outcomes.1,2,3 The timing and type of surgical procedures vary depending on the specific sutures involved and the presence of syndromic conditions.2,3 This underscores the necessity of a multidisciplinary approach for optimal management.4,5

The effective management of craniosynostosis requires a coordinated effort from various medical specialties.4,5 Pediatric neurosurgeons and craniofacial surgeons are integral in performing the delicate and complex surgical procedures needed to correct skull deformities.4,5 Pediatricians play a crucial role in early detection and ongoing child development monitoring.4,5 Geneticists diagnose and counsel families, particularly in syndromic cases where craniosynostosis may be part of a broader genetic syndrome. Anesthesiologists ensure the child’s safety during surgery, which can be extensive and complex.4,5

Additionally, ophthalmologists may be required to address any associated vision problems, while otolaryngologists manage any related airway issues.4,5 Speech and developmental therapists provide support for neurocognitive development, which may be affected by the condition.4,5 Psychologists and social workers offer essential psychosocial support to the child and family, addressing the emotional and social challenges that can arise.4,5

Craniosynostosis represents a multifaceted condition that demands comprehensive care from a diverse team of specialists.3,4,5 Their combined expertise ensures the successful surgical correction of cranial deformities and the overall well-being and development of the affected children.

2.2 Genetics in syndromic craniosynostosis

Nancy Mizue Kokitsu-Nakata1,2

1Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Hospital for the Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, University of São Paulo, São Paulo, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):2.2

Introduction

Craniosynostosis refers to the premature fusion of cranial sutures and represents the most common congenital anomaly after cleft lip and palate. When it occurs in isolation, without associated anomalies or psychomotor developmental delay, it is classified as nonsyndromic craniosynostosis, accounting for approximately 80% of cases.1 Within this group, sagittal synostosis (also known as scaphocephaly) is the most prevalent, comprising about 50% of cases, with 90% of these being nonsyndromic. In contrast, multisuture synostoses, particularly bicoronal synostosis, are typically associated with syndromic forms.

The etiology of craniosynostosis is heterogeneous, encompassing monogenic, polygenic, and environmental factors.2,3 Seven genes are most commonly implicated in syndromic craniosynostosis: FGFR2, FGFR3, TWIST1, TCF12, SMAD6, ERF, and EFNB1, though additional, less frequent genetic contributors exist.4,5,6 Notably, SMAD6 variants have been identified in both syndromic and nonsyndromic cases, suggesting a phenotypic continuum rather than a strict dichotomy.5,7,1

Accurate diagnosis requires a strategic approach, including phenotypic assessment and targeted genetic testing of the seven key genes, with particular attention to bicoronal synostosis. In settings where genetic testing is inaccessible, diagnosis relies on clinical evaluation alone.

At the Hospital for the Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, a dedicated database of approximately 15,000 registered patients with craniofacial anomalies is maintained, including 469 patients with craniosynostosis. Of these 469 patients, 89% present with syndromic forms (Fig. 1). Approximately half of these cases involve the most common syndromes, including Apert, Crouzon, Saethre-Chotzen, and Muenke (Fig. 2). However, substantial clinical variability challenges diagnosis, as some patients exhibit atypical features not consistent with classic syndromic presentations. For instance, certain patients showed craniofacial features that diverged from the typical phenotype of Apert syndrome, complicating clinical assessment.

These findings underscore the critical importance of detailed phenotypic evaluation, multidisciplinary care, and expanded access to genetic testing. Such measures are essential not only for accurate diagnosis but also for appropriate genetic counseling and management.

2.3 Prenatal diagnosis

Victor Bunduki1,2

1Faculty of Medicine, São Paulo University, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 2International Fetal Medicine and Surgery Society (IFMSS) Member

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):2.3

Introduction

Craniosynostosis has significant repercussions in perinatal and postnatal infant care, often necessitating extensive surgical procedures and long-term multidisciplinary team care. The condition’s association with numerous genetic syndromes underscores the importance of prenatal diagnosis, which has improved with advancements in genetic fetal diagnostics. Even when craniosynostosis occurs in isolation, it presents countless surgical and clinical challenges, as well as substantial social implications that impact the individual throughout their entire lifetime.

Prenatal diagnosis

Some facial and cranial fetal malformations are termed syndromes when their exact etiopathogenic mechanisms are unknown or hypothetical. Similar to other diseases that can be diagnosed prenatally, the prenatal diagnosis of craniosynostosis must adhere to certain principles: the diagnosis should occur as early as possible and be as comprehensive as possible. This includes taking a thorough family history, conducting ultrasound scans and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), and performing molecular genetic analysis.

Prenatal ultrasonographic features

Prenatal diagnosis is based on cranium shape, inter-eye orbit distance, fetal profile alterations, chin position, and the presence of a cleft.

Fetal cranial abnormalities

The fetal head has a standard oval shape that changes little with advanced gestational ages. Fetal scalp observation is even more critical in the late second or third trimester as many abnormalities involve bone calcification, which occurs a little late in gestation. Early abnormality is observed to be the most severe prognosis.

Hypertelorism

This feature is common in Apert Syndrome, Crouzon, Carpenter, frontonasal dysplasia, Noonan syndrome, and Wolf-Hirschhorn syndrome. It can also be present in cases of fetal anterior encephalocele. When isolated, hypertelorism leads only to aesthetic problems and eventual vision troubles. On the other hand, hypotelorism is seldom observed in the context of fetal craniosynostosis but can be present in specific conditions like metopic suture craniosynostosis or even in microcephaly cases. As in many cases of fetal affections, prognosis shall depend on associated abnormalities or genetic conditions under these phenotype expressions.

Fetal profile abnormalities and chin position

The standard fetal face profile is easily observed in a median sagittal view of the fetal head, the same plane used to measure fetal nasal bones. Many typical lines can be traced to guide abnormalities of chin position, frontal bone position, micrognathia, or impairment in the normal proportions of the fetal front, space between nasal bone and philtrum, and distance from philtrum to the fetal chin. Fetal profile abnormalities are seen in the Pierre Robin sequence, micro retrognathia, and Apert Syndrome.

Presence of cleft and other skeletal abnormalities

Lip and palate structures should always be observed during a structural fetal ultrasound and are of particular importance in the Pierre Robin sequence and for differential diagnosis with osteochondrodysplasias that can affect the fetal scalp and the context of fetal aneuploidies. The rest of the fetal bones should also be studied, especially the feet and hands position and fingers aspect (typical hand position in Apert Syndrome).

Prenatal ultrasonographic features of craniosynostosis will be presented as examples (Figs. 1–4).

Figures: Standard fetal cranial shape (Fig. 1 and 2) and abnormal fetal head shapes (Fig. 3 and 4).

Apert syndrome

Apart from abnormalities in cranial shape, a prominent forehead with frontal bossing is frequently observed. However, the most characteristic feature is syndactyly of the fingers, also known as spade hand, mitten hand, or rosebud hand. Finger abnormalities are a very typical aspect of fetal aperture syndrome. Mild cerebral ventriculomegaly can also be present in some cases. As with other forms of craniosynostosis, these ultrasonographic features are more commonly observed in the late second trimester.

Crouzon disease (craniofacial dysostosis)

Premature closure of cranial sutures leads to variable craniofacial deformities. Structural abnormalities seen in ultrasound include craniosynostosis, hypertelorism, relative exophthalmos, and mandibular prognathism. Together, these features constitute the so-called dentofacial hypoplasia aspect. Crouzon disease is an inherited condition with dominant autosomal transmission with a 100% penetrance but with a large phenotypic expression.

Genetic analysis for fetal craniosynostosis (fetal genomics)

Syndromic craniosynostosis represents about 20% of cases, and more than 20 genes are strongly associated with abnormal genotypes. About one-third of cases with a defined genetic diagnosis involve the FGFR2 gene (Apert, Crouzon, Pfeiffer). Another 16% involve the TWIST1 gene (Saethre-Chotzen, for instance), but FGFR3 (Muenke) and another gene can be responsible for fetal cranial abnormalities.

A genetic study should be conducted in case of abnormal ultrasound findings. In other cases, there may be a well-studied index case with a concrete possibility of familial or obstetrical recurrence. More recently, pre-implantation diagnosis can be recommended before embryo transfer for couples with a defined risk for fetal disease. In this context, molecular genetic tests can confirm diagnostic hypotheses and correlate phenotypes seen by ultrasound or MRI with genetic expression. It is important to note that these genetic tests are often invasive and carry a risk of abortion. Therefore, when there is an indication to use these invasive tests, their potential consequences should be thoroughly discussed with couples or families before being proposed.

More recently, prenatal genetic screening tests have been available and performed on maternal blood samples as monogenetic NIPT (Natera, Nim Genetics). These tests evaluate the most commonly involved genes (FGFR2, Twist1, etc.).

If an invasive procedure is chosen, then an SNP-Array may not be of much interest since it diagnoses only a minority of cases (10% to 20%) due to its inability to detect punctual variations. Craniosynostosis panels can also be performed, increasing the diagnostic performance to more than a 70% detection rate for monogenetic cases. If a fetal exome analysis is conducted, the accuracy is even higher. Referral laboratories should confirm whether they accept amniotic fluid samples for their study.

Conclusion

Craniosynostosis prenatal diagnosis is feasible for most conditions, and a specific diagnosis can often be achieved through genomic analysis. In many cases, invasive procedures are necessary to obtain an accurate diagnosis. It is crucial to discuss these concerns with parents very carefully. The best strategy is to exhaustively discuss the consequences of fetal craniosynostosis within the context of a clinical and genetic counseling consultation involving a multidisciplinary team of perinatal care professionals.

2.4 Craniofacial biology research

Matthew Harris1,2

1Department of Genetics, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Department of Orthopedics, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):2.4

Introduction

lab at Boston Children’s Hospital and Harvard Medical School specializes in comparative models of skeletogenesis. The research aims to understand the processes of skeletal formation, differentiation, and patterning, with special focus on how these processes lead to craniofacial dysplasias. The primary model organism used is the zebrafish, a robust genetic model for genetic discovery and studying gene function during development.

Research Focus

The main research question centers on understanding the shared developmental mechanisms underlying cranial vault formation. Over 80 to 100 genes are associated with unilateral and bilateral craniosynostosis. The developmental mechanisms that malfunction include loss of oxygen tension, premature ossification of sutures, altered cell proliferation dynamics, and aberrant signaling pathways. Despite these varied mechanisms, the outcome, suture fusion, is a common manifestation in most craniosynostosis cases. Understanding these underlying mechanistic processes is crucial for gaining insights into the heterogeneity of craniosynostosis and development of new approaches at controlling disease treatment and progression.

Developmental Mechanisms and Phenotype

Research in the Harris lab delves into the development of cranial plates and how the formation of these structures is altered in progression of the disease. For instance, the frontal and parietal bones arise from a population of cells above the eye that migrate dorsally along the skull. Observing these cells over time reveals active migration and differentiation. As these cells differentiate, they express bone markers and begin to ossify. Similar processes occur in zebrafish, where homologous bones form and migrate to the skull’s dorsal aspect. Overlapping sutures form as these bones develop, suggesting a regulated, rather than random, process.

Genetic Studies and Mutations

Genetic studies in zebrafish have identified mutations, such as bmp1a (“welded”), which, when not functioning correctly, disrupts normal suture formation. As this gene encodes an enzyme that modifies proteins outside the cell, this mutant highlights the role of extracellular matrix (ECM) proteins in cranial vault development. But how does alteration of these extracellular proteins instruct normal calvaria development. In collaboration with the lab of Radhika Atit at Case Western University, the Harris lab has found that Fibronectin, an ECM protein, exhibits anterior–posterior polarity in its expression along the basal to apical axis of the developing skull when the skeletal precursors are migrating. They find that this gene is essential for guiding cranial bone development. Misregulation of fibronectin is observed in zebrafish mutants with craniosynostosis and human craniosynostosis patients, underscoring its critical role in normal cranial vault development. Further experimental perturbation of this early graded expression of Fibronectin leads to altered progenitor cell migration in developing mouse skulls leading to unilateral suture fusion. These data suggest that these mechanisms are conserved and underlie common disrupted mechanisms of premature suture fusion.

Conclusion

The research underscores the importance of understanding the shared mechanisms of cranial vault morphogenesis and how dysregulation leads to craniosynostosis. Elucidating these processes aims to inform future therapeutic approaches for craniofacial abnormalities.

2.5 Potential CRISPR-based treatment therapy

Daniel M. Balkin1,2

1Department of Plastic and Oral Surgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 2Department of Surgery, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):2.5

This section aims to cover significant advancements in the understanding and treatment of craniofacial abnormalities, particularly Apert syndrome, and highlight the integration of genomic technologies in clinical practice.

Historical Context of Apert Syndrome

In 1995, Wilkie and colleagues identified the cause of Apert syndrome, discovering disease-causing missense substitutions (Ser252Trp and Pro253Arg) in the Fibroblast Growth Factor Receptor 2 (FGFR2). FGFR2 is a transmembrane cell surface tyrosine kinase receptor that initiates intracellular signaling cascades essential for embryonic development. Pathogenic mutations were discovered in 40 unrelated cases of Apert through a traditional candidate gene-based approach.

Genomic Revolution

Over the past two decades, genomics has undergone significant changes driven by technical advances that drastically reduced the cost of sequencing.In 2003, the Human Genome Project, which cost nearly $2 billion, delivered a sequence that accounted for 90% of the human genome.In the late 2000s, human genome sequencing cost around $100 million.The introduction of second-generation sequencing technologies (next-generation sequencing) between 2005 and 2010 drastically reduced costs, allowing for high-throughput parallel sequencing.By 2010–2011, third-generation sequencing technologies further reduced costs and enabled long-read sequencing of native DNA without amplification.

In 2003, the Human Genome Project, which cost nearly $2 billion, delivered a sequence that accounted for 90% of the human genome.

In 2003, the Human Genome Project, which cost nearly $2 billion, delivered a sequence that accounted for 90% of the human genome.

In the late 2000s, human genome sequencing cost around $100 million.

The introduction of second-generation sequencing technologies (next-generation sequencing) between 2005 and 2010 drastically reduced costs, allowing for high-throughput parallel sequencing.

By 2010–2011, third-generation sequencing technologies further reduced costs and enabled long-read sequencing of native DNA without amplification.

By 2010–2011, third-generation sequencing technologies further reduced costs and enabled long-read sequencing of native DNA without amplification.

These technological advancements shifted the bottleneck in genetic discovery from DNA sequencing to bioinformatics (base calling, alignment, variant calling, and variant interpretation).

Application to Craniofacial Program

At Boston Children’s Hospital, patients with rare and unusual craniofacial conditions visit our Craniofacial Clinic daily. We aim to collect DNA samples from our patients, hoping to leverage gene identification discoveries into therapeutic opportunities. At present, we enroll all patients with suspected craniosynostosis in our studies and collect buccal and/or blood samples.

Validation Using Model Organisms

The remarkable discovery of CRISPR-Cas9 by Drs. Jennifer Doudna and Emmanuelle Charpentier transformed modern biomedical research. This gene editing technology enables precise DNA modifications in cells and model organisms. We employ CRISPR-Cas9-based strategies to introduce human variants in mice and zebrafish to validate disease causality and to elucidate the pathomechanisms of disease.

Importance of Model Organisms

Research in model organisms has provided great insight into consequential biology with tremendous medical applications. For instance, the study of Drosophila melanogaster identified chromosomes as the vehicle of inheritance, and research in bacterial defense mechanisms uncovered the basis of CRISPR-Cas9. Notable medical breakthroughs based on model organism-based research include the development of antibiotics and the polio vaccine.

Integrating Genetic Insights into Clinical Practice

The Craniofacial Center at Boston Children’s Hospital aims to integrate genetic insights into clinical practice. For instance, Apert syndrome has a rather broad phenotypic spectrum. Research in our group collects whole genome datasets from our patients with Apert in an effort to identify modifying loci, mirroring successful efforts in other conditions such as cystic fibrosis. We hope that such discoveries will provide insights into optimized gene-specific patient management.

Collaborative Studies

Collaborative studies in BCH’s department and with other centers involve obtaining clinical specimens, such as cranial sutures (following suturectomy) and excess skin samples (following syndactyly release) for tissue- and cell-based analyses. These studies aim to correlate genetic findings with cellular and tissue-level changes, potentially identifying novel disease mechanisms. CRISPR-Cas9 is also adapted for whole-genome screens in cell lines to uncover genes that impact disease phenotypes.

Conclusion

The overarching goal of our craniofacial research is to identify disease-causing genes in our patients, elucidate the molecular pathogenesis of disease in the laboratory, and translate such insights into therapies to improve the lives of our patients.

3 Surgical management algorithm—protocols overview

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3

Moderators: Dr. Clarissa Turrer and Dr. Ricardo Oliveira

3.1 Hospital for rehabilitation of craniofacial anomalies in São Paulo, Brazil

Cristiano Tonello1,2, Michele Brandão1,2

1Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3.1

Introduction to Centrinho

Commonly referred to as “Centrinho” the hospital has a history spanning over five decades, dedicated to the diagnosis, treatment, and research of craniofacial anomalies. The institution traces its origins to 1965, when a room within the dental school responded to the high incidence of cleft lip and palate—then recorded at 1 in every 650 live births. From these beginnings, a nationally and internationally recognized center for craniofacial care emerged.

Several major milestones have marked this trajectory. In 1978, the center performed its first orthognathic surgery for patients with cleft lip and palate. Care for individuals with hearing impairments was introduced in 1987, followed by the first cochlear implant in Brazil in 1990. A longitudinal study launched in 1992—now spanning over 30 years and involving 466 individuals with cleft lip and palate—remains one of the largest follow-up cohorts of its kind. The study reflects the institution’s longstanding culture of international collaboration, including current partnerships such as with the University of Florida.

Over time, the center has expanded its academic and clinical reach through the establishment of medical residency programs, graduate studies, a university hospital, and more recently, a medical school. To date, more than 120,000 patients have received care, and over 2,000 professionals have been trained through specialized programs in cleft and craniofacial care.

Current Focus: Syndromic Craniosynostosis

The current focus includes the evaluation and surgical management of patients with syndromic craniosynostosis. The standard protocol involves initial posterior fossa decompression, particularly in cases requiring relief of intracranial pressure, as part of the first step in surgical management. Depending on the patient’s functional needs, such as respiratory compromise, orbital exposure, or airway protection, subsequent procedures may involve midface advancement using monobloc or Le Fort III osteotomy techniques. In selected cases, adjunctive procedures such as tracheostomy are avoided.

Challenges remain in the timing and coordination of care. Many patients arrive beyond the optimal treatment window, often due to limited local resources or geographic distance. Modifications to the protocol are sometimes required to address delayed presentations, particularly in patients who have not undergone posterior decompression or initial staged procedures.

For instance, one case involved an eight-year-old child presenting with significant respiratory distress and ocular exposure, without prior surgical intervention. Midface advancement was performed, supported by virtual surgical planning to guide osteotomy design and predict outcomes. Surgical guides were also used to assist with distractor placement, serving both clinical and educational purposes. Immediate postoperative outcomes included improved globe protection, although long-term monitoring remains ongoing.

Another child with Apert syndrome presented with similar delays but in a more stable condition. The patient underwent monobloc advancement with distractors and is currently four years post-operation. A third case involved a younger patient who arrived during an optimal surgical window but had additional complications such as cleft palate, impacting speech and airway function. After cleft repair and midface advancement, both aesthetic and functional outcomes were improved.

In some cases, particularly older patients, Le Fort III osteotomy is preferred over full monobloc advancement. One adult patient previously untreated is planned for Le Fort III advancement, while her child is undergoing monobloc frontofacial advancement. Le Fort III differs from monobloc procedures in that it is limited to the midface and excludes the frontal region.

A further case involved a child with Apert syndrome and severe obstructive sleep apnea (over 70 apneic episodes per hour), managed successfully with Le Fort III advancement and external distraction. The intervention significantly improved both function and appearance.

Collaborations with the University of São Paulo (USP) and other institutions have facilitated shared clinical pathways and postoperative monitoring. One case involved a patient previously operated on at Hospital das Clínicas, with good initial outcomes but requiring a second advancement as craniofacial growth progressed. A joint study between HC and “Centrinho” assessed outcomes, finding that while patients achieved improvements comparable to non-syndromic controls, residual facial angles often remained uncorrected, requiring secondary osteotomies or bipartition. Colorimetric mapping tools are employed to monitor growth trajectories and guide surgical planning.

Protocol for Neonatal Patients and Long-Term Follow-up

A comprehensive protocol for neonatal patients is detailed in the institution’s conduct manual, most recently updated in 2018. Upon referral, patients undergo an extensive multidisciplinary evaluation involving more than 10 professionals, including pediatricians, maxillofacial surgeons, geneticists, psychologists, and speech therapists. This initial evaluation spans three days and is often conducted for patients arriving from remote regions of the country.

When surgery is indicated, it is scheduled accordingly, with preoperative preparations including imaging, laboratory testing, and psychosocial assessments. Postoperative follow-up occurs at 30 days, 6 months, and 1 year, with another full evaluation conducted at the one-year mark. Patients are then monitored annually until adulthood, with additional interventions planned based on clinical progression and craniofacial growth.

Care may begin as early as the neonatal period, with a dedicated inpatient unit available when necessary. Comprehensive treatment is provided throughout infancy, childhood, adolescence, and young adulthood.

Addressing Structural Challenges and Future Directions

Despite a structured protocol and experienced multidisciplinary teams, significant barriers persist. Many families experience difficulty accessing specialized care due to geographic isolation, limited resources, or inconsistent referral systems. These barriers can delay diagnosis and intervention, often reducing the effectiveness of surgical outcomes.

Efforts are underway to improve outreach, enhance early referral systems, and optimize surgical timing. The goal is to ensure equitable access to comprehensive craniofacial care across the country. This discussion aims to spotlight both the progress made and the ongoing challenges, particularly in managing syndromic craniosynostosis in diverse clinical and socioeconomic contexts, and to encourage collaborative solutions among professionals, policymakers, and institutions.

3.2 Advancements in craniofacial care for Apert syndrome at Boston Children’s Hospital: A historical overview and future directions

John G. Meara1,2,3

1Program in Global Surgery and Social Change, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Department of Plastic and Oral Surgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 3Department of Surgery, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3.2

Introduction

The craniofacial program at Boston Children’s Hospital, renowned for its research and clinical excellence, continues to advance the understanding and treatment of craniofacial abnormalities. This section provides an overview of the program’s history, infrastructure, patient care approach, research advancements, and future directions.

Boston Children’s Hospital Overview and History

Boston Children’s Hospital (BCH), the number one Children’s Hospital in the United States in terms of research, has 2,000 medical staff, over 3,000 research scientists, 485 beds, and over 400 million in research funding annually. The craniofacial program at Boston Children’s Hospital has a rich history rooted in the pioneering efforts of Nobel laureate Dr. Joe Murray, who is renowned for his groundbreaking work in kidney transplantation. Dr. Murray established the craniofacial program and recruited vital figures such as Dr. John Mulliken, Dr. Joe Upton, and Dr. Leonard Kaban, marking the beginning of a legacy that now includes 12 full-time plastic and maxillofacial surgeons. In the late 1970s, Dr. Paul Tessier from Paris began a significant collaboration with BCH, fostering a 12–15-year cross-disciplinary exchange that significantly advanced the field. This partnership exemplified the importance of international cooperation in medical advancements.

The program emphasizes a team-oriented approach, essential for the comprehensive care of patients with craniofacial conditions. This multidisciplinary team ensures that patients and their families receive coordinated and compassionate care, addressing the complexities and anxieties associated with navigating the healthcare system. Moreover, recognizing the importance of cultural, ethnic, and linguistic sensitivities, it strives to provide personalized and culturally competent care, making a significant difference in the lives of patients and their families.

Further expanding its global reach, the program engaged in the Lancet Commission in Global Surgery in Brazil, initiated in 2013. This collaboration has fostered long-term relationships with Brazilian surgeons and contributed to the global exchange of knowledge and skills.

BCH Apert Protocol

Boston Children’s Hospital has significantly evolved its protocol for managing Apert syndrome. Until 2010, the primary surgical intervention for Apert syndrome patients involved Frontal Orbital Advancement (FOA). Post-2010, the introduction of endoscopic strip craniectomy marked a substantial shift in the treatment approach, leading to ongoing discussions about the long-term outcomes and subsequent necessary procedures, such as FOA or monoblock surgery. An analysis of FOA procedures within the Apert patient population revealed detailed demographic data, including transfusions, length of stay, advancements, and complications. Of the patients who underwent primary FOA without prior endoscopic procedures, a complication rate of approximately 10% was observed. Neurocognitive outcomes were noted to be insufficiently assessed, prompting the hiring of permanent neuropsychologists to integrate neurocognitive evaluations into patient care. Chronic otitis media and hearing issues were identified as significant concerns within the Apert population, emphasizing the need for comprehensive otolaryngological evaluations. Ophthalmological assessments demonstrated better morphologic outcomes in patients undergoing early endoscopic procedures than those receiving FOA at older ages, suggesting early intervention allows for improved orbital remodeling.

The introduction of endoscopic procedures has notably improved skull morphology, transforming treatment approaches. Moreover, the updated protocol, including endoscopic procedures, is particularly significant for patients presenting later than six months, who typically undergo frontal orbital advancement. This results in two distinct treatment protocols based on the timing of the patient’s arrival at BCH.

The Le Fort III procedure for craniofacial advancement remains a critical component of the protocol, with 21 patients having undergone this surgery, either with or without distraction techniques. Efforts to reduce blood transfusion rates through tranexamic acid (TXA) and preoperative erythropoietin have shown promising results. Early results from a three-week erythropoietin protocol indicate reductions in transfusion rates, with plans to extend the protocol to four weeks for even better outcomes.

BCH now collaborates with Pittsburgh to integrate 3D morphometric analysis of CT scans using AI and machine learning. This advanced technology, alongside genetic testing and neuropsychological evaluations, aims to better understand and address patients’ cognitive challenges. BCH’s evolving methodologies and collaborative efforts focus on improving the management and outcomes for patients with Apert syndrome, emphasizing the importance of early intervention and comprehensive genetic analysis. The comprehensive care model ensures patients receive coordinated treatment, minimizing the need for multiple specialist visits and reducing the overall burden on families.

Review of Apert Syndrome Cases

A comprehensive review of hospital records identified 163 cases of Apert syndrome over the past 60 years. Data analysis of 100 electronic health records has been completed, with plans to review the remaining paper records. Notably, 10% of Apert patients are international, reflecting the hospital’s global reach. Lastly, of these 100 patients, 48 were treated under the updated BCH protocol from childhood, while the remaining patients had received some or most of their treatment elsewhere before arriving at BCH. The review also explores medical comorbidities, including airway issues and genetic insights.

Future Directions

BCH will continue to analyze our Apert syndrome patients, focusing on specialized investigations across multidisciplinary lenses. The findings from these investigations aim to be presented comprehensively at the upcoming Craniofacial Meeting in Shanghai in 2025. Another aim is to develop a prospective protocol that thoroughly examines genotype–phenotype correlations and treatment outcomes. The program plans to integrate genetic testing, neurocognitive assessments, quality of life evaluations, and detailed 3D CT analysis of cranial morphology pre- and post-operatively. These efforts focus on optimizing surgical interventions and improving patient care pathways.

Additionally, BCH will continue to leverage its historic collaboration with Brazil and other international partners to advance research and education in rare conditions like Apert syndrome. It will focus on fostering innovation through collaborative research initiatives and enhancing educational opportunities to benefit future healthcare professionals. In summary, BCH’s future endeavors in Apert syndrome care underscore a commitment to advancing knowledge, refining treatment strategies, and fostering global collaboration in medical research and education.

3.3 Historical reflections and evolving surgical approaches in syndromic craniosynostosis perspectives from University Hospital Necker—Enfants Malades Hospital—France

Eric Arnaud1,2

1Craniofacial Surgery Department, University Hospital Necker—Enfants Malades, Paris, France; 2Centre de Competences pour les maladies crânio-faciales, Clinique Marcel Sembat-Ramsay, Boulogne-Billancourt, France

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3.3

Advancements in craniofacial surgery have been shaped by generations of dedicated surgeons, beginning with the foundational contributions of pioneers such as Daniel Marchac, a second-generation facial surgeon and a direct mentee of those who built the early practice of craniosynostosis care. Among these early influencers was Emile Apert, who published his seminal work in 1906, and the neurosurgeon Gérard Guyot, whose visionary proposals and open-mindedness contributed greatly to the evolution of craniosynostosis surgery. The impact of Ortiz Monasterio, with whom many trainees spent formative years, including during the pivotal period of the early 1990s, also remains central to the surgical heritage in this field.

From a genetic perspective, specific mutations have been linked to relatively stable phenotypes across ethnic groups. For instance, Apert syndrome is associated with two principal mutations, resulting in consistent clinical manifestations. In contrast, the broader mutational spectrum of Crouzon syndrome leads to greater phenotypic variability, requiring individualized treatment strategies. While clinical guidelines provide general recommendations regarding timing and type of intervention, tailored approaches are essential to address the functional and anatomical needs of each patient.

Historically, surgical approaches to midface advancement predate the widespread adoption of the monobloc or Le Fort III techniques. Notably, Sir Harold Gillies performed one of the earliest Le Fort III osteotomies in 1954. Although technically successful, Gillies noted the instability of the technique due to the lack of fixation materials at the time and advised against its routine use.

Adult patients with unoperated syndromic craniosynostosis continue to present in some settings. In these cases, subcranial approaches are often preferred over intracranial procedures, as brain plasticity is reduced by adulthood. Despite these limitations, aesthetically and functionally satisfactory results can be achieved with careful planning.

The evolution of surgical techniques has also incorporated virtual planning and the use of distractors for midface advancement. Although not always essential, these tools are valuable for surgical predictability, teaching, and research. For example, monobloc procedures have been successfully performed in patients with Apert syndrome presenting with respiratory distress and ocular exposure, often using external distractors and surgical guides. Long-term follow-up confirms the stability of results and the gradual refinement of surgical protocols.

The introduction of minimally invasive strategies further expanded treatment options for younger patients with complex craniofacial conditions. Breathing and airway assessment, often overlooked in earlier decades, have become essential components of modern care. The widespread use of MRI since the 2000s has enhanced the detection of Chiari malformations and other critical anomalies. Moreover, the introduction of polysomnography revealed a high prevalence of obstructive sleep apnea among this population, underscoring the importance of airway evaluation before and after surgery.

Posterior cranial expansion, whether with or without distractors, has become a key component of current protocols. In syndromes like Apert, posterior cranial vault issues are rare, but in Crouzon and Pfeiffer syndromes, posterior distraction may be necessary prior to or in place of monobloc advancement. Historically, monobloc procedures carried significant risk due to the inherent septic pathway created at the skull base. Mortality rates were once near 50%, leading to temporary abandonment of the procedure. The advent of distraction osteogenesis allowed for safer, gradual advancement, significantly reducing complications.

Early intervention has proven essential. Khonsari et al. demonstrated that orbital volume expansion is most effective when osteotomies are performed around 2.5 years of age. While earlier surgeries may be preferable in some cases, this age remains a critical benchmark for achieving optimal orbital outcomes.

Despite these advances, respiratory outcomes remain a challenge. In a 15-year clinical series involving Crouzon, Pfeiffer, and Apert syndromes, normalization of apnea–hypopnea index occurred in only 40% of cases. About half of the patients showed partial improvement, while others required further intervention. Data suggest that improvements in oxygen saturation are observed six months after distractor removal, once inflammation subsides.

Complication management has evolved significantly. In earlier years, omission of lateral canthopexy resulted in cosmetically undesirable outcomes. Today, protocols prioritize both function and aesthetics. However, serious complications such as tonsillar herniation, cerebrospinal fluid leaks, and infection remain risks, particularly in the absence of routine MRI and careful surgical planning. The importance of preserving frontal bone integrity is emphasized, especially in patients who may later require monobloc advancement. Procedures that compromise this region, such as certain endoscopic techniques, should be avoided if monobloc advancement is anticipated.

Monobloc surgery continues to present specific risks, particularly when excessive intracranial volume leads to deadspace and subsequent infection. Careful planning, including the use of bilateral pericranial flaps and forehead tilting techniques, has improved outcomes and reduced complications. Recent studies published by the team in Plastic and Reconstructive Surgery validate these technical refinements.

Relapse, defined as recurrence of deformity shortly after distractor removal, remains a significant concern, especially in secondary procedures. Strategies to mitigate relapse include the use of tongue-and-groove osteotomy designs, long-term internal devices with resorbable fixation, and staged distraction techniques. Posterior expansion followed by monobloc advancement, when performed early, can yield durable results and minimize the need for later interventions.

Representative cases illustrate diverse treatment pathways. For some patients, a monobloc procedure during early childhood offers up to a decade of symptom-free development, followed by Le Fort III advancement and aesthetic refinement during adolescence. Others require staged posterior expansion followed by midface advancement to address airway and craniofacial deficiencies. Long-term follow-up confirms the stability of outcomes, particularly when surgical timing and staging are optimized.

Conclusion

In conclusion, contemporary protocols for syndromic craniosynostosis must incorporate early screening for Chiari malformations, systematic tonsillectomy, and individualized timing of interventions. While monobloc advancement carries inherent risks, it remains a valuable strategy when performed under appropriate conditions to treat concomittantly exorbitism and SAOS. When successful, it offers significant improvement in function and aesthetics, supporting neurodevelopment and quality of life. Ultimately, successful treatment requires a coordinated, multidisciplinary team with expertise in genetics, neurosurgery, craniofacial surgery, and respiratory care.

3.4 Sobrapar Hospital, Campinas—Brazil

Enrico Ghizoni1,2, Cássio Raposo do Amaral3

1Faculty of Medicine, University of Campinas—UNICAMP, Campinas, SP Brazil; 2Department of Neurology, University of Campinas—UNICAMP, Campinas, SP, Brazil; 3 Hospital SOBRAPAR, Campinas, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3.4

Establishing Priorities in the Surgical Management of Rare Craniofacial Syndromes

Given the broad phenotypic spectrum of rare craniofacial syndromes, a single standardized treatment protocol is not feasible. Instead, individualized care plans must be developed based on patient-specific functional and anatomical priorities. To address this need, a “Priority Protocol” approach has been proposed, focusing on the most urgent clinical concerns to improve quality of life and long-term outcomes.

In 2018, the Apert Syndrome Association was established to support access to specialized care for affected children, particularly by facilitating referrals to high-complexity centers. In Brazil, the structure of the Unified Health System (SUS) offers a unique advantage, enabling patients from remote and underserved regions to reach reference hospitals capable of providing multidisciplinary craniofacial care.

Certain syndromic presentations, such as Pfeiffer syndrome, are frequently associated with obstructive sleep apnea and hydrocephalus due to elevated intracranial pressure. In these cases, the initial neurosurgical intervention often involves placement of a programmable ventriculoperitoneal shunt, followed by cranial remodeling procedures. Patients with rapidly increasing head circumference and post-decompression hydrocephalus typically require inpatient care before proceeding with fronto-orbital advancement.

In syndromes with severe ocular proptosis, vision preservation becomes the primary treatment priority. Without timely intervention, such patients are at risk of irreversible vision loss. In other cases, upper limb malformations may take precedence, particularly in patients with Type III syndactyly. If left untreated, complete metacarpal fusion can occur, impairing the development of individual fingers and limiting functional hand use. Since the growth rate of each digit varies, early separation is critical. In patients without hydrocephalus or airway compromise, early hand surgery is prioritized to support functional independence and sensory development.

For many patients, posterior cranial vault distraction is optimally performed between 8 and 10 months of age. This intervention can reduce the need for future fronto-orbital advancement, as current literature supports. In Apert syndrome, posterior distraction is particularly beneficial: despite increased bleeding tendencies, these patients typically exhibit lower intracranial pressure. Posterior distraction has also been associated with cognitive benefits and preserves anterior cranial structures, reducing the complexity and risks of subsequent surgeries.

In craniofrontonasal dysplasia, patients often present in adolescence or adulthood with significant craniofacial deformities. In such cases, segmental osteotomies may be required. This technique involves resection of midline cranial bone, advancement of the orbital walls, and anterior cranial base reconstruction using vascularized flaps. The goal is to medialize the orbits, thereby correcting divergent strabismus without disrupting the function of extraocular muscles.

Le Fort III osteotomy remains a viable alternative to avoid intracranial access and can be performed with an external distractor to allow controlled counterclockwise rotation of the midface. In selected cases, monobloc bipartition offers an additional reconstructive option when broader correction is needed.

This priority-based approach underscores the need for multidisciplinary decision-making and individualized planning to ensure the most critical functional deficits are addressed at the appropriate time, maximizing both developmental and surgical outcomes.

3.5 Faculty of Medicine of the University of São Paulo (FMUSP-SP)—Brazil

Hamilton Matushita1, Nivaldo Alonso1,2,3

1Faculty of Medicine, São Paulo University, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 2Faculty of Medicine of Bauru, São Paulo University, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 3Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3.5

Phenotypic Variability and Individualized Approaches in Faciocraniosynostoses

Faciocranioestenoses represent a specific subset of syndromic craniosynostoses characterized by both cranial and facial dysmorphism. The clinical and surgical management of these syndromes is highly complex due to significant variability in phenotypic presentation, even within genetically defined conditions.

Phenotypic Diversity Across Syndromes

Muenke syndrome exemplifies the diagnostic challenge posed by syndromes lacking consistent clinical features. Although genetically identifiable, Muenke syndrome often does not present with a well-defined phenotype, making clinical diagnosis difficult without molecular testing. Even among more widely recognized syndromes such as Apert and Crouzon, there is considerable phenotypic heterogeneity, complicating early identification and classification.

In contrast, Saethre-Chotzen syndrome often displays more specific clinical indicators, such as low hairline implantation. Somatic features are also important for syndromic differentiation, particularly involving the hands and feet. For example:Antley-Bixler syndrome is marked by synostosis of bones in the upper and lower limbs.Beare-Stevenson syndrome is associated with cutis gyrata, or excessive skin folding, sometimes affecting facial regions.Jackson-Weiss syndrome may resemble Apert syndrome in its craniofacial features.Muenke syndrome should be suspected in cases of bilateral coronal synostosis, especially when associated with sensorineural hearing loss.Crouzon syndrome may present with acanthosis nigricans.Michael syndrome can include hypertelorism and short palpebral fissures.Cleidocranial dysplasia typically features delayed suture closure, persistent open fontanelles, and hypoplastic or absent clavicles.

Despite advances in genetic testing, a proportion of cases remain undiagnosed, underscoring the importance of detailed phenotypic evaluation in clinical assessment.

Sutural Phenotypes and Surgical Implications

Variations in sutural involvement further inform clinical classification and treatment:Apert syndrome is divided into two types:◦Type 1: Involvement of the coronal sutures only, often associated with upper airway obstruction.◦Type 2: Involvement of both coronal sutures and posterior cranial regions, with potential impact on the oropharyngeal airway.Crouzon syndrome also presents in two forms:◦Type 1: Coronal synostosis◦Type 2: ScaphocephalySaethre-Chotzen syndrome demonstrates sutural phenotypes similar to Apert syndrome.

Treatment Considerations and Surgical Strategy

Surgical priorities are defined based on the severity and location of suture fusion, as well as the functional consequences. In severe cases, tracheostomy may be required due to compromised airway patency. In Apert syndrome, early intervention is essential to prevent restriction of brain growth and the associated risk of cognitive impairment. Additional concerns include cranio-cervical junction anomalies and foramen magnum stenosis, which can lead to serious neurologic complications such as tetraparesis.

Treatment strategies vary depending on the clinical presentation:Type 1 craniosynostosis cases typically require midface or cranial advancement.Posterior deformities are managed with posterior decompression.In certain cases, bone quality may preclude advancement due to thinning or perforation, limiting the feasibility of bone flap mobilization.When distractors are available, surgical management is simplified and staged corrections become more feasible.

Type 1 craniosynostosis cases typically require midface or cranial advancement.

Type 1 craniosynostosis cases typically require midface or cranial advancement.

Posterior deformities are managed with posterior decompression.

Posterior deformities are managed with posterior decompression.

In certain cases, bone quality may preclude advancement due to thinning or perforation, limiting the feasibility of bone flap mobilization.

In certain cases, bone quality may preclude advancement due to thinning or perforation, limiting the feasibility of bone flap mobilization.

When distractors are available, surgical management is simplified and staged corrections become more feasible.

When distractors are available, surgical management is simplified and staged corrections become more feasible.

A unique case involved a child with autism and pansynostosis who presented with headaches and a normal cephalic index. Due to behavioral considerations, distractor use was not feasible, and the surgical team opted for spiral remodeling as an alternative.

Minimally invasive techniques, including endoscopic-assisted procedures, are currently being employed in the management of selected syndromic craniosynostosis cases. Early monobloc surgery remains an option for securing the airway and preventing progressive upper airway compromise.

Conclusion

The treatment of faciocranioestenoses requires individualized planning due to the wide variability in both cranial suture involvement and extracranial manifestations. Phenotypic assessment remains indispensable in guiding early diagnosis and personalized surgical intervention. Multidisciplinary evaluation, including neurosurgery, craniofacial surgery, genetics, and developmental pediatrics, is critical to optimizing outcomes in this diverse patient population.

Publication of all patient information was authorized through explicit, informed consent obtained from the patients and their guardians.

3.6 São Paulo Federal University (UNIFESP)—Brazil

Sergio Cavalheiro, Max Pereira

Faculty of Medicine, Federal University of São Paulo, São Paulo, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):3.6

Understanding Cranial Growth Through Evolutionary Analysis

One of the critical turning points in the department’s history was the decision to investigate cranial base development to better understand the origins and surgical implications of cranial deformities. This approach was informed by comparative studies in human evolution. The analysis began with early hominins:Australopithecus africanus (approximately 4 million years ago) exhibited underdeveloped maxillary structures and lacked a defined frontal bone.In Homo habilis, while maxillary hypoplasia persisted, posterior cranial fossa expansion became evident.This evolutionary trend continued with Homo erectus, culminating in the emergence of Homo sapiens, in whom frontal bone growth and resolution of maxillary hypoplasia became defining features.

Australopithecus africanus (approximately 4 million years ago) exhibited underdeveloped maxillary structures and lacked a defined frontal bone.

Australopithecus africanus (approximately 4 million years ago) exhibited underdeveloped maxillary structures and lacked a defined frontal bone.

In Homo habilis, while maxillary hypoplasia persisted, posterior cranial fossa expansion became evident.

In Homo habilis, while maxillary hypoplasia persisted, posterior cranial fossa expansion became evident.

This evolutionary trend continued with Homo erectus, culminating in the emergence of Homo sapiens, in whom frontal bone growth and resolution of maxillary hypoplasia became defining features.

This evolutionary trend continued with Homo erectus, culminating in the emergence of Homo sapiens, in whom frontal bone growth and resolution of maxillary hypoplasia became defining features.

To quantify cranial development, the team studied:Growth of the anterior cranial fossaOrbitopterional distanceBipterygoid width

Growth of the anterior cranial fossa

Orbitopterional distance

Bipterygoid width

Bipterygoid width

An important observation during this analysis was the consistent positioning of the sella turcica, which served as a central anatomical reference point in both normal and pathologic growth patterns. In cases of brachycephaly, characterized by frontal retrusion and maxillary underdevelopment due to early coronal suture fusion, the sphenoid bone’s structure—notably its lever-like relationship to the sella turcica—was found to be pivotal. It was observed that newborns possess a sphenoid bone shape reminiscent of early hominins, and correcting lever mechanics at this anatomical center improved outcomes.

Innovations in Bloodless Surgery: Adapting Techniques for Jehovah’s Witness Patients

A second major clinical challenge involved the care of Jehovah’s Witness children, for whom blood transfusions are prohibited. This led to the development and integration of minimally invasive endoscopic techniques designed to reduce intraoperative bleeding.

Key innovations included: Endoscopic trephination for targeted bone removalA custom-designed surgical drill that preserves the dura mater integrity

Endoscopic trephination for targeted bone removal

A custom-designed surgical drill that preserves the dura mater integrity

A custom-designed surgical drill that preserves the dura mater integrity

These approaches are particularly useful in delaying definitive surgical intervention in complex cases while minimizing transfusion risk. However, they require helmet therapy postoperatively to guide cranial remodeling. This adaptation demonstrates the department’s commitment to culturally sensitive, individualized care.

Long-Term Outcome Monitoring and Quality Metrics

To ensure the quality and safety of surgical outcomes in craniosynostosis care, long-term follow-up remains essential. Core goals of treatment include:Prevention of cognitive impairmentMonitoring for intracranial hypertensionManagement of obstructive sleep apneaMinimization of surgical procedures and operative durationThese outcomes are assessed through routine evaluations and appropriate diagnostic modalities.

Comprehensive Multidisciplinary Management

Optimal care for patients with syndromic craniosynostosis requires a comprehensive, multidisciplinary team, typically including: Craniofacial surgeon, Neurosurgeon, Otolaryngologist, Ophthalmologist, Anesthesiologist, Orthodontist, Speech therapist, Psychologist, Geneticist.

Polysomnography is a critical diagnostic tool for determining the need for midface advancement surgery, particularly in patients with suspected or confirmed obstructive apnea. When warranted, CPAP therapy or tracheostomy may be initiated.
