Treatment Timing and Protocol Strategy

The surgical strategy is tailored based on the age at which the patient presents for treatment. Early intervention is prioritized where feasible:Frontoorbital and posterior cranial advancements are generally performed between 6 and 12 months of age.Midface advancement is typically reserved for later stages of growth.

Frontoorbital and posterior cranial advancements are generally performed between 6 and 12 months of age.

Frontoorbital and posterior cranial advancements are generally performed between 6 and 12 months of age.

Midface advancement is typically reserved for later stages of growth.

Midface advancement is typically reserved for later stages of growth.

The department prioritizes modified Le Fort III osteotomy over traditional monobloc procedures due to lower complication rates and improved functional outcomes.

Secondary procedures, performed as needed, may include: Repeat frontoorbital advancement, Reconstructive rhinoplasty, Orbital decompression, Le Fort I advancement, Malar augmentation.

Each treatment pathway is customized, guided by clinical presentation, age at referral, anatomical considerations, and patient-specific functional priorities.

Conclusion

The treatment of syndromic craniosynostosis requires a patient-centered, multidisciplinary approach informed by cranial growth patterns and clinical priorities. Advances such as bloodless surgical techniques and early cranial base analysis have improved safety and outcomes. By individualizing treatment plans and optimizing surgical timing, the team continues to address complex anatomical and functional challenges while prioritizing long-term quality of life.

4 The challenges to provide optimal care for patients with syndromic craniosynostosis

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):4

Moderators: Dr. Pedro Hamamoto and Dr. Enrico Ghizoni

4.1 Craniosynostosis and endoscopic treatment: BCH experience

Mark Proctor

Department of Neurosurgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):4.1

Background

The skull deformity in Apert syndrome is typically characterized by the premature closure of the bilateral coronal sutures. This leads to a distinctive deformity known as turribrachycephaly, which results in a high and shortened head shape. Traditionally, this deformity has been addressed through frontal orbital advancement; however, a significant reduction in head height has been challenging with this method. Recent studies suggest that early surgical intervention can mitigate the progression of turricephaly.

Early Release Techniques

Several early-release techniques have been explored, with posterior expansion emerging as a common method worldwide. This approach involves the use of distractors, springs, or direct open surgery without additional hardware replacement, and by elongating the skull it effectively reduces the head height. At Boston Children’s Hospital, based on a literature review and initial experience with bilateral coronal synostosis, a protocol emphasizing early anterior coronal suture release has been adopted.

Posterior Distraction Techniques

Various studies, including those by Eric Arnaud using springs a decade ago, have examined posterior distraction techniques across different global settings. While increasingly common, this approach is part of a multi-stage treatment strategy involving distractor placement, a second operation for distractor removal, and subsequent frontal procedures.

Endoscopic Techniques

Endoscopic techniques, pioneered by David Jimenez and his colleagues, have been described for managing bilateral coronal synostosis. The primary goals of early intervention are to improve cranial form by advancing the frontal region and correcting the turribrachycephaly, and safeguard neurological and ocular functions over the long term.

Protocol at Boston Children’s Hospital

The endoscopic protocol involves preoperative CT scans for all patients. Surgery is ideally performed between two and four months of age, though this timing can vary based on individual patient needs. To optimize outcomes, patients wear cranial helmets to control the height and allow for adequate skull elongation, with an average duration of seven months post-surgery.

Surgical Technique and Efficacy

The bone release technique is used during the early stages of intervention. Reports from Brazil further underscore the efficacy of these releases, even in Apert syndrome, emphasizing the importance of achieving adequate bone mobilization to address turribrachycephaly and frontal plagiocephaly.

Outcome Analysis

Analysis of outcomes across patient cohorts has demonstrated significant cranial index improvements post-surgery. Comparative studies, including those with Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia’s posterior distraction group, indicate the open and endoscopic results are comparable, but with fewer surgical procedures being required in the endoscopic group. A recent cohort study of 18 Apert syndrome patients showed a reduction in cranial index from preoperative levels, suggesting favorable outcomes comparable to or better than those achieved with open surgery techniques.

Results

Overall, 27 endoscopic surgeries have shown promising results, with an average surgery duration of approximately one hour. Complications and transfusion rates are low. Ongoing follow-up indicates that approximately 40% of patients may require a second surgery to address frontal flattening due to suture re-closure.

Conclusion

Endoscopic suturectomy combined with postoperative helmet therapy represents a viable treatment option for managing turribrachycephaly in children with Apert syndrome. While posterior operations also yield positive outcomes, the multi-stage nature of posterior distraction techniques often necessitates a three surgery treatment algorithm, unlike the more streamlined approach offered by early endoscopic intervention, where only 40% of patients have gone on to need a second operation. Continued long-term monitoring is essential in this young patient cohort to assess ongoing cranial growth and functional outcomes.

Ethical Acknowledgment

Publication of all patient information was authorized through explicit, informed consent obtained from the patients and their guardians.

4.2 How to improve syndromic craniosynostosis patient treatment in Brazil. “What do we have, what we don’t”?

Eduardo Jucá1,2

1Brazilian Society of Pediatric Neurosurgery (SBNPed); 2Pediatric Neurosurgery Service of the Hospital Infantil Albert Sabin, Fortaleza, CE, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):4.2

Introduction

Craniosynostosis encompasses a diverse group of diseases with some characteristics in common: early fusion of one or more cranial sutures, cranial deformity and restriction of brain expansion, and the possibility of other entities, neurological or not, in the same patient, such as hydrocephalus or Chiari deformity. The potential for damage to neurological development and the child’s integration into the social environment, including school and the community, is constantly present. Therefore, the treatment of craniosynostosis needs to be continually improved and disseminated so that more patients benefit.

Children with craniosynostosis are found worldwide, necessitating advances that address different scenarios, both in developed countries and in developing regions with limited technical resources. Understanding the situation in Brazil is particularly important in this context. The country exhibits significant diversity in access to highly complex treatments, with some regions facing severe resource constraints while others boast centers of excellence. This disparity highlights the need for tailored approaches to ensure that all children receive the care they need regardless of location.

Given these regional and global disparities, carrying out a situational diagnosis of the current state of the means to treat craniosynostosis in each region is essential. The scenarios observed in each location can be extrapolated to other regions with the same characteristics around the globe. With these assumptions, we aimed to evaluate the conditions for treating craniosynostosis in Brazil, identifying the elements that are already present and those in which progress can be made.

Methods

The analysis of the elements related to improving the treatment of craniosynostosis was divided into four parts: 1. Human resources and specialized personnel; 2. Technical and material resources; 3. Policies oriented to the area, and 4. Awareness and articulation. In addition, a questionnaire was sent to pediatric neurosurgery services recognized in the country for providing high complex care and for being responsible for covering at least one regional extension as a reference center. The questions on this form involved characterizing the volume of surgeries in each service and the existing resources for treating craniosynostosis. Comparisons were made regarding the level of disparities among regions of the country.

Results

Considering the responses to the form sent, the research involved 24 pediatric neurosurgery services distributed across the Brazilian regions: North 4.3%, Northeast 39.1%, Midwest 13%, Southeast 39.1%, South 4.3%. Of these, 13% operate in private settings, 26.1% in public institutions, and 60.9% in both modalities. Regarding surgical volume, 20.26% of craniosynostosis correction surgeries were syndromic, while 79.73% were non-syndromic. Although there is an equivalence between the number of services that operate large or small numbers of non-syndromic craniosynostosis, only a small sample has a frequent surgical volume of syndromic craniosynostosis with an average of one case or more per month. All reported having access to CT scan, craniotomes, and intensive care unit at their facilities. Magnetic resonance imaging is available to 73.9% of the services within the main hospital and 33.3% outside the hospital. Only 8.3% had access to red blood cell retrievers, the lowest rate among the resources evaluated in the questionnaire. Access to osteogenic distraction implants was reported by 45.8%, piezoelectric motors by 50%, and an effective partnership with plastic and/or maxillofacial surgeons also by 50%. Other resources had higher availability, such as a surgical adhesive (biological glue), available to 95.8% of the practitioners, and a reciprocating saw with lateral cut, available to 62.5%. Absorbable plates and pins were reported as available by 62.5% of neurosurgeons, while 50% had access to metal plates and pins (Fig. 1).

Discussion

Human resources and qualified personnel.

Human resources and qualified personnel.

Human resources and qualified personnel.

Brazil has international recognition for the high level of neurosurgery practiced and the technical training of its neurosurgeons. This picture is replicable when considering pediatric neurosurgery. The Brazilian Society of Pediatric Neurosurgery has 205 members and promotes articulation and scientific training. The Brazilian Congress of Pediatric Neurosurgery takes place every two years. The Latin American Pediatric Neurosurgery Course, also biannual, always has a large participation of Brazilian neurosurgeons. The society also has its scientific journal, the Archives of Pediatric Neurosurgery, indexed in several databases, including Scopus, and has an established partnership with the journal Child’s Nervous System. Brazilian pediatric neurosurgeons have specialized training and are qualified to treat cases of craniosynostosis. Some of them have specific fellowships and training in treating syndromic craniosynostosis.

Nevertheless, despite the existing highly specialized workforce, there is a marked disparity in the geographic distribution of professionals in the country. In the responses to the form, there is a concentration of specialists in the Southeast and Northeast, with isolated professionals in the North, Central-West, and South. The majority of neurosurgeons work at the same time in the public and private environments. Moreover, the regional concentration of specialized care becomes even more pronounced with complex procedures such as osteogenic distraction, frequently required for treating syndromic craniosynostosis. This is evidenced by the limited number of high-volume centers for syndromic craniosynostosis, all of which are situated in the Southeast region of the country.

Our analysis also showed that only half of the services surveyed have an effective partnership between a neurosurgeon and a plastic surgeon specializing in craniofacial surgery. This is an important marker of the ability to perform advanced procedures such as the advancement of the middle third of the face, an important part of most treatments for syndromic craniosynostosis. Therefore, it is necessary to make the distribution of human resources trained to treat craniosynostosis in the country more equitable. The reasoning that centers of excellence should receive all cases has difficulty being applied in such a populous country with continental dimensions. The North and Northeast regions have 60 million inhabitants and no reference service in craniofacial surgery with a high annual surgical volume.

2.Technical and material resources.

2.Technical and material resources.

Technical and material resources.

Most services have basic material and structure for craniosynostosis surgeries, such as craniotomy and pediatric intensive care units (ICU). However, many services do not have absorbable material for bone fixation, which leads to the need for alternative means of fixation. Less than half of the services have access to distraction osteogenesis, an important resource in syndromic craniosynostosis treatment. Therefore, efforts are needed to provide the material and structure necessary for surgical treatments to be carried out properly.

3.Oriented policies.

3.Oriented policies.

Oriented policies.

The duality of types of assistance, private and public, could be improved in patients’ access to health services. Although this reality is difficult to evaluate in a practical way, competition for beds and places in the operating theaters of public services with patients with acute cases of intracranial hypertension causes delays that reduce the effectiveness of surgical procedures. In this sense, there must be policies aimed at enabling children’s access to surgeries, and not just their registration in hospitals, where the wait can be long for the possibility of carrying out the procedures.

4.Awareness and articulation.

4.Awareness and articulation.

Awareness and articulation.

As previously discussed, there is a need for permanent public awareness and advocacy for craniosynostosis patients so that access to adequate treatment is facilitated. The media and social networks must be vehicles for these advocacy actions, as it is necessary to influence both the medical community and decision-makers in public policies, as well as the population in general so that the diagnosis is carried out early enough to allow for adequate treatment.

Conclusion

Brazil has professionals trained to treat both syndromic and nonsyndromic craniosynostosis, but their distribution is uneven across the country. Increasing equity between regions requires improved access to adequate materials and infrastructure. Developing public policies and complementary assistance programs that facilitate access to craniosynostosis surgeries is essential. Advocacy actions in specialized circles and the media are necessary to achieve these goals.

Fig. 1 (abstract 4.2).Access to technical resources to treat craniosynostosis among Brazilian pediatric neurosurgery services in Brazil

Access to technical resources to treat craniosynostosis among Brazilian pediatric neurosurgery services in Brazil

4.3 Challenges to treat syndromic craniosynostosis in the Northeast of Brazil

Marcelo Lins

Faculty of Medicine, Universidade Federal de Pernambuco, Recife, PE, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):4.3

Introduction

This lecture aimed to highlight the significant challenges of performing high-complexity craniofacial surgeries in Brazil’s Northeast region, with a focus on syndromic craniosynostoses. These cases often require complex procedures such as facial advancements and bone distraction, surgeries that are among the most technically demanding in craniofacial practice. The challenges are especially acute in the Northeast, a region marked by high levels of socioeconomic vulnerability, as will be detailed below.

Given the international nature of this conference, the intention is to begin by providing key demographic context for Brazil’s Northeast. The region is home to approximately 54.6 million people, making it the second most populous region in the country.1 It has a demographic density of about 35 inhabitants per square kilometer. Despite its size, it is also the poorest region in Brazil.1 According to national data from the Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics (IBGE), the Northeast ranks second-lowest in per capita income and GDP, offering a sobering overview of the socioeconomic landscape in which craniofacial surgical care must operate.1

The disparities are alarming: 53.2% of all Brazilians living in poverty and 44.8% of those in extreme poverty reside in the Northeast. The region also falls below the national average in the Human Development Index (HDI), with a score of 0.754, placing it 87th in global rankings a clear indication of the structural barriers to accessing advanced healthcare. 2,3,4

Financial and Systemic Barriers to Care

The first major challenge, and the one from which many others stem, is financial resource allocation. As a historically underserved region, most public healthcare funding in the Northeast is directed toward primary care and high-incidence, high-urgency conditions. In contrast, syndromic craniosynostosis is a low-incidence, high-complexity condition often perceived as lower priority within public health agendas.

Craniofacial surgery demands significant financial and infrastructural investment. Unlike cleft lip and palate care, which is more common and relatively less costly to manage, syndromic craniosynostosis treatment often involves ICU support, highly specialized surgical materials, and coordinated interdisciplinary care, all of which strain public resources. Despite this, the Northeast receives between 1 and 6 billion BRL annually in federal health transfers, as per the Brazilian Transparency Portal.5 However, most of these funds are allocated to basic health services, with only a small fraction dedicated to specialized pediatric or disability-related care.

This brings us to the second major challenge, and perhaps the most critical: the lack of targeted public policies to ensure adequate funding and institutional support for the treatment of children with syndromic craniosynostosis. Without dedicated health policy frameworks or reimbursement mechanisms, hospitals and care teams lack the means to build sustainable treatment pathways for this vulnerable population.

The Case of IMIP: Infrastructure and Institutional Gaps

One illustrative example is the Instituto de Medicina Integral Professor Fernando Figueira (IMIP), based in Recife. 6 IMIP is well-known as Brazil’s second-largest center for cleft care and has an outstanding infrastructure for managing patients with cleft lip and palate.6 It offers complete, high-quality services and multidisciplinary teams for cleft-related treatments.

However, for syndromic craniosynostosis, the situation is vastly different. Despite spending ten years working in IMIP’s outpatient clinic, the surgical team often had to postpone or cancel surgeries due to lack of resources. Common barriers included the absence of anesthesiologists, unavailability of bone distractors or surgical materials, and the lack of pediatric support for postoperative care.

These persistent resource gaps made it nearly impossible to provide comprehensive surgical care to children with syndromic craniosynostosis. While IMIP is a national reference for cleft care, it does not currently have the infrastructure to support the complexity required for syndromic craniosynostosis surgery. This mismatch reflects a broader issue: the absence of strategic planning and investment in specialized surgical services for rare and resource-intensive conditions in Brazil’s most vulnerable regions.

Conclusion

Delivering high-complexity craniofacial surgery for syndromic craniosynostosis in Northeastern Brazil presents profound challenges, from limited funding and inadequate infrastructure to the absence of public policies that prioritize rare and resource-intensive conditions. While the region has well-established centers for cleft care, there remains a critical gap in capacity for treating syndromic cases. Addressing this disparity will require strategic investment, targeted health policy, and a commitment to equity that ensures every child, regardless of geography, has access to the comprehensive care they need.

4.4 Challenges to treat craniosynostosis in the South of Brazil, at the University

Marcus Vinicius Martins Collares

Plastic and Craniomaxillofacial Division, Federal University of Rio Grande do Sul, Porto Alegre, RS, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):4.4

Hospital Overview

The more active hospitals regarding craniosynostosis treatment in the state of Rio Grande do Sul are:

The Hospital de Clínicas de Porto Alegre, a large university hospital conglomerate, operates with over 1,000 beds and 42 operating rooms (ORs), although 19 remain inactive due to public policy constraints. It has well established groups in pediatrics, craniofacial surgery, genetics, neurosurgery and other associated specialties.The Santa Casa de Misericórdia de Porto Alegre with 1,200 beds and units with operating rooms exclusively focused on neurosurgery (3 ORs) and pediatric surgery (6 Ors). Here are performed most of the craniosynostosis cases.The Moinhos de Vento Hospital, a 485 beds private institution, with 16 operating rooms, collaborates with a well-established craniofacial, pediatric and neurosurgery group, performing many craniofacial cases.

The Hospital de Clínicas de Porto Alegre, a large university hospital conglomerate, operates with over 1,000 beds and 42 operating rooms (ORs), although 19 remain inactive due to public policy constraints. It has well established groups in pediatrics, craniofacial surgery, genetics, neurosurgery and other associated specialties.

Diagnosis and Imaging

Diagnosis is primarily clinical, with imaging examinations such as CT scans and 3D CTs utilized for complex cases or those suspected of neurological damage. MRI is indispensable for syndromic cases. Special attention is needed for partial craniosynostosis; when a sutural closure is incomplete, especially with cranial deformity and anteroposterior shortening, these cases are often misdiagnosed and overlooked.

Objectives Early diagnosis and treatment of severe cases.Treatment of non-severe cases between 6–9 months of age.Focus on brain decompression and craniofacial remodeling.

Early diagnosis and treatment of severe cases.

Early diagnosis and treatment of severe cases.

Treatment of non-severe cases between 6–9 months of age.

Treatment of non-severe cases between 6–9 months of age.

Focus on brain decompression and craniofacial remodeling.

Focus on brain decompression and craniofacial remodeling.

Case Examples Cerebral Decompression: A case of cloverleaf deformity treated at 4 months of age. The bone was extremely fragile, and successful bone release allowed for the removal of the distractor. Reconsideration of screw placement was essential, resulting in satisfactory outcomes.Posterior Fossa Evolution: Addressing both the posterior fossa and anterior cranial structures.Frontofacial Advancement with Lengthening: Testing the efficacy of osteotomies to achieve facial advancement.

Craniofacial Brachycephaly

Case Example 1: Total cranial and orbital remodeling for bicoronal brachycephaly, reducing torrencephaly and improving orbital contours.

Case Example 2: Unilateral craniosynostosis treated with parieto-frontal-orbital remodeling, eliminating the need for absorbable plates and instead using nylon for repositioning and contour improvement.

Post-Operative Care and Outcomes for Non-Syndromic Patients

→Surgical Time: Approximately 152 min per procedure→Blood Loss: 26% blood loss on average, with transfusions required in all cases.→Recovery: Quick recovery, typically extubated immediately post-op with 24–48 h in the ICU. Patients are discharged within 4–5 days.→Follow-up: Review is conducted until at least 8 years of age, ideally until the end of craniofacial skeleton development (around 18 years old).

4.5 Patient’s experience in the Brazilian Private Sector

Renato da Silva Freitas1,2

1Plastic Surgery Unit of Federal University of Paraná, Curitiba (UFPR), Curitiba, PR Brazil; 2 Assistance Center for Cleft Lip and Palate, Hospital do Trabalhador SESA PR, Curitiba, PR Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):4.5

Introduction

Syndromic craniosynostoses, such as Crouzon and Apert syndromes, are rare congenital disorders caused primarily by mutations in the fibroblast growth factor receptor (FGFR) genes. These syndromes are characterized by premature fusion of cranial sutures, midface hypoplasia, and variable neurodevelopmental and respiratory challenges. Surgical management typically involves multiple staged interventions throughout the patient’s growth, aiming to alleviate intracranial hypertension, improve airway function, and address craniofacial deformities.

The Assistance Center for Cleft Lip and Palate (CAIF), based at the Hospital do Trabalhador in Curitiba, Brazil, has been active since 2002. The center performs approximately 100 cleft lip and palate surgeries per month and manages 2–3 complex craniofacial procedures monthly. This report presents a descriptive analysis of the center’s experience in managing patients with Crouzon and Apert syndromes, with emphasis on patient demographics, timing of presentation, surgical decision-making, and functional outcomes.

Crouzon Syndrome

Among the 22 patients with Crouzon syndrome, there were 12 females and 10 males. Cranial morphology at presentation included 19 cases of brachycephaly and 3 of scaphocephaly.

Age at presentation:Under 1 year: 3 patientsBetween 1 and 4 years: 5 patientsBetween 5 and 15 years: 7 patientsOver 16 years: 7 patients

Under 1 year: 3 patients

Under 1 year: 3 patients

Between 1 and 4 years: 5 patients

Between 1 and 4 years: 5 patients

Between 5 and 15 years: 7 patients

Between 5 and 15 years: 7 patients

Over 16 years: 7 patients

Over 16 years: 7 patients

Many patients had undergone previous surgeries at other institutions and were referred for evaluation of residual deformities or functional concerns, particularly obstructive sleep apnea (OSA). Surgical plans were adapted based on prior interventions, midface retrusion severity, and respiratory function.

Representative cases:A female patient referred at 6 months underwent posterior cranial decompression, followed by fronto-orbital advancement at 17 months and midface advancement at age 3. By age 8, craniofacial growth was stable, and respiratory symptoms had improved.A 10-year-old patient with no prior treatment underwent monobloc advancement with positive long-term outcomes after 4 years of follow-up. A 14-year-old with prior fronto-orbital advancement presented with persistent midface hypoplasia and underwent Le Fort III osteotomy, with sustained improvement over 3 years. A notable familial case involved a mother and daughter, both with Crouzon syndrome and scaphocephaly. The mother underwent Le Fort III at the center; her daughter was operated on at age 7, with favorable advancement and functional outcomes.

Apert Syndrome

Among 31 patients with Apert syndrome, there were 19 females and 12 males. Cranial morphology included 29 cases of brachycephaly and 2 of plagiocephaly.

Age at presentation:Under 1 year: 18 patientsBetween 1 and 4 years: 5 patientsBetween 5 and 15 years: 6 patientsOver 16 years: 2 patients

Under 1 year: 18 patients

Under 1 year: 18 patients

Between 1 and 4 years: 5 patients

Between 1 and 4 years: 5 patients

Between 5 and 15 years: 6 patients

Between 5 and 15 years: 6 patients

Over 16 years: 2 patients

Over 16 years: 2 patients

Due to more overt craniofacial features, patients with Apert syndrome tended to present earlier than those with Crouzon syndrome. In some cases, recurrence of synostosis necessitated additional interventions.

Representative cases:A newborn with marked right-sided brachycephaly underwent fronto-orbital advancement at 6 months, but showed recurrence by age 2. At age 8, with an AHI of 12 events/hour, the patient underwent Le Fort III advancement, with stable progression. At age 14, the patient was undergoing orthodontic preparation for maxillomandibular advancement. Another patient, diagnosed in infancy, underwent posterior decompression and fronto-orbital advancement by age 3, with favorable craniofacial development at age 4. Of 10 patients initially treated with fronto-orbital advancement, 5 later required Le Fort III, 1 underwent monobloc advancement, and 7 underwent posterior decompression.A significant case involved a 43-year-old female previously untreated, who sought care after bringing her son for evaluation. She underwent Le Fort III advancement due to severe OSA, with substantial clinical improvement. Another patient who had undergone fronto-orbital advancement at age 2 presented with persistent OSA. At age 6, she underwent Le Fort III with skeletal support using regional flaps, achieving clinical stability by age 18 and awaiting orthognathic refinement.

A newborn with marked right-sided brachycephaly underwent fronto-orbital advancement at 6 months, but showed recurrence by age 2. At age 8, with an AHI of 12 events/hour, the patient underwent Le Fort III advancement, with stable progression. At age 14, the patient was undergoing orthodontic preparation for maxillomandibular advancement.

Another patient who had undergone fronto-orbital advancement at age 2 presented with persistent OSA. At age 6, she underwent Le Fort III with skeletal support using regional flaps, achieving clinical stability by age 18 and awaiting orthognathic refinement.

Another patient who had undergone fronto-orbital advancement at age 2 presented with persistent OSA. At age 6, she underwent Le Fort III with skeletal support using regional flaps, achieving clinical stability by age 18 and awaiting orthognathic refinement.

Conclusion

The surgical management of Crouzon and Apert syndromes requires a multidisciplinary, individualized approach. Age at presentation, prior surgical history, and functional impairments,including airway compromise and intracranial hypertension, inform treatment planning. This case series illustrates the diversity of clinical trajectories and underscores the need for long-term follow-up, surgical adaptability, and integrated care to optimize outcomes for patients with syndromic craniosynostoses.

5 The first year: multidisciplinary care for the neonate and infant with craniosynostosis

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5

Moderators: Dr. Adriano Yacubian and Dr. Nelci Zanon

5.1 The Comprehensive Care Model at Boston Children’s Hospital

Jay Berry

Department of Pediatrics and Plastic and Oral Surgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA.

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5.1

Introduction

This section aims to describe the patient’s experiences with craniosynostosis care, focusing on our model of care. Specific aspects of care activities include screening and diagnosis, shared decision-making for surgery, preoperative preparation, and postoperative care.

Model of Care

Boston Children’s Hospital’s craniosynostosis care approach uses the American Society of Anesthesiologists’ Perioperative Surgical Home model, a coordinated system of perioperative care centered on the patient and family. An integrated interdisciplinary craniosynostosis team whose mission is to provide the safest, most efficient, and clinically effective perioperative care. The core team consists of surgeons, anesthesiologists, and ophthalmologists. Additional team members include a clinical coordinator, physician’s assistant, and complex care pediatrician. The clinical coordinator manages referrals, diagnostic testing, and operating room scheduling and is a primary point of contact for families, enhancing the patient experience.

Screening and Diagnosis

The screening and diagnosis process involves a head shape assessment team that receives referrals from community pediatricians and cranial orthotists. The team distinguishes between benign and concerning head shapes, triaging accordingly and conducting necessary imaging and surgical reviews. Early education and reassurance for families are crucial components of this process.

Shared Decision-Making

Shared decision-making is a collaborative model that involves patients, families, and clinicians in mutually agreeing on treatment plans. This approach promotes transparent discussions about surgical goals, strategies, risks, and benefits. Comprehensive health and risk assessments are conducted for each child, considering all medical and social factors that may impact perioperative safety.

Preoperative Preparation

For children undergoing surgery, preoperative preparation includes:Hematopoietic Optimization: Involving iron and Epogen administration to increase hemoglobin levels, reducing the need for intraoperative transfusions.Comprehensive Health Evaluation: The anesthesiology team conducts a comprehensive evaluation to mitigate all potential risks.

Hematopoietic Optimization: Involving iron and Epogen administration to increase hemoglobin levels, reducing the need for intraoperative transfusions.

Hematopoietic Optimization: Involving iron and Epogen administration to increase hemoglobin levels, reducing the need for intraoperative transfusions.

Comprehensive Health Evaluation: The anesthesiology team conducts a comprehensive evaluation to mitigate all potential risks.

Comprehensive Health Evaluation: The anesthesiology team conducts a comprehensive evaluation to mitigate all potential risks.

Postoperative Care

Postoperative care options include inpatient neurosurgery and plastic surgery teams, as well as a dedicated complex care hospitalist team for children with medical complexity. Healthcare providers schedule regular follow-ups through age five and continue care for those with ongoing needs.

Reflections

Integrating diverse specialists into the craniosynostosis care team has been highly effective. In-person collaboration enhances the patient and family experience, fostering trust and improving clinical outcomes. The success of this model is evident in the gratitude expressed by families and the optimized health of the children treated.

Conclusion

The comprehensive care model for craniosynostosis at Boston Children’s Hospital exemplifies the benefits of interdisciplinary collaboration and patient-centered care. The team’s commitment to innovation and continuous improvement ensures that each child receives the highest quality care, enabling them to live their best lives.

5.2 Where and how to get access to a craniofacial reference center

Natan Monsores1,2

1Department of Public Health, University of Brasilia, Brasilia, DF, Brazil; 2General Coordination of Rare Diseases—Brazilian Ministry of Health, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5.2

Introduction

In response to the increasing visibility and urgency surrounding rare diseases, Brazil’s Ministry of Health established the General Coordination for Rare Diseases, a dedicated body tasked with implementing actions and policies to improve care within the Unified Health System (SUS). This initiative stems from Portaria nº 199 and the Política Nacional de Atenção Integral às Pessoas com Doenças Raras (PNAIPDR), a national policy framework centered on inclusive, person-centered care.

Far from being conditions too rare to prioritize, many rare diseases are chronic, complex, and deeply impactful for families. The care model must focus not solely on the diseases but on the individuals living with them, offering structured, multidisciplinary support throughout the patient’s therapeutic journey.

A Person-Centered Approach to Rare Diseases

At the core of Brazil’s rare disease policy is a paradigm shift: from disease-centric to person-centered care. This approach emphasizes:Quality of life as a central objectiveMultisystem involvement in syndromic conditionsFamily-centered support networksEquitable access to specialized technologies and treatments

Quality of life as a central objective

Quality of life as a central objective

Multisystem involvement in syndromic conditions

Multisystem involvement in syndromic conditions

Family-centered support networks

Family-centered support networks

Equitable access to specialized technologies and treatments

Equitable access to specialized technologies and treatments

Rare diseases often have a strong genetic basis and affect multiple organ systems, necessitating collaboration across a wide range of specialties. No single provider can manage these conditions alone, hence comprehensive, team-based care is essential.

Challenges in Classification and Tracking

One of the persistent challenges lies in the classification and monitoring of rare diseases. Brazil currently relies on ICD-10 (CID-10), which frequently fails to capture syndromic craniofacial anomalies and other complex presentations. The expected implementation of ICD-11 in 2025 is anticipated to improve diagnostic specificity, enable better tracking of rare conditions, and support epidemiological analysis.

The government also draws on international classification systems, such as Orphanet, to identify services, patient demographics, and clinical needs. However, even with such tools, real-time patient identification remains limited, especially during early clinical encounters.

The Therapeutic Journey: From Suspicion to Diagnosis

A critical gap in Brazil’s rare disease infrastructure is the diagnostic delay, patients often wait five to ten years from first suspicion to confirmed diagnosis. This prolonged timeline jeopardizes early intervention, particularly in conditions where treatment windows are narrow.

The Ministry aims to reduce this delay by:Improving initial screening protocolsEnsuring early documentation of morphological anomalies (e.g., through the Declaração de Nascido Vivo—Live Birth Declaration)Strengthening referral and counter-referral systems across levels of care

Improving initial screening protocols

Improving initial screening protocols

Ensuring early documentation of morphological anomalies (e.g., through the Declaração de Nascido Vivo—Live Birth Declaration)

Ensuring early documentation of morphological anomalies (e.g., through the Declaração de Nascido Vivo—Live Birth Declaration)

Strengthening referral and counter-referral systems across levels of care

Strengthening referral and counter-referral systems across levels of care

Unfortunately, this process is frequently neglected or improperly executed at birth, representing a lost opportunity for early intervention.

Developing Protocols and Access to Therapies

The Ministry of Health is actively publishing Clinical Protocols and Therapeutic Guidelines (PCDTs) for a growing list of rare diseases. Although not yet comprehensive, protocols are expanding in alignment with new treatments and robust evidence. Currently, eight protocols are in development, with additional care pathways underway, including for craniofacial anomalies.

These guidelines aim to:Standardize care pathwaysSupport clinician decision-makingEnable patients to access high-complexity treatments through SUS

Standardize care pathways

Standardize care pathways

Support clinician decision-making

Support clinician decision-making

Enable patients to access high-complexity treatments through SUS

Enable patients to access high-complexity treatments through SUS

Strengthening the Care Network

The Brazilian model recognizes the need for comprehensive care that spans from primary care to super-specialized services. Rare disease reference centers serve as bridges, facilitating:Coordinated care across levelsMonitoring of service delivery, workforce, diagnostics, and patient flowsIdentification of service gaps to strategically allocate professionals and resources

Coordinated care across levels

Coordinated care across levels

Monitoring of service delivery, workforce, diagnostics, and patient flows

Monitoring of service delivery, workforce, diagnostics, and patient flows

Identification of service gaps to strategically allocate professionals and resources

Identification of service gaps to strategically allocate professionals and resources

This system allows the Ministry to map needs, such as where geneticists or pediatric specialists are lacking, and to redirect funding where necessary.

Strategic Priorities Moving Forward

To advance equity and effectiveness in rare disease care, key priorities include:Early Identification: Syndromic presentations should be flagged at birth, enabling early referrals to specialized centers. Proper documentation, especially of visible craniofacial or morphological differences, is critical from day one.Multidisciplinary Sensitization: Conditions such as syndromic craniosynostoses require coordination across specialties. Primary care providers, pediatricians, geneticists, and surgeons must be aligned in recognizing the complexity of these patients.Classification Improvements: Transitioning to ICD-11 will enhance our ability to capture, categorize, and monitor rare conditions.Diagnostic and Treatment Infrastructure: More resources must be allocated for diagnostics, genetic testing, and access to novel therapies. Diagnosis without available care leads only to frustration.Legislative and Policy Support: The foundation of the Rare Disease Coordination under formal legal frameworks ensures that rare disease care has a protected space within the national health system.Empowered Reference Services: These centers are critical nodes within SUS, designed not only to deliver care but to provide training, data collection, and cross-sector coordination.

Early Identification: Syndromic presentations should be flagged at birth, enabling early referrals to specialized centers. Proper documentation, especially of visible craniofacial or morphological differences, is critical from day one.

Early Identification: Syndromic presentations should be flagged at birth, enabling early referrals to specialized centers. Proper documentation, especially of visible craniofacial or morphological differences, is critical from day one.

Multidisciplinary Sensitization: Conditions such as syndromic craniosynostoses require coordination across specialties. Primary care providers, pediatricians, geneticists, and surgeons must be aligned in recognizing the complexity of these patients.

Multidisciplinary Sensitization: Conditions such as syndromic craniosynostoses require coordination across specialties. Primary care providers, pediatricians, geneticists, and surgeons must be aligned in recognizing the complexity of these patients.

Classification Improvements: Transitioning to ICD-11 will enhance our ability to capture, categorize, and monitor rare conditions.

Classification Improvements: Transitioning to ICD-11 will enhance our ability to capture, categorize, and monitor rare conditions.

Diagnostic and Treatment Infrastructure: More resources must be allocated for diagnostics, genetic testing, and access to novel therapies. Diagnosis without available care leads only to frustration.

Diagnostic and Treatment Infrastructure: More resources must be allocated for diagnostics, genetic testing, and access to novel therapies. Diagnosis without available care leads only to frustration.

Legislative and Policy Support: The foundation of the Rare Disease Coordination under formal legal frameworks ensures that rare disease care has a protected space within the national health system.

Legislative and Policy Support: The foundation of the Rare Disease Coordination under formal legal frameworks ensures that rare disease care has a protected space within the national health system.

Empowered Reference Services: These centers are critical nodes within SUS, designed not only to deliver care but to provide training, data collection, and cross-sector coordination.

Empowered Reference Services: These centers are critical nodes within SUS, designed not only to deliver care but to provide training, data collection, and cross-sector coordination.

Conclusion

The path forward for rare disease care in Brazil involves early identification, legal protection, integrated service delivery, and targeted resource allocation. While much progress has been made, challenges remain in classification, timely diagnosis, and equitable access to specialized treatment. Syndromic craniosynostoses exemplify the need for a coordinated, person-centered approach that leverages multidisciplinary expertise, strengthens data systems, and empowers families to navigate their care journey with dignity and support.

By reinforcing reference centers, improving early screening, and expanding therapeutic guidelines, Brazil is laying the groundwork for a more responsive and inclusive rare disease strategy—one that honors the principle that rare does not mean invisible.

5.3 Patient’s experience in the French Public Sector

Eric Arnaud1,2

1Craniofacial Surgery Department, University Hospital Necker—Enfants Malades, Paris, France; 2Centre de Competences pour les maladies crânio-faciales, Clinique Marcel Sembat-Ramsay, Boulogne-Billancourt, France

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5.3

Introduction

Early identification and referral are essential for effective treatment of syndromic craniosynostosis. From the first clinical encounter, health systems should be equipped to recognize possible rare diseases and refer patients to specialized centers. These centers bring together multidisciplinary teams capable of addressing the complex needs of these children, including craniofacial, neurological, respiratory, and genetic evaluations. When recognition occurs early, it is possible to initiate treatment sooner, significantly altering the clinical trajectory and improving long-term outcomes.

The Importance of Early Multidisciplinary Assessment

At our institution, most patients with faciocraniosynostosis undergo monobloc frontofacial advancement. Among those 10% of the patients are operated on early by 18 months of age, with individualized planning based on urgency and anatomy. A complete assessment at a reference center includes evaluation of vision, intracranial pressure (ICP), respiratory function, and other systemic conditions. Even simple interventions, such as eyelid sutures or nasal tubes, can play a crucial role in preserving function and preventing complications while waiting for definitive surgical correction.

Surgical Pathways and Strategies

In cases of raised ICP, decompressive osteotomies, particularly in the early stages, are used to relieve pressure and allow brain development. For exorbitism, a monobloc procedure is often required to protect the eyes and correct midface deficiency. Airway compromise may necessitate early tracheostomy or nasal support. Historically, treatment followed a two-stage protocol, but a three-stage strategy is now frequently used, especially for more severe cases.

One such strategy includes: Posterior cranial vault expansion (for ICP and Chiari malformations)Monobloc advancement (to address orbital protection and breathing)Le Fort III osteotomy at a later stage, once the patient reaches skeletal maturity

Posterior cranial vault expansion (for ICP and Chiari malformations)

Posterior cranial vault expansion (for ICP and Chiari malformations)

Monobloc advancement (to address orbital protection and breathing)

Monobloc advancement (to address orbital protection and breathing)

Le Fort III osteotomy at a later stage, once the patient reaches skeletal maturity

Le Fort III osteotomy at a later stage, once the patient reaches skeletal maturity

Where feasible, Le Fort III bipartition with internal and external distraction can be performed. Internal devices are used primarily for consolidation, while external distraction offers more controlled advancement. This staged approach allows greater adaptability based on growth and airway stability.

Representative Clinical Cases

Patient 1 (3 months old): Underwent monobloc advancement due to severe, non-operable airway compromise. Although technically challenging, the monobloc was successful at this very early age.Patient 2 (4 months old): A frontofacial monobloc was attempted with a 2.2 mm trans-facial pin from the maxilla to the orbit, using mild traction (2.5 kg) over time. Although there was zygomatic calcification, the bone lacked sufficient mechanical support. Absence of tongue-and-groove fixation and only two distractors led to relapse within one year. A second procedure, combined fronto-orbital and Le Fort III advancement, allowed tracheostomy removal.Patient 3 (16 months old): Underwent conventional monobloc at 2 years of age. Le Fort III was performed at age 13. The patient was able to attend school and lead a functionally normal life throughout childhood.Patient 4 (Pfeiffer Syndrome): Underwent monobloc with internal distractors and trans-facial pin. Overcorrection allowed room for brain growth. Later required bilateral mandibular distraction to normalize occlusion. Attending school by age 6, with Le Fort III at 14 and orthognathic surgery later in adolescence.Patient 5 (Crouzon with hydrocephalus and cloverleaf skull, FGFR3 mutation): Required posterior expansion followed by monobloc using a reversed distractor on the vertex to contract and reshape the skull. Le Fort III was performed at age 12.Patient 6 (Severe Pfeiffer Syndrome at 1 year): Underwent posterior expansion followed by monobloc with four distractors, external traction via trans-facial pin, and an aggressive suspension system.Patient 7 (14 months old): Referred late. Due to absence of early intervention, fibrotic tissue developed, compromising skeletal stabilization and necessitating more aggressive surgical measures.

Patient 7 (14 months old): Referred late. Due to absence of early intervention, fibrotic tissue developed, compromising skeletal stabilization and necessitating more aggressive surgical measures.

Key Principles in Airway Management

Early tracheostomy and timely decannulation are among the most difficult and critical challenges. Prolonged airway support is associated with high morbidity and mortality. Many of our treatment plans are designed around strategies to enable early decannulation, especially through monobloc and midface advancements.

Lessons Learned Vision: If visual impairment or risk of blindness is suspected, early evaluation and surgical protection are essential.Raised ICP: Decompression is required, posterior expansion is often the first step.Exorbitism: Indicates the need for early monobloc.Severe airway compromise: May require a combination of monobloc and tracheostomy.Chiari malformation: Often best addressed through posterior expansion with distraction.

Vision: If visual impairment or risk of blindness is suspected, early evaluation and surgical protection are essential.

Chiari malformation: Often best addressed through posterior expansion with distraction.

Conclusion

The monobloc frontofacial advancement (FFMBA) is a life-saving procedure in selected patients and can be safely performed in infancy with careful planning. When used early and appropriately, FFMBA enables decannulation in approximately 70% of tracheostomized patients. These complex cases require more than one procedure across a patient’s life. Therefore, successful outcomes rely on early referral, a staged surgical approach, and long-term follow-up within a well-coordinated, multidisciplinary network. The ultimate goal is not only structural correction but also preservation of neurocognitive development, functional breathing, and quality of life, including school attendance and social integration.

Publication of all patient information was authorized through explicit, informed consent obtained from the patients and their guardians.

5.4 Patient’s experience in the Brazilian Private Sector

Goldete Priszkulnik1,2,3

1Brazilian Society of Medical Audit, Brazil; 2The Brazilian Journal of Medical Audit, Brazil; 3 Institutional Affairs at the International Society for Pharmacoeconomics and Outcomes Research—ISPOR—Brazil Chapter

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5.4

To understand the complexities of healthcare in Brazil, specifically in the case of syndromic craniosynostosis, it is essential to understand the functioning of the Brazilian health system. Brazil is located in South America being the largest country in the southern cone. It has continental dimensions, covering 8,514,876 km2 and an estimated population of approximately 203 million inhabitants according to the demographic census carried out in 2022.1 Its population is unevenly distributed, mainly concentrated in the southern and southeastern regions.1 Brazil is divided into 26 states, 5,570 municipalities, and the federal district, which is located in the central-west region.1

The health system is public and universal (SUS) and must provide care coverage for the entire Brazilian population, from primary care to quaternary attention, including transplants, cancer, and high-cost drugs.2 The funding is provided through financial transfers from the federal level to states and municipalities and from the state level to municipalities through percentages agreed among federative entities.2

The private sector, known as supplementary health, covers 25% of the Brazilian population. Coverage in the private sector is directly linked to employability, considering that 85% of plans are related to companies that provide health plans for their employees.3 The population served by supplementary health is concentrated in the southern and southeastern regions of the country, with over 55% of the population.3. Another region with a higher density of assisted people is the central-west, mainly due to the federal district.3 There are various modalities of health plan operators active in the Brazilian market, each with its business characteristics: medical cooperatives, group medicines (HMO), health insurers, and self-managements.4

Regardless of the modality of the health plan operator that will offer care coverage for syndromic craniosynostosis, the difficulties of the care journey will be the same. Rare genetic syndromes have diagnostic difficulties precisely because they are rare and little known by doctors and professionals involved in maternal and infant care.5

Prenatal diagnosis through morphological ultrasound examinations in the last trimester can be a way to identify craniosynostosis. However, not all medical centers and services are equipped to perform such detailed ultrasounds, nor do they always have professionals with the expertise necessary to interpret the images. These exams may reveal anomalies in the skull, face, hands, and feet, which could indicate a possible syndromic fetus.5

Postnatal diagnosis, in most cases, will require a complete clinical assessment and specialized tests, including molecular genetic testing, which is used to confirm the diagnosis at birth or during the first months of infancy.5 Complex cases require multi-professional follow-up and multiple surgeries at reference centers. These are cases that require continuous care throughout life. Ideally, treatment should begin soon after birth, and diagnosis should be made as soon as possible. A multidisciplinary approach (respiratory, cerebral, dental, ocular, and orthopedic) is necessary since the syndrome affects several systems.5

In Brazilian reality, in the sphere of supplementary health, care coverage for the surgical follow-up of anomalies found in these children is assured. Multidisciplinary follow-up, including dental care, is also contemplated if the family has dental assistance. The necessary therapies for neuropsychomotor development have full coverage, with services in physiotherapy, speech therapy, occupational therapy, psychology, and nutrition.6

The most considerable difficulty will always be having centers of excellence offering comprehensive treatment for these children. We have a few places in Brazil that can offer global and complete assistance with all resources available in one place. In a continental country like Brazil, this is certainly the biggest challenge, in addition to disseminating knowledge about pathology and the need for early intervention.

The patient’s experience in the Brazilian private health sector will be much faster than the experience in the public service network. After diagnosis and request for procedures, the private network must comply with maximum service deadlines according to normative resolutions issued by the health supplement regulator, the National Health Agency—ANS.7

Patients with syndromic craniosynostosis typically require lifelong care, although there is currently no effective treatment beyond surgeries and therapies aimed at neuropsychomotor development.8 Our role as players in the supplementary health sector is to help disseminate knowledge about pathologies related to syndromic craniosynostosis so that diagnoses are made as early as possible and surgical interventions can be carried out at the opportune moment, aiming for the child’s best development.

5.5 The first year of life: multidisciplinary care for craniosynostosis in the Brazilian Public Health System

Isabel Cristina Drago Marquezini Salmen1,2

1Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Hospital for the Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5.5

Care for children with syndromic craniosynostosis requires an integrated, multidisciplinary approach capable of addressing complex anatomical, functional, and developmental challenges from birth through childhood.1,2,3 At a specialized reference center, this care model is built around close collaboration between multiple specialties, with the pediatrician playing a central role in coordinating evaluations, guiding early management, and ensuring continuity of care across all stages of treatment.

At Hospital de Reabilitação de Anomalias Craniofaciais (HRAC/Centrinho), children with syndromic craniosynostosis are managed through a multidisciplinary model that brings together professionals from multiple specialties with expertise in craniofacial conditions. In this context, the pediatrician plays a fundamental role in integrating care across disciplines, monitoring the child longitudinally, and ensuring that systemic complications are identified and addressed early.

Prenatal diagnosis has become increasingly relevant in this context. Advances in diagnostic capabilities have led more families to seek pediatricians and surgeons even before birth. This early engagement allows the healthcare team to provide clear information to parents, reducing anxiety by explaining anticipated treatment stages and clinical evolution. It is important that the team is prepared to provide clear information to reduce parental anxiety, explain the stages of treatment, and discuss expected outcomes.

Prenatal diagnosis is also important for the team that will receive the child, as some newborns may present with airway compromise requiring advanced airway management immediately after birth, or may require cesarean delivery due to cranial disproportion. While imaging studies contribute to diagnosis, clinical assessment remains essential, particularly in specialized centers, where attention to signs and symptoms can accelerate diagnosis and treatment.4 Familiarity with syndrome-specific features, such as syndactyly in Apert syndrome, midface hypoplasia in Crouzon syndrome, or associated cardiac findings in Pfeiffer syndrome, allows pediatricians to direct timely referrals and expedite multidisciplinary evaluation.3,5,6 Genetic assessment supports diagnostic confirmation, but early clinical suspicion remains central to care.

Airway management represents one of the most frequent and serious challenges in these patients.5 Many children present with significant upper airway obstruction and a high prevalence of obstructive sleep apnea related to craniofacial anatomy, including midface deficiency and laryngotracheal anomalies. Importantly, children with initially mild deformities may later develop clinically relevant sleep-disordered breathing. In our practice, airway evaluation may include endoscopy to identify the level of obstruction. Management strategies include the use of nasopharyngeal tubes, non-invasive ventilatory support, and tracheostomy in severe cases. Surgical advancement of the midface can contribute to airway improvement in selected patients.

Feeding difficulties are also common and frequently associated with respiratory compromise.3 Children who struggle to breathe effectively often cannot feed orally. Our service relies on a specialized dysphagia team composed of pediatricians, neurologists, and other professionals to manage these complex cases. Feeding strategies range from adapted breastfeeding techniques and specialized nipples to nasogastric feeding, gastrostomy, and, in rare cases, parenteral nutrition. Nutritional status and growth are closely monitored throughout follow-up.

Neurological involvement requires continuous surveillance. Increased intracranial pressure may result from premature suture fusion, venous abnormalities, hydrocephalus, or chronic respiratory obstruction.2,7,8 Pediatricians monitor for clinical signs of intracranial hypertension, recognizing that diagnosis can be challenging, particularly in infants. Persistently elevated intracranial pressure may occur even after cranial expansion, underscoring the need for ongoing follow-up and multidisciplinary reassessment.

Visual and auditory complications are frequently observed.5,6 Visual loss may arise from amblyopia or optic nerve compromise related to intracranial hypertension, while exophthalmos increases the risk of corneal injury. Hearing disorders, often conductive, place children at risk for speech and language delays.6 Our multidisciplinary team includes ophthalmology and otolaryngology specialists to address these issues early and longitudinally. In our experience, structured follow-up has demonstrated normalization of language abilities in approximately two thirds of patients.

Psychosocial and neurodevelopmental aspects are integral to care. Children with syndromic craniosynostosis are at increased risk for cognitive deficits, attention difficulties, and social challenges. Early identification and intervention are essential, as timely support may significantly reduce or prevent long-term impairment. Recommended testing for children with syndromic craniosynostosis includes psychodiagnostic testing at age 2 years and when entering elementary school; additional screening for learning disabilities should occur at approximately 7 to 9 years of age.6 Psychological follow-up is particularly important during critical periods, including diagnosis, surgical treatment, and adolescence.

Postoperative care is another central responsibility of the pediatrician. These children undergo multiple complex surgical procedures and require close monitoring for complications such as blood loss, electrolyte disturbances, infections, and neurological changes. In our experience, standardized perioperative protocols and intensive care monitoring have contributed to low morbidity and mortality. Our postoperative protocol includes careful fluid management, prophylactic antibiotics, analgesia, correction of anemia and electrolyte imbalances, and close monitoring in the intensive care unit.

In summary, the pediatrician serves as the primary integrator of care for children with syndromic craniosynostosis, bridging specialties and maintaining a holistic view of the child’s health and development. Experience from a specialized reference center underscores that early diagnosis, vigilant longitudinal follow-up, and sustained multidisciplinary collaboration are essential to optimizing outcomes for these complex patients.

5.6 High-quality information: role of parents associations

Élida Brandão

Patient & Family Partner | (@craniossinostosetemcura- Instagram)

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):5.6

Introduction

This presentation explores the transformative role of parent-led associations in providing high-quality information, emotional support, and advocacy for families navigating craniosynostosis. Drawing from her personal experience as a mother, Élida Brandão shares how her son Bento’s diagnosis catalyzed the creation of a communication-centered network rooted in empathy, affective listening, and accessible health education.

A Personal Journey into Advocacy

Élida’s involvement began when Bento was diagnosed with craniosynostosis. Like many families, she faced an overwhelming emotional and informational void at the time of diagnosis. In response, she began documenting her family’s experience, seeking and sharing reliable health information, and ultimately creating a structured platform for other parents in similar situations. Her grassroots effort soon evolved into a robust digital community of care and empowerment.

Communication as a Tool for Healing

Currently pursuing a master’s degree in communication, Élida’s advocacy is grounded in affective listening, a practice of responding to emotional distress with informed empathy. This principle guides her daily work on social media platforms, especially through Instagram (@craniossinostose_tem_cura) and organized WhatsApp support groups. Her mission is not only to inform but to humanize the medical journey for families.

Impact and Reach

Through strategic use of digital platforms, the initiative now reaches over 5,000 followers and provides active support to more than 400 families across Brazil. The content includes curated medical information, personal stories, professional recommendations, and real-time peer support, fostering a space where families can share practical insights and emotional strength.

Navigating Care through Peer Support

Élida’s own experience in São Luís, Maranhão, a city with limited access to pediatric neurosurgical services, highlights the importance of peer-to-peer guidance. Her advocacy underscores how community recommendations can bridge geographical and systemic gaps in access to timely and quality care. Through the network, many families have been able to access specialized surgical centers they may not have otherwise known about.

Information as Empowerment

For many parents, the fear surrounding craniosynostosis is amplified by a lack of clear, compassionate communication. Élida emphasizes the importance of preparing families for surgery, recovery, and long-term outcomes through accessible and accurate information. Empowering families with knowledge transforms the narrative from fear to agency.

Building a Caring Community

The platform serves as more than an information hub, it is a lifeline of solidarity. Parents exchange tips, share trusted referrals, support each other through the perioperative process, and celebrate each child’s progress. The organization also triages daily messages from parents in distress, responding with sensitivity and building trust through active listening and shared experience.

From Support to Structural Advocacy

Beyond daily support, the association engages in broader advocacy efforts, promoting the rights to health, communication, and dignified care. Their work demonstrates the critical need for partnerships between civil society, healthcare professionals, and public institutions to improve care pathways for rare craniofacial conditions.

Conclusion

Parent-led associations like Craniossinostose Tem Cura are reshaping the landscape of craniosynostosis care in Brazil. By combining affective listening, structured communication, and peer solidarity, these networks transform individual pain into collective resilience. They are not only support systems but key actors in advancing compassionate, rights-based healthcare.

Acknowledgements

Élida expresses her gratitude to the mothers and collaborators who have built this network alongside her, Monalisa, Melissa, Aline and Nathan from Belo Horizonte, Amanda, and many others. She dedicates her advocacy to Bento and all families facing craniosynostosis, stating: “Each time we help another person, it is our way of thanking God for the cure.”

Ethical Acknowledgment

This section was written based on Élida words and presentation at the Bauru Symposium and was reviewed and authorized by her to share her story.

6 Neurosurgical management: chiari and hydrocephalus

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):6

Moderators: Dr. Franklin Rocha and Dr. Nelci Zanon

6.1 Hospital for rehabilitation of craniofacial anomalies—Bauru—Brazil

Michele Brandão1,2

1Faculdade de Medicina de Bauru, Universidade de São Paulo, Bauru, SP, Brazil; 2Hospital de Reabilitação de Anomalias Crânio-Faciais, São Paulo, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):6.1

Neurosurgical management: Intracranial Pressure

The evaluation of intracranial pressure (ICP) in patients with craniosynostosis is crucial due to the prevalence of intracranial hypertension (ICH), which represents one of the main concerns related to these patients (1,2). At the Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies (HRAC), the assessment of ICH is based on imaging tests, evaluation of the optic nerve head through retinography, and recently, non-invasive monitoring of ICP using the brain4care monitor and measurement of the optic nerve sheath diameter by ultrasound. The patients treated at HRAC face challenges such as the great distances separating their hometowns from specialized centers and the low socioeconomic level of their families. To overcome difficulties like these, patients stay up in the city to be evaluated by the multidisciplinary team and have the exams done.

The images (tomography scans and magnetic resonance) may reveal signs of ICH, such as sulci and cisterns rarefaction, displacement of cerebellar tonsils, hydrocephalus, and disproportion of the cranial volume. Depending on the patient’s degree of cooperation, we perform retinography in the outpatient clinic with a photographic recording of the eye fundus (using Phelcom Eyer) and noninvasive monitoring with the brain4care monitor. For patients who do not cooperate with the tests (due to age or cognitive alteration), the assessment was conducted in the surgical center under anesthesia, according to a protocol previously established to minimize the effects of anesthesia on intracranial pressure (3). The image obtained by the retinographer was classified according to the Frisen classification (4), which follows these criteria: Grade 0: no halo of obscuration of the peripapillary nerve fiber layer; Grade 1: a C-shaped halo of edema of the retinal nerve fiber layer obscuring the peripapillary retina; Grade 2: the halo is now circumferential, with no temporal gap and no vessel obscuration; Grade 3: major vessels are obscured by edema as they exit the disc; Grade 4: major vessels are now obscured by edema on the optic disc; Grade 5: all vessels are at least partially obscured by edema.

For non-invasive ICP monitoring with the Brain4care monitor, the patient must be quiet and lying down with the head of the bed at 30 degrees. The monitor works by capturing and amplifying the micro-oscillations of the skull caused by the pulsation of ICP (5). Briefly, the pulses of the ICP curve result from the dispersion of blood volume caused by each cardiac cycle within the skull and the mechanisms of cerebral autoregulation (6). Each ICP pulse has at least three peaks that consistently appear, named P1, P2, and P3(7). P1 reflects the blood volume added to the cerebral blood circulation, and P2 shows the dispersion of this volume through the blood vessels. Under normal conditions, the P1 peak is higher than P2 and P3, but if there is an increase in resistance to the dispersion of blood inside the skull, P2 rises and becomes higher than P1 (8,9). This situation indicates that cranial compliance (CC) is reduced and can lead to an ICH when the mechanisms of cerebral autoregulation are exhausted (10). The reduction in compliance is demonstrated in two ways: by the P2/P1 index and by the waveform. The P2/P1 index is considered normal when less than one, intermediate between 1 and 1.2, serving as an alert and elevated above 1.2. As P2 becomes higher than P1, the waveform changes, losing its characteristic aspect and becoming triangular or rounded (11) (Fig. 1).

Since 2021, 42 sequential evaluations have been conducted on 35 patients (analysis done until 2023). Two patients had papilledema and altered non-invasive curves (class 3 and 4) with a P2/P1 index above 1.2 (12). These two individuals had yet to be operated on at the assessment time. Nine patients showed alteration in non-invasive ICP monitoring and did not have papilledema. Seven of these individuals were assessed before surgery, reinforcing the need for meticulous evaluation of intracranial pressure. Recently, we added the measurement of the diameter of the optic nerve sheath to the routine for patients, but we still need more data for a complete statistical analysis.

Patients with craniosynostosis undergo surgery to prevent or control ICH, and the technique depends on the diagnosis and age of the individual. The hospital’s routine includes posterior cranial decompression (and facial advancement posteriorly), frontofacial advancement, and, more recently, endoscopic suturectomy.

6.2 Management of hydrocephalus in craniosynostosis patient in Sobrapar Hospital—Campinas—Brazil

Enrico Ghizoni1,2, Cássio Raposo do Amaral3

1Faculty of Medicine, University of Campinas—UNICAMP, Campinas, SP Brazil; 2Department of Neurology, University of Campinas—UNICAMP, Campinas, SP, Brazil; 3 Hospital SOBRAPAR, Campinas, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):6.2

Management of hydrocephalus in craniosynostosis patients

Patients with syndromic craniosynostosis (SC) have genetic mutations that present standard clinical features such as differing degrees of exorbitism, obstructive sleep apnea, and intracranial hypertension. Intracranial hypertension is present in approximately 30% to 50% of these patients and has a multifactorial cause: mainly venous hypertension, hydrocephalus, craniocerebral disproportion, and airway obstruction [1]. A comprehensive surgical protocol that effectively addresses elevated intracranial pressure is essential, as cerebral physiology may be severely affected by elevated intracranial pressure, resulting in long-lasting neurocognitive impairment and progressive vision loss. Treating hydrocephalus in this population can be challenging; therefore, understanding the physiology and natural history is fundamental to providing the best treatment.

Several definitions and classifications of hydrocephalus are in the literature, sometimes leading to misinterpretations and confusion. The definition proposed by Rekate seems to be very objective and easy to understand: “Hydrocephalus is an active distension of the ventricular system of the brain resulting from the inadequate passage of cerebrospinal fluid from its point of production within the cerebral ventricles to its point of absorption into the systemic circulation” [2]. This definition is fundamental to differentiating between hydrocephalus (requires treatment) and ventriculomegaly (does not require treatment). The incidence of hydrocephalus in SC patients is much higher than in single-suture closure patients and the average population. Cloverleaf patients have a chance of 70% to 100% to present hydrocephalus, Pfifer patients 28% to 64%, Crouzon patients a chance of 16% to 26%, and Apert patients only 4% to 12% [3].

The main possible etiologies that promote hydrocephalus in SC patients are the obstruction of the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) because of a crowded posterior fossa, chronic tonsillar herniation leading to foramen magnum obstruction, and malabsorption of the CSF resulting from the extremely common venous hypertension in this population [3].

Ventricle peritoneal shunt (VPS) is the primary treatment of hydrocephalus in SC and is very effective in reducing intracranial hypertension. On the other hand, VPS has several and sometimes severe disadvantages in SC patients beyond the common ones to this procedure (infection and mechanical failures). The unique pathophysiology of hydrocephalus in this population increases specific shunt complications such as over drainage, pseudotumor statuses like intraventricular bleeding, epidural dead space, and microcephalus. Therefore, it becomes clear that the decision to shunt or not must be diligent and only do so when a clear hydrocephalus is present. Endoscopic third ventriculostomy has been advocated as an alternative to shunt, but no precise data about its effectiveness exists.

Our institution’s protocol [4, 5] aims to differentiate two populations: those with mild to moderate ventricle enlargement and those with severe and progressive ventricle enlargement. The first ones must have their craniosynostosis treatment (bone work) prioritized, and if, after decompression, some ventricle enlargement comes, a new decision process about VPS is made. Patients presenting with severe and progressive hydrocephalus, resulting in intracranial hypertension and Swiss cheese-like bone, are directed to shunt implantation. We advocate using programmable shunts with anti-siphon devices to avoid intracranial bleeding and the premature closure of all sutures that result in microcephaly.

We reviewed the results of 75 SC patients of our institution who followed our protocol of cranial expansion with posterior vault distraction osteogenesis (PVDO) at six months of age, reserving the shunt for those with evident intracranial hypertension and severe hydrocephalus either before or after the PVDO. Five patients needed VPS, two before PVDO and three after PVDO; all presented an Evans index superior to 0.52. The median Evans index of those who did not require VPS was 0.32 (standard deviation of 0.12). No mortality was related to PVDO or VPS, and no shunt infection or malfunction. Only one patient developed spinal cord syringomyelia and needed posterior fossa decompression.

In conclusion, the installation of a VPS must be very critical and only used if evident intracranial hypertension is detected and severe hydrocephalus is diagnosed. Whenever possible, the bone work must come before the shunt.
