6.3 Chiari I: malformation in craniosynostosis

Mark Proctor

Department of Neurosurgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):6.3

The Chiari I Malformations

Chiari 1 malformation was first discovered in the late 1800s. There are four types of Chiari malformations, which should be understood as distinct conditions rather than progressive stages of a single disorder. Chiari 1 does not become Chiari 2, 3, or 4; they are distinct conditions named by the same physician, Dr. Chiari, who named them after himself. Dr. Arnold was another doctor working at the same time, and they both worked on identifying these conditions.1

So, what exactly is a Chiari 1 malformation? It is a downward displacement of the back of the brain, specifically the posterior cerebellum or the cerebellar tonsils. Unlike Chiari 2, which is associated with spina bifida, in Chiari 1, the brainstem is usually in the correct position. To diagnose a Chiari malformation, the cerebellar tonsils must be displaced at least 5 mm downward.

Embryology and etiology of Chiari I malformation

Often, Chiari 1 malformation results from a mismatch between brain growth and skull bone growth, particularly in patients with craniosynostosis. Most Chiari 1 malformations are idiopathic, though they may also be familial.

Common Presentations of Chiari

Infants/Toddlers: Breathing and swallowing troubles are common symptoms. Older children and adults often present with headaches and neck pain, and sometimes a curvature of the spine. Sleep apnea is relatively common, and sleep studies frequently reveal issues. Other less common symptoms are related to cerebellar compression.

Chiari I and Syrinx

A syrinx is a collection of fluid within the spinal cord, often caused by a Chiari malformation that disrupts the normal flow of cerebrospinal fluid from the brain. Many surgeons view a Chiari-induced syrinx as an imperative for surgery, even in the absence of other clinical symptoms, due to the significant problems it can cause over time. 2 Scoliosis, for example, is seen in up to 85% of patients with Chiari malformations and syrinx.3

Incidence of Chiari Malformation

With the increased use of MRI, Chiari malformation is being detected more frequently. It currently affects about 3% to 4% of children.1 The incidence is higher in children because their skulls become fixed in size while their brains may atrophy as they age, potentially causing the malformation to regress.

In craniosynostosis, the risk of Chiari malformation increases. For example, if the lambdoid suture is closed, the risk is about 55%, while the sagittal suture poses almost no increased risk compared to the general population. 4 Coronal suture closure has a slightly higher risk, with about 4% of children with single-suture synostosis developing Chiari malformation. In syndromic patients with multiple suture synostosis, around 35% to 60% have Chiari malformation, depending on the sutures involved. 5

A recent case showed that Chiari malformation in a child with sagittal synostosis resolved after a cranial expansion procedure, highlighting that increasing skull space can alleviate the condition. Previously, there was limited information on Chiari malformation, but current studies indicate that it is often benign, and most children do not need to worry about it.

Natural History and Surgical Necessity of Chiari Malformation

Chiari malformation generally has a benign natural history. Many recent studies, including one from 2021, have shown this benign course.6 An early study by Dr. DiRocco in Italy found that only 14% of children with Chiari malformation required surgery, which was the highest rate reported.7 Other studies reported even lower rates: a study from Miami found that only 4% needed surgery, while one from Michigan reported an 11% surgery rate.8,9

A comprehensive meta-analysis conducted about seven years ago found that approximately 6% to 7% of patients with a diagnosed Chiari malformation needed surgery. 10 Overall, the data suggest that only about 5% to 10% of children with Chiari malformation may eventually require surgical intervention, while the majority (over 90%) do not require any treatment.

Risk of Catastrophic Injury with Chiari Malformation

The risk of catastrophic injury with Chiari malformation is very low. This conclusion comes from a study conducted at our institution, where we followed a group of athletes, including those involved in high-risk collision sports. Over 1627 athletic seasons (191 of which involved collision sports), none of the athletes sustained an injury resulting in Chiari-related complications, coma, or other injuries. This indicates a 0% risk.11

Given these findings, there is ongoing debate about whether it should even be called a “malformation,” as this term can unnecessarily scare patients. At our hospital, it is often called a “Chiari deformation,” which sounds less alarming. In the context of craniosynostosis, it is sometimes described as “cerebellar tonsil ectopia,” indicating that the tonsils are pushed down but not necessarily causing problems. This perspective aligns with the understanding that Chiari malformation is often a benign or incidental finding.

Treatment of Chiari Malformation

In children with synostosis requiring treatment for Chiari malformation, the primary approach involves skull expansion techniques. This may include traditional Chiari decompression, where surgical procedures aim to alleviate compression of the Chiari malformation with options to open or not open the dura. Another approach is craniocervical junction fusion, which stabilizes the junction between the skull and cervical spine, prevents further compression, and manages associated symptoms. Additionally, posterior distraction techniques create additional space in the posterior cranial fossa, reducing pressure on the brain and spinal cord. These interventions aim to relieve symptoms and improve quality of life, particularly in cases complicated by craniosynostosis.

Chiari Decompression

Chiari decompression typically involves a posterior fossa craniectomy with C1 laminectomy. This procedure aims to alleviate the compression of the Chiari malformation by creating more space for the brain. It often includes removing a piece of the back of the skull and sometimes part of the C1 vertebra. Whether to open the dura (the covering of the brain and spinal cord) during this procedure has been a topic of debate, but recent studies, such as those from the Park Reeve Study consortium, suggest that opening the dura does not significantly increase complication rates and may improve efficacy in symptom relief.

Other Surgical Considerations

In addition to posterior fossa decompression, other procedures may be necessary, especially in syndromic and synostosis patients who may also require spinal fusions due to complex spine issues. Only a small percentage of patients, approximately 2%, require skull-to-spine fusion, typically at the same level due to specific practice patterns and patient needs.

Posterior Calvarial Distraction

Another approach, posterior calvarial distraction, has successfully treated Chiari malformation in most cases, although it may carry a higher complication rate of around 50%.

Conclusion

Chiari malformation, while relatively common in the general population (1% to 3%), is more prevalent among children with synostosis. It is less common in conditions like Apert Syndrome. Surgery is not recommended for asymptomatic individuals due to the benign natural history of the condition. When surgery is necessary, several options exist, including different approaches to decompression and, in some cases, fusion procedures.

6.4 Management at University Hospital Necker—Enfants Malades Hospital—France

Eric Arnaud1,2

1Craniofacial Surgery Department, University Hospital Necker—Enfants Malades, Paris, France; 2Centre de Competences pour les maladies crânio-faciales, Clinique Marcel Sembat-Ramsay, Boulogne-Billancourt, France.

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):6.4

Introduction

Craniofacial syndromes like Apert and Crouzon are an uncommon group of congenital conditions that demand multipspecialty management from birth. We have established an extensive surgical approach for such patients in Necker-Enfants Malades Hospital in Paris, based on early referral with coordinated evaluation from pediatricians, radiologists, geneticists, as well as craniofacial surgeons.

Early detection and early referral for specialized centers are paramount. Direct referral from community pediatricians with virtual consultation when necessary is supported. Referral leads to a uniform assessment of the three major domains: vision, brain (intracranial pressure, neuroimaging), and respiration.

As necessary, initial procedures can involve oculoplastic operations in order to shield the cornea or decompress the orbit, as well as respiratory procedures from nasal stenting through tracheostomy.

We emphasize cranial vault expansion prior to 9 months of age when the skull is amenable to remodeling. In about 10% of our patients, monobloc surgeries are done prior to18 months, necessitating technique modification.

Surgical Techniques

Monobloc Advancement

Monobloc frontofacial advancement is employed for patients with exorbitism, raised intracranial pressure, and midface hypoplasia. We have performed monobloc procedures as early as 3 weeks of age, using techniques such as spring-assisted distraction and external traction via transfacial pins.

One such case involved a neonate with a non-operable associated cardiac malformation. Despite surgical risks, a monobloc with external traction achieved meaningful decompression. We highlight technical challenges encountered in early procedures, such as insufficient bone rigidity in distracted segments and early relapse due to poor mechanical integrity despite apparent radiological ossification.

Staged Approach

For those of moderate severity, spring-assisted cranioplasty with subsequent fronto-orbital advancement can postpone further extensive procedures. Subsequently, modified Le Fort III osteotomies with, or without, midline bipartition facilitate the treatment of residual midface hypoplasia and orbital dystopia. Internal distractors provide an esthetic as well as functional benefit, with reduced external hardware time.

Careful staging of these procedures is necessary in syndromic airway compromise patients. Monoblocs in the early stages can lead to decannulation, with dramatic quality of life improvement.

Case Highlights

Mild Apert Syndrome: Managed with spring-assisted cranioplasty during infancy, followed by fronto-orbital advancement and a delayed Le Fort III bipartition. The patient achieved age-appropriate neurodevelopmental milestones and successfully integrated into mainstream schooling.Severe Apert Syndrome with airway compromise: Early intervention with monobloc frontofacial advancement, followed by Le Fort III osteotomy with bipartition, enabled successful decannulation and restoration of normative respiratory function and craniofacial aesthetics.Neonatal case with early monobloc using transfacial pin traction: Enabled controlled osteodistraction in a clinically fragile infant; however, suboptimal bone regeneration necessitated secondary surgical intervention due to inadequate consolidation.Crouzon Syndrome with FGFR3 mutation: Represented a highly complex presentation, requiring posterior cranial vault expansion, cerebrospinal shunt revision, vertex distractor application, and monobloc advancement. Despite initial decompression success, the patient required multiple staged procedures to address persistent cranial and airway pathologies.

Results

Patients receiving early monobloc advancement (before 18 months of age) showed higher rates of tracheostomy decannulation compared to those treated later. However, monobloc alone was not sufficient in many cases, ENT procedures, Le Fort III distraction, and minor interventions were often necessary in a multi-round treatment pathway.

7 Anesthesia protocols

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):7

Moderators: Dr. Claudia Marquez Simões and Dr. Fernanda Leite

7.1 Bleeding management and transfusion strategies

Susan M. Goobie

Department of Anesthesiology, Critical Care and Pain Medicine, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):7.1

Introduction

The objective of this section is to describe the incidence of bleeding and transfusion in craniosynostosis surgery, review the benefits and risks of blood transfusion for pediatric patients, introduce patient blood management guidelines, and discuss five perioperative rules for bleeding and transfusion management in pediatric craniosynostosis surgery.

Incidence of Bleeding and Transfusion

Historically, the highest categories of pediatric surgeries requiring transfusion of allogeneic red blood cell products over the last five years include cardiac, major gastrointestinal, major orthopedic, major neurosurgery, and craniosynostosis surgery, with blood transfusion rates exceeding 50%.

Indications for Pediatric Blood Transfusion

Indications for Pediatric blood transfusions include:→Acute major blood loss→To manage critical anemia restoring oxygen delivery to vital organs→To manage critical hypovolemia and/or hypotension associated with blood loss→To correct significant hemostatic derangements→To treat critical laboratory abnormalities

→Acute major blood loss

Acute major blood loss

→To manage critical anemia restoring oxygen delivery to vital organs

To manage critical anemia restoring oxygen delivery to vital organs

→To manage critical hypovolemia and/or hypotension associated with blood loss

To manage critical hypovolemia and/or hypotension associated with blood loss

→To correct significant hemostatic derangements

To correct significant hemostatic derangements

→To treat critical laboratory abnormalities

To treat critical laboratory abnormalities

Risks of Blood Transfusion

Although hemovigilance has reduced reported transfusion-related infections over the last ten years, significant risks remain, particularly in neonates and infants and include:→Incorrect blood product transfusion→Over-transfusion→Febrile, allergic, and hypotensive reactions→Increased incidence of adverse events (2.5 times more common in children)→Immunomodulation leading to wound infection, sepsis, immunization, and graft failure, which is under recognized and under-reported

→Incorrect blood product transfusion

Incorrect blood product transfusion

→Over-transfusion

Over-transfusion

→Febrile, allergic, and hypotensive reactions

Febrile, allergic, and hypotensive reactions

→Increased incidence of adverse events (2.5 times more common in children)

Increased incidence of adverse events (2.5 times more common in children)

→Immunomodulation leading to wound infection, sepsis, immunization, and graft failure, which is under recognized and under-reported

Immunomodulation leading to wound infection, sepsis, immunization, and graft failure, which is under recognized and under-reported

Association Between Blood Transfusion and Mortality

A recent multicenter report from the NISQIP ( National Surgical Quality Improvement Program led by the American College of Surgeons) database for pediatric North American hospitals revealed that 30-day mortality rates were twice as high in pediatric patients who had blood transfusions compared to matched cohorts. Higher volumes of blood transfusion were associated with increased postoperative complications and mortality.

Complications in Craniosynostosis Surgery

In a study of over 3,000 craniosynostosis patients from the multicenter PCCG (Pediatric Craniofacial Collaborative Group), an overall complication rate of 15% was reported, with high volume blood transfusion being a significant predictor. Transfusions greater than 40 ml/kg had an odds ratio of 9.2 for increased complications. Other predictors for increased risk included low body weight, prolonged surgical time, and use of hemostatic products.

Patient Blood Management (PBM)

Patient Blood Management (PBM) is a patient-centered, evidence-based strategy to improve outcomes by managing a patient’s own blood, minimizing loss, and optimizing blood conservation strategies, focusing on three pillars: treating anemia, minimizing blood loss, and improving tolerance to anemia, reducing reliance on donor blood transfusions. It’s a multidisciplinary approach covering all care stages (pre-, intra-, post-op) to enhance safety, reduce costs, and improve recovery by preserving the patient’s blood. PBM aims to keep the patient’s own blood in their bdy patient and minimize unnecessary transfusions through a multidisciplinary approach, incorporating evidence-based medical and surgical concepts. Key strategies include:➢Perioperative Anemia Management: Screening and treating (with non-transfusion modalities) anemia 3–6 weeks before surgery, primarily addressing iron deficiency.➢Avoid Over-Transfusion: Utilizing restrictive thresholds for transfusions (e.g., a hemoglobin level of 7 g/dL or higher) and not transfusing over a Hb > 9.5 g/dL if the patient is physiologically stable.➢Implement Blood Conservation Strategies: Using antifibrinolytics such as tranexamic acid (TXA) to reduce blood loss and cell salvage.➢Employ Optimal Blood Use: Goal-directed transfusion protocols and massive hemorrhage guidelines.➢Treat the Patient, Not the Number: Transfusions based on clinical/physiological status rather than lab values alone.

➢Perioperative Anemia Management: Screening and treating (with non-transfusion modalities) anemia 3–6 weeks before surgery, primarily addressing iron deficiency.

Perioperative Anemia Management: Screening and treating (with non-transfusion modalities) anemia 3–6 weeks before surgery, primarily addressing iron deficiency.

➢Avoid Over-Transfusion: Utilizing restrictive thresholds for transfusions (e.g., a hemoglobin level of 7 g/dL or higher) and not transfusing over a Hb > 9.5 g/dL if the patient is physiologically stable.

➢Treat the Patient, Not the Number: Transfusions based on clinical/physiological status rather than lab values alone.

Perioperative Rules for Optimal Bleeding and Blood Transfusion Management

Perioperative Anemia Management

Perioperative Anemia Management

Perioperative Anemia Management

Anemia is a significant global health issue, particularly in low and middle-income countries, however it is also prevalent in high income countries.. Preoperative anemia increases surgical risks and is associated with higher complication rates. Boston Children’s Hospital recently implemented a perioperative hematopoietic optimization protocol to optimize the preoperative red blood cell mass with a goal to reduce unnecessary transfusions and potentially improve outcomes.

2.Avoid Over-Transfusion

2.Avoid Over-Transfusion

Avoid Over-Transfusion

Using restrictive thresholds for transfusions minimizes over-transfusion risks. For hemodynamically stable pediatric patients, transfusions should not be considered if the starting Hb is 8 g/dL and not exceed a hemoglobin level of 9.5 g/dL post transfusion unless in critical situations.

3.Implement Blood Conservation Strategies

3.Implement Blood Conservation Strategies

Implement Blood Conservation Strategies

Antifibrinolytics such as tranexamic acid (TXA) are considered standard of care and highly recommended routinely for neonates and children undergoing craniosynostosis surgery to reduce blood loss, decrease transfusion needs and potentially improve postoperative complications. TXA administration should be guided by pharmacokinetic recommendations ( ina range of 10–30 mg/kg loading dose and 5010 mg/kg/h intraoperatively). There may also be a role for postoperative TXA use in high risk cases however data is limited supporting TXA use in this setting. Cell salvage is another blood conservation strategy that can be employed if available. Of course surgical hemostasis and hemovigilance is necessary to prevent clinically significant blood loss.

4.Optimal Blood Use

4.Optimal Blood Use

Optimal Blood Use

Goal-directed strategies ensure that the right blood product is given at the correct dose and time to the right patients for the right reason. Massive hemorrhage guidelines, transfusion algorithms and point of care viscoelastic testing can be used to strategically guide transfusion decisions. Recombinant products such as factor concentrates (fibrinogen concentrate) can be considered as alternatives to allogeneic blood components.

5.Treat the Patient, Not the Number

5.Treat the Patient, Not the Number

Treat the Patient, Not the Number

Clinical and physiological status should guide individual patients’ transfusion needs, with research supporting restrictive transfusion strategies guided by physiological parameters rather than only on laboratory values alone. Physiological markers include dynamic hemodynamic parameters, serial lactic acid and base excess values, and somatic and regional oximetry trends.

Conclusion

Every drop of blood counts. Avoid preoperative anemia by timely optimizing preoperatively, prioritize keeping blood in the patient, minimize unnecessary bleeding and routine blood work, employ blood conservation strategies such as antifibrinolytics and cell salvage, use institution specific goal-directed transfusion protocols, and treat patients based on clinical status. These practices aim to reduce bleeding and the need for blood transfusions, ultimately improving patient outcomes. Many centers are successfully and routinely using multimodal blood conservation strategies to reduce blood product exposure for infants and children undergoing craniofacial surgery. More prospective evidence is needed to support and further define improvements in patient center outcomes employing patient blood management.

7.2 Global anesthesia training

Craig D. McClain1,2,3

1Anaesthesia, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Perioperative Anesthesia, Department of Anesthesiology, Critical Care and Pain Medicine, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 3Program in Global Surgery and Social Change, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):7.2

Introduction

The evolution of global surgery has transitioned from a humanitarian mission approach to a systems-based and public health-focused strategy. This shift underscores the pivotal role of surgical care in addressing a substantial portion of the global disease burden. Initial estimates from 2008 indicated that 11% of the global disease burden was treatable by surgical care, a figure that has likely increased due to advancements in trauma care, management of malignancies, congenital anomalies, and pregnancy complications.

Insights from the Lancet Commission on Global Surgery

Drawing from the Lancet Commission’s seminar report in 2015, there is an alarming disparity in access to safe surgical care across different regions of the world. Billions of people lack access to essential surgical and anesthesia services, with approximately 1.7 billion children affected. The report identified several critical findings, including the urgent need for increased surgical interventions, the severe financial implications of untreated surgical conditions, and the substantial return on investment from enhancing surgical and anesthesia services.

Workforce Challenges and SAO Density Analysis

Addressing the workforce challenges, the concept of SAO workers (surgeons, anesthesiologists, and obstetricians) and their uneven distribution globally is crucial. The Lancet’s analysis correlating SAO density with maternal mortality rates indicates a significant association, underscoring the minimum threshold of 20 SAO workers per 100,000 population necessary to mitigate maternal mortality effectively. Highlighting a map from the Lancet report, the global distribution of SAO workers reveals stark disparities, with regions in South Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa, and parts of Latin America facing acute shortages. Of the total of 20 SAO workers per 100,000 population, the WFSA estimates that a minimal number of anesthesiologists should be 4 per 100,000 population.1 Unfortunately, most LMICs are well below this number with particular workforce shortages in rural areas.

Initiatives and Programs Addressing Workforce Shortages

Several impactful initiatives have been implemented to strengthen anesthesia workforce capacity globally. One noteworthy example is the Human Resources for Health (HRH) program in Rwanda, a multi-disciplinary, multi-institutional effort funded by USAID that ran from 2012–2018. This initiative significantly increased the number of anesthesiologists through partnership with the Ministry of Health of Rwanda by aiding in bedside teaching as well as administrative and logistical support of preexisting residency programs. By the completion of the program, the number of anesthesiologists had increased by approximately 300%, with continued full residency classes ensuring even more anesthesiologists in the training pipeline. The proliferation of anesthesiologists in Rwanda resulting from this novel type of partnership demonstrates the model’s effectiveness in addressing local healthcare needs. However, even with this expansion of the anesthesia workforce, Rwanda still is well below the established threshold of 4:100,000.2,3 This illustrates the vast gap in available versus necessary workforce.

Task Shifting as a Strategic Solution

Task shifting is a strategic approach to mitigating workforce shortages. It involves redistributing tasks from highly trained professionals to those with shorter training and fewer qualifications in order to optimize resources and healthcare delivery. Successful examples from rural India show that trained medical officers can perform spinal anesthesia competently and comparable to consultant anesthesiologists.4 Ensuring the safety and efficacy of task-shifting practices requires structured education and skill development programs.

Future Directions and Innovative Programs

Future directions and emerging programs aim at advancing global anesthesia training. Programs like the Pediatric Anesthesia Training in Africa address the critical shortage of trained pediatric anesthesiologists in underserved regions. We are eager to continue our efforts by concentrating on collaborative initiatives, sustainable educational programs, and innovative approaches to address global surgical disparities and enhance healthcare outcomes worldwide effectively.

7.3 Anesthesiology approaches and local block in craniofacial surgery

Luciano Brandão Machado1,2

1Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies HRAC, University of São Paulo, Brazil; 2Brazilian Society of Anesthesiologists

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):7.3

Several anesthesia topics are involved in children’s or adult craniofacial surgical procedures. We highlight bleeding management, advanced airway control, and postoperative pain treatment.

Minimizing bleeding is essential for homeostasis and gives the surgeon a clean field for identifying anatomical structures during surgery. Preoperative medication, such as Midazolam 0.5 mg/Kg, is helpful as it’s common for the patient to have more than one surgery during all the surgical treatment. It’s preferable to choose total venous anesthesia, but if Sevorane is administered, the dosage should not exceed 0,5 to 1,0 MAC as cranial vessel vasodilation may increase craniofacial bleeding. Other maneuvers during surgery are remifentanil, which helps lower 10 to 20 percent of initial cardiac frequency, elevating the 30-grade back position, avoiding hypothermia, hemodilution, and high peep levels.1 Tranexamic acid should be part of intraoperative therapeutics; it has both antifibrinolytic and anti-inflammatory effects; we recommend a 30 mg/Kg IV loading dose and a 10 mg/Kg/h infusion until the end of the procedure.2,3,4 There is evidence of preoperative erythropoietin before surgery; among several protocols, we practice a 1000UI/Kg weekly dose for 4 to 6 weeks before surgery associated with supplemental Iron, folic acid, B12 vitamin, and E vitamin.5–8 Fibrinogen administration (HaemocompletamÒ) may also play a role in the future of major craniofacial surgeries.7,9

The anesthesiologist on a craniofacial surgical team must have training in complex airway management and nasofibroscopy. Several syndromes, such as Pierre Robin Sequence, Apert Syndrome, and Treacher Collins Syndrome, have anatomical challenges for direct laryngoscopy.10 In cases of Oculo Auriculo Vertebral Spectrum (OAVS) and Goldenhar Syndrome, it is known that craniocervical instability may be present in 26,9% of cases, and fibroscopy helps minimize cervical manipulation during tracheal intubation.11,12 Finally, it is essential to distinguish between a complex airway with no intubation and adequate facial mask ventilation with no intubation and difficult mask ventilation. That last is common in Robin and Apert patients; in those cases, it is recommended to intubate with fibroscopy under spontaneous ventilation.10

Pain control strategies are fundamental when treating infants. They don’t have the comprehensive feeling of adults, and excessive pain may cause postoperative agitation and bleeding. Basic analgesics, anti-inflammatory drugs, and opioids are helpful, and there are some effective craniofacial nerve blocks for pain control. An infraorbital nerve block is recommended for cleft palate and lip surgery.13 It is possible to perform the ultrasound-guided Suprazygomatic maxillary nerve block for major craniofacial procedures with satisfactory postoperative analgesia (Fig. 1).14

Some other techniques, such as Transversus Abdominal Plane Block (TAP Block) and Erector Spine Plane Block, are effective for analgesia of donation areas in cases of intercostal or iliac bone grafts.15 In Apert syndactyly surgery, an ultrasound-guided infraclavicular brachial plexus block can be performed for pain control.

In our practice, with satisfactory bleeding control, airway, and pain management, there’s usually no need to keep the patient intubated. If indicated, dexmedetomidine is useful for extubating a patient at the end of surgery in the operating room. For minor procedures such as cleft lip and palate surgery, it is adequate to implement an ambulatory protocol.16 Nowadays, with the endoscopic treatment of craniosynostosis, there is considerable abbreviation of the in-hospital period.17 To improve treatment, we recommend that the anesthesia team maximize technical support, construct a solid surgical team, and have the same surgeon and anesthesiologist working together for several surgeries, creating a solid partnership.

7.4 The “frequent flyer” surgical kid: should we be concerned about anesthesia-related neurotoxicity?

Vinícius Caldeira Quintão1,2

1Department of Pediatrics, Faculdade de Medicina, Universidade de São Paulo, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Discipline of Anesthesiology, Faculdade de Medicina, Universidade de São Paulo, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):7.4

The FDA’s pharmacovigilance communication in late 2016, which recommended a black box warning for intravenous and inhaled anesthetics, has brought the potential neurotoxicity of general anesthetics to our attention.1 This communication specifically cautioned against the risk of neurotoxicity in children under 3 years old and fetuses of pregnant women in the third trimester who underwent general anesthesia or sedation.

The FDA’s publication raised concerns within the anesthetic community, listing nearly all intravenous and inhaled anesthetics. In fact, only opioids and alpha-2 agonists were excluded. The FDA’s communication was based on numerous preclinical studies with rats or mice exposed to general anesthetics for extended periods.2–5

Following the FDA’s communication, a series of editorials were published questioning the document’s validity.1,6,7 The primary concern was a potential delay in crucial, time-sensitive procedures, leading to an increase in emergency surgeries and subsequent morbidity and mortality. But can we translate these findings to our children and newborns? Aside from physiological differences between animals and humans, the vast majority of pediatric anesthesia procedures are of short duration.

The GAS trial, the only randomized trial in this field, holds significant weight in our understanding of the neurotoxicity of general anesthetics. This international, multicenter study randomized newborns up to 60 weeks post-conception to general anesthesia or neuroaxial block without sedation. In the interim analysis at 2 years of follow-up, they assessed children’s neurodevelopment using the Bayley scale and found no difference between the groups.8 At 5 years of follow-up, they evaluated the primary outcome, measured with the Wechsler Intelligence Scale, and again found no difference between the groups.9 Despite the limitations of this study, particularly the significant loss to follow-up, it was crucial and is considered the most methodologically robust.

Other important studies were observational, such as PANDA, MASK, and AVON.10–12 These studies also found no differences in neurodevelopmental assessments or intelligence quotients between children exposed or not exposed to general anesthesia or sedation. These findings are significant as they provide further reassurance about the safety of general anesthetics in pediatric anesthesia practice.

Recently, colleagues at the Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia conducted a retrospective evaluation of children undergoing cardiac surgery, cyanotic or not, undergoing correction with extracorporeal circulation.13 This study also did not demonstrate changes in neurodevelopment measured by the Bayley scale. They observed a poorer motor response, which could be associated with ketamine use, but due to the study’s nature, it could not be conclusive. This study shed light on neurotoxicity in children undergoing cardiac surgery, a high-risk population that had not been evaluated until then. This was another study demonstrating the safety of general anesthetic drugs regarding neurotoxicity, but it also highlighted the need for further research to understand the potential effects of specific anesthetic drugs.

Despite the numerous preclinical studies showing mechanisms and neurocognitive changes in animals exposed to anesthetic drugs, all clinical studies have been negative. This consistent trend of reassuring results from clinical studies gives clinians a sense of confidence, especially when we are questioned by parents or guardians about potential neurocognitive alterations from general anesthetics.

8 Dentistry, orthodontics and orthognathic refinements

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):8

Moderator: Dr. Renato Yaedu and Dr. Tiago Turri

8.1 Hospital for rehabilitation of craniofacial anomalies—Brazil

Adriano Porto Peixoto1,2

1Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Department of Orthodontics, Hospital for the Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):8.1

Introduction: Three-Dimensional Evaluation of Craniofacial Anomalies

Three-dimensional evaluation of patients with craniofacial anomalies is essential for a comprehensive understanding of their condition. We know that, starting at the age of 6, these patients begin regular dental follow-ups, with orthodontics monitoring them throughout their treatment. To ensure an accurate and effective approach, incorporating three-dimensional assessment of the craniofacial structures into the diagnostic and treatment planning process is critical.

Three-dimensional evaluation involves analyzing the craniofacial structures in the transverse, sagittal, and vertical planes, providing a complete view of the present deformities. This approach helps identify alterations in maxillomandibular relationships as well as potential deficiencies in the transverse, sagittal, and vertical growth of the bone structures. Furthermore, three-dimensional assessment facilitates more detailed and personalized planning for interventions such as maxillary expansion or frontofacial advancements.

By adopting this methodology, we can not only diagnose more accurately but also develop more effective treatment strategies, aligning each patient’s specific needs with the best orthodontic and surgical practices.

Transverse Plane Evaluation

Transverse Plane Evaluation

Transverse Plane Evaluation

In the transverse plane, we observe altered maxillomandibular relationships, particularly when examining these patients. There is often an impression of atresia, which we must question: Is it real or relative?

Before proceeding with advancement, it is crucial to evaluate if maxillary expansion is necessary for all cases. A recent study evaluating patients with craniofacial syndromes (Apert and Crouzon) in a three- dimensional context revealed1:Patients with Apert and Crouzon Syndrome exhibit significantly reduced transverse dimensions of the maxilla, including perimeter and length of the upper arch, compared to controls.The lower arch is less affected, with no significant transverse problems observed in these Syndromes.Mild dentoalveolar expansion in the maxilla is sufficient for transverse adaptation of dental arches before frontofacial advancement.Apert Syndrome patients typically present with anterior crossbite, while Crouzon Syndrome patients have an overbite.

Approximately 15–20% of patients may require rapid maxillary expansion to correct arch discrepancies, particularly in cases requiring sagittal plane adjustment.

3.Vertical Plane Evaluation

3.Vertical Plane Evaluation

Vertical Plane Evaluation

In the vertical plane, many patients present with an excessive vertical gap before initiating therapy. This presents a challenge as these patients will likely undergo frontofacial advancement (either monobloc or Le Fort III). The goal is to manage this issue at an earlier age to reduce the complexity of the surgery and the future instability of the results. Patients with craniosynostosis can show exacerbated growth in the midface and a clockwise rotation of the mandible, leading to anterior open bite, a hallmark malocclusion at the start of treatment 4.

Clinical Approach and Case Studies

Transverse Plane Treatment: When maxillary expansion is indicated, and early ossification of the circummaxillary structures is present, alternatives include:Classical Rapid Maxillary Expansion (RME) with Surgical Assistance.Mini-implant anchored expansion devices, which can extend the window for expansion beyond the typical age for non-syndromic patients.

Case Study: A 12-year-old patient with Crouzon Syndrome, whose circummaxillary sutures were ossified, presented with significant atresia and sagittal discrepancy. A skeletal anchorage device with 2,000 implants was used for 14 days, achieving a 2 mm/day activation rate. After 14 days, we observed separation of the mid-palatal suture. Although there was no structural opening, dental inclination was noted, which is a highly recurrent issue in this context. We also suspect the lack of corticalization in the implants may have contributed to the results (Figs. 1 and 2).

2.Sagittal Plane Treatment: For patients undergoing Le Fort III advancement, we use intraoral anchorage whenever possible to ensure vector control and minimize maxillary anti-clockwise rotation.

2.Sagittal Plane Treatment: For patients undergoing Le Fort III advancement, we use intraoral anchorage whenever possible to ensure vector control and minimize maxillary anti-clockwise rotation.

Sagittal Plane Treatment: For patients undergoing Le Fort III advancement, we use intraoral anchorage whenever possible to ensure vector control and minimize maxillary anti-clockwise rotation.

Case Study: A patient with a typical Crouzon Syndrome occlusion was treated with Le Fort III advancement, showing significant post-surgical improvement. However, the patient developed increased anterior open bite, which likely requires further orthodontic preparation for Le Fort I correction.

3.Vertical Plane Treatment: In younger patients with anticipated excessive vertical growth, we use a simpler approach, beginning with leveling archwires and light intermaxillary elastics to minimize the risk of anti-clockwise rotation during treatment. This preventive measure aims to ease the future frontofacial surgery.

3.Vertical Plane Treatment: In younger patients with anticipated excessive vertical growth, we use a simpler approach, beginning with leveling archwires and light intermaxillary elastics to minimize the risk of anti-clockwise rotation during treatment. Case Study: A young patient with a potential open bite responded well to this approach, with weekly adjustments to the elastic vectors. At the end of treatment, the maxilla’s response was promising, demonstrating how early management can simplify treatment and improve outcomes for both orthodontists and surgeons.

Conclusion

Our treatment protocol is not rigid but evolves with experience. The main goals are to avoid over-treatment and to optimize outcomes for patients with craniofacial syndromes.Transverse Plane: Maxillary expansion is indicated when necessary, with consideration for non- conventional methods if early ossification is present.Sagittal Plane: Le Fort III advancements are the standard, with intraoral anchorage to control rotation.Vertical Plane: Managing vertical discrepancies early with simple orthodontic mechanics ensures less complicated future surgeries.

Transverse Plane: Maxillary expansion is indicated when necessary, with consideration for non- conventional methods if early ossification is present.

We continually refine these protocols based on patient outcomes, and the success of our cases reflects the cohesive efforts of our multidisciplinary team.

8.2 Orthognathic surgery for patients with syndromic craniosynostosis at Boston Children’s Hospital

Bonnie L. Padwa1,2

1Oral and Maxillofacial Surgery, Harvard School of Dental Medicine, Boston, MA, USA; 2Leonard B. Kaban, DMD, MD Chair in Oral and Maxillofacial Surgery and Oral Surgeon-in-Chief Department of Plastic and Oral Surgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):8.2

Orthognathic Surgery for Patients with Syndromic Craniosynostosis

Orthognathic surgery plays a critical role in the long-term management of patients with syndromic craniosynostosis, particularly for correcting residual jaw misalignment following midface advancement. At Boston Children’s Hospital, this procedure represents the final reconstructive stage—addressing malocclusion and optimizing both function and aesthetics.

Surgical Process and Planning: Orthognathic surgery is performed after mid-facial advancement, which aims to reposition the midface based on the infraorbital rim’s alignment with the corneal apex. Misjudging this alignment can lead to aesthetic imbalances. Therefore, any remaining malocclusion is addressed with orthognathic procedures once skeletal maturity is reached, as the upper jaw ceases growth earlier than the lower jaw.

In this patient population, “surgery-first” approaches are generally avoided due to significant malocclusion and dental crowding. Proper orthodontic preparation is essential. The advent of virtual surgical planning has enhanced surgical precision and outcomes.

Multidisciplinary Coordination: Successful outcomes depend on collaborative planning between surgeons, orthodontists, and other specialists. Treatment goals must focus on achieving both functional occlusion and optimal facial aesthetics. Residual sleep-disordered breathing, including obstructive sleep apnea, must also be assessed preoperatively, with sleep studies when necessary.

Three-Dimensional Analysis: Diagnosis involves evaluating skeletal deformities in vertical, sagittal, and transverse planes. The chin, mandible, and maxilla are assessed for asymmetries and dimensional deficiencies. Previous research at our institution found patients with syndromic craniosynostosis often have smaller mandibular lengths and obtuse gonial angles. Midface advancement shifts the mandible posteriorly and inferiorly, reducing the need for mandibular setback and helping maintain airway patency.

Surgical Techniques and Pearls: Common procedures include Le Fort I osteotomy, bilateral sagittal split osteotomy, and genioplasty. Technical challenges may arise from previous surgeries and scarring, particularly at the pterygomaxillary junction. Ensuring maxillary mobilization without excessive force is critical. Techniques like using Smith spreaders between the pterygoid plate and posterior maxilla can facilitate safe advancement while preserving critical vasculature.

Conclusion

Orthognathic refinement represents the final stage in bony reconstruction for patients with syndromic craniosynostosis. The overarching goals are to improve occlusion, achieve harmonious facial profiles, and address potential breathing concerns. Multidisciplinary planning and modern surgical tools are key to delivering successful, patient-centered outcomes.

9 Apert’s hand

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):9

Moderator: Dr. Nivaldo Alonso

9.1 Apert hand management

Hugo Alberto Nakamoto

Department of Surgery, Faculty of Medicine, São Paulo University, São Paulo, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):9.1

Apert hands involves a spectrum of disturbances across the soft tissue and bony structures such as syndactyly, symphalangism, clinodactyly, carpal coalitions and metacarpal synostosis.1

This massive and bilateral involvement often entails numerous surgical procedures and poses significant challenges to the pediatric hand surgeon.2

Craniofacial surgeries are always prioritized over hand surgery, and a global planning concerning timing, combined surgeries and type of hand deformity (Upton’s classification) must be developed with the multidisciplinary team. When there is no indication for early craniofacial intervention, the hand surgeries must start as soon as four months old.3

Upton’s classification defines three types of Apert hands according to increasing severity. Type I involves fusion of the three central digits and a shallow first web space with a largely free thumb in a spade conformation, Type II involves a concave palm due to fusion of the three central digits and thumb in a mitten conformation and Type III involves tight fusion of all five digits in a rosebud conformation.4

Numerous treatment algorithms have been proposed, but all of them consist of small clinical series with poor evidence.3,5–7 Only recently the first articles containing long-term results and outcomes were published.8,9

The goals of the surgical treatment are to obtain a five-digit hand whenever possible, operating both hands at the same time and dividing the procedures in two or three times depending on the complexity of the deformities. Completing surgical management in the least number of sessions is preferable as this reduces total anesthetic time and physiological disturbance. Also the psychosocial impact surgery may have on patients and family members.2 The interval between procedures are four to six months, depending on scar management and the patient’s clinical conditions. As with most congenital hand differences, the thumb should take priority, and the 4th web space (as the fifth finger is almost always the less affected finger) should be the subsequent focus. The decision to proceed with ray amputation will be dictated by anatomical limitations.

As advocated by Fearon, we prefer using straight-line incisions rather than zigzag flaps along the fingers, and triangular flaps rather than long dorsal rectangular flaps at the commissure for the correction of syndactyly.7 Cordray et al. showed that rectangular commissural flaps had 1,9 times greater complication risk than interdigiting triangular flaps, including 11,2 times greater risk of web creep.4 Zigzag volar finger flaps showed 1,8 times greater complication risk than straight-line incisions.

Conclusion

Whenever possible, achieving a five-digit hand is the main objective. Early surgery, ideally at 4 months old, is recommended. Bilateral surgery is often performed, with craniofacial procedures taking priority when necessary.

9.2 Apert’s hand: beyond syndactyly release

Brian I. Labow1,2

1Surgery, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Department of Plastic and Oral Surgery, Boston Children's Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):9.2

Importance of Hand Surgery in Pediatric Patients

The critical role of hand surgery, particularly in young patients, is stressed, highlighting its significance alongside craniofacial surgery. The variability in anatomical structures and individual patient factors necessitates tailored approaches rather than standardized protocols.

A crucial perspective shift is highlighted: while adults view hands as tools, for children, hands are essential for early interactions and development. This nuanced perspective guides hand surgeons in their approach to treating complex conditions.

Surgical Techniques and Evolving Protocols Triangular Flap Approach: This method avoids a zigzag pattern which is used to avoid flexion contractures in non-Apert hands. The use of bidirectional flaps also provides some flexibility when distal vascular bifurcations are encountered and the preferred web depth is shallower than anticipated.Nail Matrix Excision: Precision in excising excess nail matrix to achieve appropriately sized fingertips was highlighted, which is crucial for both function and appearance.Retrograde Dissection: Ensuring meticulous preservation of neurovascular anatomy during surgery, adapting to individual variations to optimize outcomes.

Complex Cases and Multistage Procedures

Challenges and Innovative Solutions Abduction Issues: Proximal 4th-5th metacarpal synostosis ostectomies are used to address small finger abduction and correct hand alignment, improving everyday tasks like gripping and dexterity.Phalangeal Deficiencies: Techniques involve staged procedures to preserve and enhance finger segments, avoiding unnecessary amputations through meticulous bone grafting and growth management.

Patient Outcomes and Quality of Life

Surgical interventions positively influenced patients’ daily lives and social interactions, demonstrating the enduring benefits of pediatric hand surgery. Despite functional limitations in pinch strength and task speed, patients reported high satisfaction with their hand function and appearance.

Conclusion

In an evolving landscape of pediatric hand surgery, we must emphasize the importance of personalized treatment approaches and the ongoing advancements in surgical techniques. These innovations and tailored strategies can significantly enhance young patients’ functional and aesthetic outcomes.

10 Multidisciplinary approach in the treatment of patients with syndromic craniosynostosis

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10

Moderators: Dr. Sergio Cavalheiro and Dr. Cynthia Martins Rocha

10.1 Airway in syndromic craniosynostosis—Crouzon syndrome

Cristiano Tonello1,2

1Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10.1

Airway Concerns in Craniofacial Syndromes: Addressing the Challenges in Pediatric Care

Airway management remains a significant concern in children with craniofacial syndromes, as these patients often face persistent challenges due to their anatomical and functional airway abnormalities. We frequently encounter cases where the primary airway intervention required is a tracheostomy, a reality for many healthcare services, particularly in Brazil. These patients are often transferred from remote regions to the Southeast of Brazil, with tracheostomy being a common requirement for safe transportation.

Over the years, however, there has been a marked shift in how we approach these cases. Research has demonstrated a steady decline in the use of tracheostomies for craniofacial patients, from 27% of cases in the 1970s to approximately 8% in more recent years.1 This improvement can be attributed to advances in facial surgery, particularly facial advancements and protocols involving significant mobilization of the facial skeleton. These surgical advancements have not only improved facial harmony but also provided better protection for the orbit and airway.

Airway Obstruction and Craniofacial Syndromes

Children with craniofacial syndromes, particularly those with craniosynostosis, are highly prone to obstructive sleep apnea, which is a direct indicator of airway compromise.2 Syndromes like Apert exhibit more severe respiratory conditions compared to others like Crouzon, but the exact severity can vary significantly depending on the individual case. Despite this, it is often easier for anesthesiologists and pediatricians to suggest tracheostomy early in the treatment process due to the immediate airway concerns. However, recent trends in management have shown that with proper surgical intervention, tracheostomies can be avoided in many cases.

Dealing with Severe Airway Obstruction: A Case Study Approach

In some cases, such as patients with severe micrognathia (not related to craniosynostosis), we have successfully avoided tracheostomies by opting for mandibular advancement, resulting in improved respiratory function and weight gain. This principle can similarly be applied to patients with craniosynostosis syndromes. While these patients present with significant retrognathia, the anatomical differences between each syndrome (Apert, Crouzon, Pfeiffer) should not be overlooked. Though they share common symptoms, their underlying anatomical structures, particularly the midface, are distinct.

Though the respiratory issues in these patients may appear similar, their anatomical structures, particularly in the jaws and midfacial region, differ considerably. This distinction is crucial as it affects the treatment approach. The work that we have conducted with craniofacial syndromic patients demonstrates that while Apert, Crouzon, and Pfeiffer syndromes share certain characteristics, they also present significant anatomical differences.3,4

Surgical Advances in Airway Management

Through collaborations with institutions like Yale University, we have been able to study the airway structures of craniosynostosis patients before surgery.5 Interestingly, we observed that the sites of airway obstruction differ with age and syndrome. For example, Crouzon syndrome patients typically exhibit more severe nasal airway obstruction up to the age of 2, whereas untreated patients with other syndromes, such as Apert or Pfeiffer, experience significant pharyngeal airway restriction as they grow. This difference suggests that our surgical approach should evolve based on the age and specific syndrome of the patient.

When we analyzed the airway volumes in different craniofacial syndromes, we found that patients with Apert syndrome had significant narrowing of the nasal passages, with a marked stenosis of the coanal region. In comparison, patients with Crouzon syndrome showed less severe midface malformation, but still presented with significant airway compromise, especially in the nasal region.

Enhancing Surgical Techniques: The Role of Facial Advancements

Surgical advancements, such as the use of midfacial advancements, have allowed us to significantly improve the airway space and nasal volume in these patients. For instance, in cases where mandibular advancement alone was insufficient, we employed bipartition techniques to elongate the midface and enhance the posterior airway space, resulting in a more functional airway.

Case Example: Mandibular Advancement for Airway Improvement

One case study involved a patient with Pfeiffer syndrome, where mandibular advancement alone provided noticeable improvement in the patient’s airway. The procedure successfully relieved the obstruction, eliminating the need for a tracheostomy. Such advancements have demonstrated the potential for avoiding more invasive interventions and significantly improving respiratory function.

We have demonstrated in this study that the advancements are sufficient to achieve a value close to the control group in terms of anteroposterior distance.6 However, what we cannot change is the extremely flattened central facial region, particularly in the nasal cavity area, which compromises both the nasal cavity and, consequently, respiratory function.

Some authors and surgeons have proposed modifications, including bipartition of the midface, particularly for those with hyperterolism and severe obstruction.7 This technique may enable us to elongate the midface or gain additional distance in this area, thereby improving the posterior airway space. These observations offer potential modifications that could be implemented in the treatment of these patients.

Conclusion: Rethinking Tracheostomies in Craniofacial Surgery

In conclusion, tracheostomy should no longer be seen as the default solution for airway management in craniofacial patients. While it remains an option in certain cases, we now have more sophisticated surgical techniques that allow for airway improvement without the need for a permanent tracheostomy. Midfacial advancements, mandibular repositioning, and other surgical refinements should be considered on a case-by-case basis. The goal is always to provide individualized care that minimizes invasiveness while addressing the root cause of the airway obstruction.

10.2 Airway in syndromic craniosynostosis—Apert syndrome

Ricardo santos de Oliveira1,2,3,4, Matheus Ballestero1,5

1Division of Neurosurgery Ribeirão Preto Medical School; 2Department of Neurosurgery, Hospital of Ribeirão Preto Medical School, University of São Paulo, Ribeirão Preto, SP, Brazil; 3 Brazilian Pediatric Neurosurgery Society (2019–2021); 4 Archives of Pediatric Neurosurgery; 5Department of Medicine, Federal University of São Carlos, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10.2

The Hospital of Ribeirão Preto Medical School, University of São Paulo, is a center dedicated to the complex and delicate nature of faciocraniosynostosis surgeries. The primary aim of these surgeries is to prevent cerebral damage and correct facial retrusion, which involves exorbitism and upper airway impairment. Notably, airway compromise in Apert syndrome patients is primarily due to pharyngeal rather than nasal obstructions.

From 2000 to 2023, our team managed a series of 402 surgeries on 368 patients, displaying the significant breadth of our experience. The conditions we often address include multiple synostosis, raised intracranial pressure, maxillary retrusion, obstructive sleep apnea, and exorbitism.

The primary surgical intervention we utilize is the fronto-facial monobloc advancement, which involves systematic disjunctions and divisions of the midline structures to allow the operative mobilization of the fronto-facial assembly. This technique is critical for addressing these patients multilevel nature of airway obstruction.

Our approach is evidenced through a detailed review of 36 syndromic patients who underwent craniofacial distraction surgery using both internal and external devices from 2003 to 2023. Key procedural steps include a latency period before activation of the distractor, followed by an activation period and a consolidation phase.

We have documented several case studies to highlight the practical applications and outcomes of our surgical techniques: Case 1—Diagnosed with Crouzon syndrome, underwent a classic fronto-orbital advancement in 2000, followed by a fronto-facial (monobloc) advancement in 2003; Case 2—Born with Apert Syndrome, Samilly received a fronto-facial advancement at 7 months old, showcasing significant improvements post-operatively; Case 3—Presented with laryngomalacia and spine malformation, requiring emergency interventions including tracheostomy and fronto-orbital advancement to address orbital luxation.

In conclusion, distraction osteogenesis has proven effective in early correction of respiratory impairments and exorbitism in patients with faciocraniosynostosis. However, a complete preoperative assessment and a multidisciplinary team approach are imperative for achieving successful outcomes. Our experience underscores the necessity of perseverance through the challenges and complexities encountered during these surgical interventions.

10.3 Ophthalmologic management

Isabella Parizotto1,2

1Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Bauru Clinical Hospital, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 3Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, BrazilDepartment of Ophtamology, Hospital das Clínicas de Bauru, Bauru, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10.3

Crouzon Syndrome

Symptoms of the disease include the premature fusion of the coronal and sagittal sutures, which develop within the first year of life. Once the sutures are closed, further development is stunted. Midfacial hypoplasia, shallow orbit, and maxillary hypoplasia are all common side effects of premature skull base fusion brought on by multiple sutural synostoses. Upper airway obstruction can also occur in these cases. 1

In Crouzon Syndrome, the orbit exhibits the following characteristics: regression of the lateral and medial orbital walls, reduced length of the orbital floor, augmented globe prominence, shortened distance between the anterior pole of the cornea and the facial plane, and increased distance to the lateral rim of the orbit. 2

The characteristics of early suture closure that leads to orbital changes in these patients proptosis and exorbitism, hypertelorism. Consequently evolves with corneal exposure, keratiris and conjunctivitis, and optic nerve complications like edema or atrophy. In order to treat corneal keratitis due to exposure, ointments and lubricating eye drops are initially recommended until a procedure is performed.Cases where exorbitism is important, with lagophthalmos and ocular lubrication deficiency, due to the risk of permanent damage and visual loss, temporal tarsorrhaphy of the eyelids is indicated.

Eyeball dislocation is a serious complication of the shallow orbit in patients with Crouzon syndrome. This complication, in addition to remaining calm, topical anesthesia and replacement of the eye into the orbit are important. In the vast majority of cases it occurs during excessive exertion and crying and regression is spontaneous.

The optic nerve is more frequently affected in Crouzon syndrome than in Apert. The most common changes are due to increased intracranial pressure, which leads to edema of the optic nerve papilla, which, as it progresses, generates atrophy and visual loss.

Intracranial pressure has more recently been assessed using the non-invasive Brain care method, which combined with fundus examination, documented by retinography and ultrasound of the optic nerve sheath are decisive for the indication of craniofacial surgery.4

Excessive persistence of myelin fibers is also a finding in the fundus of these patients and is related to FGFR2, generating abnormal regulation of myelination.

Apert Syndrome

Apert’s syndrome is a severe autosomal dominant disorder characterized by craniosynostosis (Coronal, sagittal and lambdoid sutures and Sphenoparietal Shenofrontal), midface hypoplasia, and syndactyly of the hands and feet.It is one of the most common craniofacial synostosis syndromes, yet it is a rare condition with an estimated prevalence of 12.5 to 15.5 cases per million live births. Not rarely associated with cleft palate. 3

The orbits of patients present important peculiarities: Hypertelorism and Proptosis; Roof and floor shorted; Position of the Lateral orbital wall close to normal – protusion (greater wing of the sphenoid); Mean height increased.

Hypertelorism is always presente. Inner and outer canthal distances were measured directly from patients with Apert syndrome, with most values above the 75th or 97th centiles. The eyebrows sometimes have a break in continuity. The explanation probably involves the underlying bony defect.The orbital plate of the frontal bone is extremely short, resulting from early fusion of the sphenoparietal and sphenofrontal sutures. This leads to marked retrusion and elevation of the supraorbital margin, most pronounced laterally. The interruption of the eyebrow corresponds to this defect. 2

The eyelids commonly present with telecanthus, down-slanting palpebral fissures, lagophthalmos, and, rarely, palpebral ptosis. Surgery is indicated when ptosis covers the visual axis, leading to visual deficits and a biased head position. In these cases, poor upper eyelid elevator muscle function is typical, and using the frontalis muscle suspension technique, fascia lata or silicone thread, is recommended.

In cases of lagophthalmos, ointment and lubricating eye drops are always recommended. If corneal exposure to keratitis and the risk of vision loss occurs, tarsorrhaphy is urgently indicated to prevent irreversible vision loss and potential eye perforation.

Amblyopia and strabismus are a significant concern in these patients. The most common is exotropia, with a “V” pattern associated with hyperopia and astigmatism. For these cases, the indication is refraction and the use of corrective lenses if indicated, use of alternating occlusion to treat amblyopia, and, according to the schedule of complex craniofacial surgery, surgical correction of strabismus.3 The optic nerve and retina in Crouzon Syndrome can exhibit tortuous retinal vessels and uncommon atrophy of the optic nerve. These conditions are often due to the later closure of the sagittal suture and anterior fontanelle.2

Follow our protocol for Apert and Crouzon syndrome ophthalmological care and management.

10.4 Multidisciplinary approach in syndromic craniosynostosis: psycho-social and cognitive considerations

Mariani da Costa Ribas1,2,3

1Bauru Medical School, University of São Paulo, Bauru, São Paulo, Brazil; 2Hospital for Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies (HRAC/USP), University of São Paulo, Bauru, SP, Brazil; 3Smile Train Brazil partner—advisor of Psychosocial Area

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10.4

Emotional Issues

Psychology works with Syndromic Craniosynostosis helping with emotional issues and cognitive assessment. The rehabilitation of a Syndromic Craniosynostosis comprises numerous surgical procedures from an early age. The psychology professional works in this rehabilitation process, having the role of helping families understand and accept the diagnosis and the rehabilitation process.1,2

During the consultation, before a surgical procedure, the psychologist checks the knowledge of family members and/or patients about the procedure and the care that involves it, identifies expectations regarding the surgical results and evaluates emotional issues present in the moment, such as high level of anxiety and presence of depressive symptoms. Techniques such as psychoeducation and desensitization are commonly used. In children, playful resources are also used, which aim to facilitate their understanding of the moment experienced, as well as help to deal with fears and fantasies related to the surgical procedure. 3,4

Cognitive Assessment

Craniosynostosis is characterized by premature fusion of one or more cranial sutures that prevent skull growth from accompanying increased brain volume. With it exists esthetical impairment and the appearance of neurological, respiratory, visual, and auditory disorders, and increased intracranial pressure. This latter inhibits blood flow, which brings greater risk to cognitive and neuropsychomotor development. Craniosynostosis may be associated with syndromes and cause great social and economic impact on individuals, caregivers, and the health system.6–8 For better esthetic and functional results to be achieved, surgical treatment should be early, and imaging tests and intracranial pressure monitoring are essential to ensure intervention if there is a new fusion of sutures.

In this context, it is recommended to evaluate the cognitive development of subjects with a craniosynostosis diagnosis because craniosynostosis can be associated with cognitive changes. The assessment contributes to establishing a baseline and monitoring cognitive development before and after a surgical procedure, like decompression, Le For III, and others.

A study in development (IRB 6.682.904) in the Hospital of Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies—Sao Paulo University aims to characterize the cognitive development of subjects with craniosynostosis diagnosis. Preliminary data indicate that between 2018 and 2022 44 patients diagnosed with craniosynostosis were evaluated. Ages ranged from 06 months to 40 years, and 24 patients were female. The neuropsychological tests used varied according to the age at assessment and specificity on each case (visual acuity and the presence of hearing loss and syndactyly), being: Gesell and Amatruda Behavioral Development Scale, Operationalized Portage Inventory, SON-R 2 ½—7 [a] Non-verbal Intelligence Test, Raven’s Colorful Progressive Matrices (CPM RAVEN) and Wechsler Abbreviated Scale of Intelligence (WASI). The Gesell and Amatruda Behavioral Development Scale (1 to 36 months) and Operationalized.

Portage Inventory (1 month to 6 years) were used to assess neuropsychomotor development (NPMD). The SON-R 2 ½—7 [a] Non-verbal Intelligence Test and CPM RAVEN were allocated into fluid intelligence assessment, while WASI was used to assess fluid and crystalized intelligences.

Of 44 participants, 34% (n = 15) present Crouzon Syndrome as a diagnosis; 27.3% (n = 12) Apert Syndrome; 2.3% (n = 1) Pfeiffer Syndrome; and 36.4% (n = 16) Non-syndromic Craniosynostosis, of these, 12.5% (n = 2) are brachycephaly; 12.5% (n = 2) scaphocephaly; 12.5% (n = 2) trigonocephaly; 25% (n = 4) plagiocephaly; and 37.5% (n = 6) are non-defined. In relation to results of assessment, 52.3% (n = 23) of participants presented performance below average, of these, 82.6% (n = 19) are diagnosed with syndromic craniosynostosis. Average neurocognitive development was observed in 43.2% (n = 19), being 36.8% (n = 07) with syndromic craniosynostosis. No participant presented performance above average. The assessment was not considered in 4.5% (n = 2) of participants, because they presented inconsistent results.

In conclusion, the neuropsychological assessment is a predictor to indicate craniosynostosis surgical correction; it is observed that the unsatisfactorily results obtained through the instruments, greater the damage to development. These results suggest that patients with syndromes have lower performance. It is remarkable that the application of instruments is not standardized, due to the specificity of each case, so, the importance of a flexible protocol. One of the limitations of the study is the absence of instruments that specifically assess other cognitive functions, such as attention and memory.

10.5 Genetic considerations in cognitive development

Luiza do Amaral Virmond

Craniomaxillofacial surgery team, Hospital for the Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies (HRAC/Centrinho), University of São Paulo (USP), São Paulo, SP, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10.5

Introduction

Craniosynostosis (CS) refers to a group of disorders involving the early fusion of cranial sutures, potentially affecting skull morphology and brain development. It is categorized into syndromic craniosynostosis (SCS), where it occurs alongside other anomalies, and non-syndromic craniosynostosis (NSCS), which presents in isolation1. While most genetic research has focused on syndromic cases, emerging evidence suggests that genetic factors may also influence outcomes in non-syndromic presentations, particularly with respect to cognitive development.2

Genetic Aspects of Craniosynostosis

Mutations in the fibroblast growth factor receptor (FGFR) gene family are well-documented in SCS. These genes are essential in craniofacial development and also contribute to neural maturation and brain structure, indicating their dual role in skull formation and cognitive function.1

For NSCS, a multifactorial etiology is proposed. Environmental factors such as maternal diabetes, smoking, thyroid disorders, and prenatal drug exposure have been implicated.3 Concurrently, the identification of rare variants in genes like SMAD6 and variants in genes previously implicated in SCS in isolated cases, has highlighted a possible genetic origin for many sporadic forms. Variability in the expression and penetrance of these variants, some of which are associated with cognitive impairment, exposes the genetic complexity of CS.2,4

Craniosynostosis and Neurodevelopment

Cognitive development in CS results from a complex interplay of genetic, structural, and environmental influences. Two major hypotheses explain the association between CS and neurocognitive deficits 5:Early suture fusion may mechanically restrict brain growth, impacting cognitive development.Is secondary to primary brain malformations, with bone synostosis simply representing an additional aspect of the condition.

Early suture fusion may mechanically restrict brain growth, impacting cognitive development.

Early suture fusion may mechanically restrict brain growth, impacting cognitive development.

Is secondary to primary brain malformations, with bone synostosis simply representing an additional aspect of the condition.

Is secondary to primary brain malformations, with bone synostosis simply representing an additional aspect of the condition.

Syndromic forms are more frequently associated with moderate-to-severe cognitive impairment. This is partly attributed to the involvement of FGFR genes in cortical development and myelination processes. White matter integrity, vital for inter-neuronal connectivity and cognitive functioning, appears especially vulnerable to these genetic disruptions.5

Phenotypic Variation and Clinical Significance

Not all individuals with the same disease-causing variant experience the same cognitive outcomes. While some may present with severe developmental delays, others show no measurable impairment.6 This phenotypic variability is influenced by environmental factors and possibly by genetic modifiers.6

Additionally, chromosomal abnormalities involving contiguous genes can further elevate the risk of cognitive impairment by disrupting genes essential for neurodevelopment.7 These findings emphasize the importance of individualized clinical assessment and genetic evaluation, extending beyond simple syndromic classification.7b

Non-Syndromic Craniosynostosis and Cognition

Although traditionally considered less impactful, NSCS may also carry a risk for cognitive difficulties. Even in the absence of elevated intracranial pressure, studies have shown that children with NSCS may perform below average on cognitive assessments compared to the general population.2 The identification of SMAD6 mutations in some of these individuals supports the hypothesis that NSCS can have a genetic basis and warrants consideration for genetic testing in all craniosynostosis cases, syndromic or not.2

Conclusion

Both genetic and environmental factors influence cognitive development in individuals with craniosynostosis. Evidence of neurocognitive impairment in both syndromic and non-syndromic cases reinforces the need for comprehensive genetic evaluation in all individuals with craniosynostosis. Genetic testing can provide crucial insights for diagnosis, prognosis, early intervention, and long-term care planning. As such, genetic assessment should be integrated into the standard multidisciplinary management of craniosynostosis to ensure personalized and effective care.
