10.6 Technology and innovation: extended-reality in syndromic craniosynostosis

Mauricio Yoshida1,2

1Department of Plastic Surgery Sabará Children’s Hospital, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 2Faculty of Medicine, Centro Universitário ABC, Santo André, São Paulo, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):10.6

Mixed reality—Hololens in syndromic craniosynostosis planning

Craniosynostosis surgeries are technically demanding due to complex and altered craniofacial anatomy, the need for simultaneous neurosurgical and craniofacial approaches, and reliance on accurate three-dimensional spatial understanding. Despite advances in computer-assisted surgery, most preoperative planning still relies on two-dimensional imaging, requiring surgeons to mentally reconstruct three-dimensional anatomy, which increases cognitive load, operative time, and the risk of error.

Extended-reality technologies have emerged as promising tools to address these limitations. In particular, mixed-reality allows virtual anatomical models to coexist with the real environment, enabling interactive, spatially accurate visualization of patient-specific anatomy. Evidence suggests that holographic visualization improves depth perception, reduces mental workload, and enhances anatomical understanding compared with conventional two-dimensional displays. In the context of craniosynostosis, mixed-reality has the potential to improve preoperative planning by enhancing three-dimensional comprehension of complex craniofacial anatomy and reducing planning time.

For readers interested in learning more about the mixed-reality technology discussed in the lecture, including Microsoft HoloLens and its applications in surgical planning, please refer to the following resource: [https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12233888/].

11 Policy and advocacy from the professional perspective

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):11

Moderators: Dr. Mark Proctor and Dr. Patrícia Dástoli

11.1 Experience in global surgery

John G. Meara1,2,3

1Program in Global Surgery and Social Change, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA; 2Department of Plastic and Oral Surgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA; 3Department of Surgery, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):11.1

Global Surgery

Approximately 5 billion people worldwide lack access to safe, timely, and affordable surgical and anesthesia care. It is estimated that low—and middle-income countries fall short by 143 million surgeries each year. By 2030, there will be a need for 1.27 million specialists in surgery, anesthesia, and obstetrics. A lack of investment in this sector could lead to a loss of $12.3 trillion in global productivity between 2015 and 2030.

On June 29, 1980, Dr. Mahler, then Director-General of the WHO, highlighted the critical importance of surgical care in public health at the World Congress of the International College of Surgeons in Mexico, describing it as a sharp reflection of health inequality.1 Nearly three decades later, in 2008, Drs. Paul Farmer and Jim Kim reiterated this notion, labeling surgery as public health’s ‘neglected stepchild’.2

In 2015, three foundational documents for global surgery were published: a chapter dedicated to surgery in the third edition of the World Bank’s book on disease prevention and control; the Lancet Global Surgery Commission report (LCoGS); and World Health Commission Resolution 68.15.3–5 These texts lay the groundwork for future discussions and strategies. Later, Dr. Tedros, Director of the WHO, emphasized in 2019 the essentiality of surgery in achieving universal health coverage, urging countries to invest in this field.

LCoGS’s vision is to ensure universal access to safe and affordable surgical, obstetric, and anesthesia care, recognizing them as indispensable rights and inseparable components of healthcare.

The Program in Global Surgery and Social Change Impact

The Program in Global Surgery and Social Change was involved in the creation of LCoGS and focused on strengthening surgical systems globally, promoting social justice and equity, zero tolerance for discrimination, support, and solidarity.6 This program advocates for evidence-based innovation and implementation.

Its pillars include research, policy, advocacy and leadership training in global surgery through equitable collaborations. Research focuses on the intersection of surgery and public health, using data, statistics, and developing models to visualize access to surgical care.

The program collaborates with local leaders and organizations, building local capacities and strengthening surgical systems. It also works with governments and high-level stakeholders to integrate surgical care through National Surgical Plans.7

NSOAPs are policy frameworks that offer a systematic approach for governments to strengthen their surgical systems. These plans are strategically designed for integration into national health policies, ensuring broad implementation and scalability.7 NSOAPs address six health system building blocks: infrastructure, service delivery, information management, workforce, financing, and governance.7 Worldwide, over 40 countries are in different stages of the NSOAP process, with 11 countries in the implementation phase and more than 11 others currently in the developmental phase.7

Notably, in recent years, the National Plan for Surgical Strengthening in Ecuador was designed under the leadership of the Vice Presidency of the Republic and the Ministry of Public Health in collaboration with Harvard University and the Pontifical Catholic University of Ecuador. This plan identified critical gaps in national surgical care and promoted healthcare restructuring to ensure that Ecuadorians can access timely, high-quality, and safe surgeries. As a result, Ecuador became the first country in the Americas to implement such a pioneering initiative, inspiring healthcare system improvements throughout the region.

The availability of safe, timely, and affordable surgical care is essential for achieving UHC and ensuring equity in healthcare. Surgical care is a fundamental component of a well-functioning health system, impacting emergency care, maternal and child health, non-communicable diseases, and injury management. Therefore, it is indispensable to integrate surgical care into public health dialogues, recognizing and addressing barriers such as geographic disparities, workforce shortages, and financial constraints. By prioritizing surgical care, we can build resilient health systems that deliver comprehensive care to all.

11.2 Advancing equitable access to surgical care in Latin America: the imperative of public policies and collaborative Action. The case of Ecuador

Alfredo Borrero Vega1,2

1Latin American Health Corporation; 2 Ecuador (2021–2023)

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):11.2

In recent years, the discussion on global surgery and its impact on the lives of millions of patients has gained an important momentum worldwide. This brings much hope, but the discussion has not yet been impactful enough to address reality.

We know that 5 billion people globally lack timely access to safe surgical and anesthesia care when needed, translating to 364 million in Latin America alone.1 Additionally, the Lancet Commission estimates low- and middle-income countries will suffer a $12.3 trillion economic productivity loss due to surgical conditions by 2030.2

Since 2015, there has been growing interest from governments, ministries, multinational organizations, professional societies, and doctors in increasing access to surgical, obstetric, trauma, and anesthesia (SOTA) services. This has somehow allowed that efforts in 48 countries ended in the development of National Surgical Strengthening Plans integrated into national health policies to ensure nationwide implementation and scaling. However, only one of them corresponds to Latin America.3,4

Therefore, considering the indicators of gaps and the quantity and quality of existing policies in Latin America, we can say that the absence of public policies in Latin America with a decisive focus on guaranteeing equitable access to SOTA and ECO care, influences directly and negatively the disparity in surgical care, limiting timely treatment, exacerbating health inequities, and increasing the risks of preventable mortality.1

Public policies play a crucial role in addressing the gaps in access to global surgery for several reasons:Equitable Distribution: Public policies can ensure that surgical services are distributed equitably across regions, populations, and socioeconomic groups. By targeting resources to underserved areas and marginalized communities, policies can help bridge the gap in access to surgical care;Resource Allocation: Effective public policies allocate resources, including funding, infrastructure, and healthcare personnel, to areas where they are most needed. This ensures that essential surgical services are available to all individuals, regardless of their geographic location or financial status;Regulatory Frameworks: Public policies establish regulatory frameworks and standards for surgical practice, ensuring that procedures are performed safely, ethically, and with high quality. Regulations can also promote the adoption of evidence-based practices and the use of appropriate technologies to improve surgical outcome;Health Systems Strengthening: Policies aimed at strengthening health systems can enhance the overall capacity of healthcare systems to deliver surgical services. This may involve investments in training programs for healthcare providers, improvements in infrastructure and equipment, and the development of referral networks for complex surgical cases;Data Collection and Monitoring: Public policies can mandate the collection of data on surgical outcomes, patient demographics, and access to care. This information is essential for identifying gaps in service delivery, monitoring progress over time, and guiding future policy interventions;Advocacy and Awareness: Public policies can raise awareness about the importance of surgical care as an essential component of healthcare. By advocating for the inclusion of surgical services in national and global health agendas, policies can mobilize support and resources to address the disparities in access to surgery.

Equitable Distribution: Public policies can ensure that surgical services are distributed equitably across regions, populations, and socioeconomic groups. By targeting resources to underserved areas and marginalized communities, policies can help bridge the gap in access to surgical care;

Resource Allocation: Effective public policies allocate resources, including funding, infrastructure, and healthcare personnel, to areas where they are most needed. This ensures that essential surgical services are available to all individuals, regardless of their geographic location or financial status;

By advocating for the inclusion of surgical services in national and global health agendas, policies can mobilize support and resources to address the disparities in access to surgery.

Therefore, increasing the amount of global surgery sensible policies is the main goal to enable many of the essential foundations of the aspired equity. Truly believing on this, we established for ourselves in CLS a goal in terms of policy: Promote an enabling environment, in terms of public policy, in the Americas region, favoring strengthened and coordinated intervention on behalf of access to surgery, obstetrics, trauma, and anesthesia services as well as emerging, critical, and operative care.

For that, we would draw upon Ecuador’s own experience, which I had the honor of leading during my tenure as Vice President of the Republic of Ecuador. With a clear vision and a strategic alliance with the Global Surgery and Social Change Program Ecuador designed its Surgical, Obstetric, Anesthetic, and Trauma Strengthening Plan. Deep research and information gathering processes were developed, including 4 studies comprising qualitative, quantitative, and ethnographic approaches across 202 healthcare facilities and various hard-to-reach communities. Furthermore, in December 2022, a gathering of diverse stakeholders from the surgical ecosystem, including private and public sectors, academia, international organizations, and civil society, convened to foster participatory spaces for policy development and priority setting through ongoing public policy processes. With these inputs, national and international interinstitutional teams were formed for plan drafting and subsequent validation. This process culminated in the policy launch in Ecuador on November 13, 2023, marking a milestone in our country’s health history and equitable access to healthcare services.5,6 It also stands as the first policy to integrate all stakeholders, including users, in designing a health policy.

During this process, we faced the challenge of strategically planning and foreseeing the policy’s sustainability foundations, thereby ensuring its implementation and financing. Costing and budgeting were conducted to identify gaps and the state’s financial implications to cover them. Subsequently, Ecuadorian representatives initiated a significant management and coordination process to engage potential partners and allies in achieving these objectives. Several approaches were made to international cooperation, resulting in many collaborative alliances.

But beyond the excellent technical work carried out and the benefits we knew a policy would entail in addressing a public issue, it’s worth highlighting the decisive benefit that the policy design process itself had, demonstrating tangible results from that point forward: The opening of 42 new operating rooms nationwide; The strengthen of diagnostic support services, 220 laboratories, acquisition of 8 tomographs and 2 resonators; Repair and improvement of the ambulance boat Acquisition of 186 ambulances and 123 more in planning; Investment 2022–2023 Infrastructure: $26.7 mm; Equipment: $98.8 mm (52 anesthesia machines, 83 monitors, 31 surgical tables, laparoscopic towers, electric scalpel and CBows, among others); Workforce: 6,785 linked to an investment amount of 79.5 mm, of which 4,281 destined for surgical areas.

We are definitely full of hope regarding the rest of the changes in the oncoming years. However, the challenge is still significant, and if anything, the global health context has taught us in recent years, it is that we cannot change reality alone. Thus, we embarked on an even more ambitious challenge: To advocate for a regional policy at Pan American Health Organization.

It is imperative that all Latin American countries work together to collectively build solid surgical systems in the region. This is done by developing political commitments; mutual knowledge and information transfer, resource sharing, advocating for funding and celebrating mutual achievements.

In September 2023, Ecuador motioned an initiative in the 173rd PAHO Executive Committee, with the purpose of forming a Working Group to collect information and prepare a proposed resolution, which allows equitable access to surgery, obstetrics and anesthesia services to be included in the regional public policy agenda as a priority to achieve universal health coverage, in compliance with Resolution WHA68.15.7

As we stand on the threshold of a new era marked by unprecedented global challenges, it is imperative that we come together as nations and stakeholders to address issues of public policy relevance with unwavering determination and collective action.

Today, we find ourselves at a critical juncture where the need for effective public policies has never been more pressing.

More than ever, we must recognize the power and potential of public policy to affect meaningful change and drive progress towards a more equitable and sustainable future for all. It is through strategic and inclusive policymaking that we can confront the complex challenges facing our societies and chart a course towards a brighter tomorrow.

However, the task ahead of us is daunting, and the road to meaningful policy reform is fraught with obstacles and complexities. Yet, the core goal deserves action.

Therefore, I urge the medical community, academia, decision-makers, civil society, and everyone, to heed the call to action and join hands in a spirit of collaboration. We possess the knowledge, resources, and the collective will to effect positive change and promote more policies to shape a future that is just, inclusive, and sustainable for generations to come.

11.3 Are syndromic craniosynostosis considered rare diseases according to Brazilian legislation? Benefits of laws for people with rare diseases that can benefit children with CS

Goldete Priszkulnik1,2,3

1Brazilian Society of Medical Audit, Brazil; 2The Brazilian Journal of Medical Audit, Brazil; 3 Institutional Affairs at the International Society for Pharmacoeconomics and Outcomes Research—ISPOR—Brazil Chapter

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):11.3

According to the World Health Organization, a rare disease affects fewer than 65 people per 100,000, which is approximately 1.3 people for every 2,000 individuals. These are degenerative, chronic, progressive, and disabling diseases. The exact number of diseases considered rare is not known. It is estimated that there are between 6,000 and 8,000 different types. Eighty percent (80%) of these result from genetic factors, while the rest are due to environmental, infectious, immunological, and other causes.1

In Brazil, a disease is considered rare when it affects 65 individuals in every group of 100,000 people. It is estimated that at least 13 million people are affected by rare diseases in the country.1

One of the greatest challenges in this vast nation is ensuring equal access for all through the Unified Health System (SUS), which must provide for the entire population from primary care to quaternary treatment, including transplants, cancer, and high-cost medications.2

The Brazilian Ministry of Health has established a public policy to organize the SUS to care for people with rare diseases and to enable the accreditation of specialized services in rare diseases throughout the country, in addition to supporting a network of care for people with disabilities.3

The Brazilian Ministry of Health prioritizes: 1) Structuring care in an integrated and coordinated manner at all levels, from prevention, reception, diagnosis, and treatment (based on clinical protocols and therapeutic guidelines) to support, resolution, follow-up, and rehabilitation; 2) Access to diagnostic and therapeutic resources; 3) Access to information and care; 4) Genetic Counseling (GC), when indicated.

Beyond the Brazilian Ministry of Health’s guidelines and public health policies, Brazil has a policy for rare disease care that includes a range of benefits, rights, and tax exemptions.4

Rights of Patients with Rare Diseases

Patients with a rare disease are entitled to retirement benefits: 1) Due to age, specific contributions, or disability; 2) Sickness benefits are available if the inability to work is proven; 3) Transportation Rights for Patients with Rare Diseases—Free or discounted use of public transportation; Use of special parking spaces in public and commercial areas; 4) Entertainment Benefits for Patients with Rare Diseases—Half-price tickets for cinemas, film clubs, theaters, musical and circus performances, and educational and sports events. This benefit applies to both the patient and a companion. 5) Access to withdrawal from FGTS and PIS/PASEP funds; 6) Exemption from Income Tax (for specific diseases); 7) Exemption from vehicle taxes such as IPVA, ICMS, IPI, and IOF when purchasing an adapted vehicle, if the disease prevents driving a standard vehicle; 8) Mortgage discharge.

By considering syndromic craniosynostosis as a rare disease within Brazil’s rare disease policy, we can legally ensure for the patients and their families a series of tax benefits and exemptions that assist in their care journey, whether through easier access to specific medications or the possibility of having an adapted vehicle or a paid-off mortgage.

It is the responsibility of authorized specialized services to guide patients and their families about their rights and obligations according to local characteristics (states and municipalities) to make the care journey less exhausting and burdensome.”

12 Policy and advocacy from the society’s perspective. The Patient’s Journey

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):12

Moderators: Dr. Helena Romcy and Dr. Melissa Antoneli

12.1 A patient with Apert syndrome—João’s Journey

Igor Cunha, Natalia Jereissati

Patient Family Partner, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):12.1

“And destiny surprised us.

After an uneventful pregnancy, our third child was born. Fingers and toes stuck together; the impact could not have been different: perplexity for everyone in the delivery room. As we watched the nurses take him to the ICU, we asked unanswered questions. The silence was broken on the third day with the diagnosis of Apert Syndrome, a rare genetic condition in which, typically, the bones of the skull and extremities fuse during pregnancy.

Heart, digestive, visual, hearing and cognitive problems were among possible complications that could occur. Given our ignorance and mental confusion, the first week was one of paralysis. I dedicated myself to the ICU schedule for 10 days for feedings and the professional resources offered for my mental stability. As we don’t live in our hometowns, little by little, parents, siblings, friends arrived in São Paulo to support us and better understand the syndrome.

At some point, we received the news that our son would undergo several surgeries. The first of these would have to be in the next few months to release the skull bones. Due to the magnitude of the intervention, he would have to reach a minimum weight and spend a few days in the ICU, in addition to preparing for a blood transfusion. We spoke to each sub-specialist separately. Despite the care everyone took with us, the doctors’ experiences varied between just a few cases and literature knowledge. The information arrived fragmented and misaligned.

We felt as if we were in an immense darkness.

Meanwhile, our dedicated support network formed a search effort to acquire more knowledge. And so, we found a hospital in Boston that, in addition to being a large research center, offered a multidisciplinary approach. One of the first materials that we had access to was the typical patient’s journey with a surgery timeline, a piece of clarifying information. After conversations and exams, the medical team at Boston proposed a much less invasive technique for this first intervention. However, it would have to be done until the third month of life, and, subsequently, my son would have to wear a helmet to shape his head for 9 months, which would force us to move to America. We made our decision and then got goosebumps when we looked at the illustration randomly chosen for the painting in the maternity ward: a house being carried by the wind by a large balloon to which a fluttering flag with the name João was tied. It seemed like an unconscious premonition.

The fact is that there was no time to waste. We set the date for the surgery, packed some bags with basic clothes, and set off, without any planning, on a great life adventure with our three children. The first year was full of uncertainty. As an example, we only had the confirmation that our son would be able to see around the sixth month. Nor would we have quick answers for João’s entire development. We dove into reading neuroscience books and dedicated ourselves on trying different stimulation techniques. Looking back, it was a true exercise in faith and hope.

After 6 years, one of the most interesting aspects was watching João’s strong and resilient personality blossom. At 4 he was already negotiating with us the exact way he wanted to receive anesthesia, always appearing conscious and calm before entering the operating room. During this same period, he underwent a major intervention to correct the position of his forehead. He spent a few days sleeping in the hospital. When he was released, the nurses helped him to get up cautiously. He played with them, rolled around, clapped his hands, looked at me and said: “I’m alive!”.

Another time, after a 4-month sequence with his arms and hands immobilized to correct his thumbs, he told us that he wanted to go back to studying. It was November, the end of the first school semester in the Northern Hemisphere. The school was not sure about accepting him but gave him a 1-day experience to evaluate his adaptation. The older brother was quick to give tips: Don’t hit anyone, lend toys, and hug new friends. We were all anxious when we returned to pick him up. When he got into the car, he was categorical: “I did everything right!” He must have done so, as the approval arrived immediately.

We continue, therefore, to admire the naturalness with which João approaches and overcomes each stage. We still have many challenges, but we also have many achievements to celebrate, which motivated us to somehow help other families. We’ve always had access to wonderful doctors; We’ve had the means to the best of what the health system could offer us in Brazil; We’ve also had a fantastic support network of family and friends by our side. Nevertheless, we felt hopeless with so little information when my son was born. At first, we started by asking the hospital at Boston to have its website content translated to Portuguese. We thought it would be a good source of knowledge with easy access to anyone in Brazil. Then, after many conversations with Dr. Meara, Plastic Surgeon-in-Chief at Boston Children’s Hospital, the idea of a Symposium came up.

Today this dream becomes reality. After a pandemic and years later, Boston Children’s Hospital partnered with Hospital for the Rehabilitation of Craniofacial Anomalies (HRAC-USP) in Brazil and together we built this dream: The first international Craniosynostosis Symposium in the country. The main goals are: 1) To raise awareness to Apert and other syndromic craniosynostosis conditions through major media vehicles in a national campaign; 2) To gather the related international medical community to exchange experience and protocols; 3) To gather patient and family organizations, governmental institutions and related stakeholders to understand the health system gaps in Brazil associated with craniofacial syndromes to create a more organized ecosystem; 4) To sensitize the international medical community to raise efforts for more collaborative research.

It is necessary to talk about Apert and related syndromes, which are still so little known in Brazil. It is necessary to ensure that information reaches affected families and leads to a more hopeful future. Apert Syndrome is a detail in my son’s life. We hope that this could also be true to other kids in a similar condition. And, yes, fate surprised us. But today when we look back it is impossible not to think: Maktub!

João, you were meant to be OURS!”.

Ethical Acknowledgment

The patient’s guardians wrote this section as presented during the symposium and gave their informed and explicit consent to publish their information in an open access online journal.

12.2 The influence of parents associations in health public policies

Graciela Mendanha Sobrinho

Patient Family Partner and President of Apert’s Brazilian Society, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):12.2

Introduction

Active involvement by parent organizations has been a vital force in shaping effective public health policies, particularly in supporting families of children with rare diseases. My personal experience as the mother of Isadora Maria Teodora, who was born with Apert Syndrome, underscored the need to create a structured and compassionate environment of care, not just for our family, but for others navigating similar challenges.

Diagnosis and First Steps

Throughout my pregnancy, all prenatal tests up to the seventh month indicated normal fetal development. However, a follow-up ultrasound drastically changed everything.

At the time, I had just been hospitalized for a double J catheter placement due to a kidney issue. After discharge, I underwent the ultrasound, which revealed concerning findings: absence of fingers, foot abnormalities, and macrocephaly. A second opinion brought even more alarming news—this physician suggested my daughter had only one kidney, would experience stunted development, and lacked ears and hands. She explained it would take at least a week to reach a possible diagnosis. In the meantime, we were left without answers.

We were living in Maringá, Paraná, but the case was referred to Curitiba, the state’s capital, where specialized care was more accessible. Fortunately, my husband was already based in Curitiba for work, which made the transition manageable.

The Journey for Treatment

In her first days of life, Isadora spent five days in a neonatal ICU, under observation. Despite the level of care in Curitiba, no one there could confirm her diagnosis. We began consulting neurosurgeons and geneticists in search of answers.

One pediatric neurosurgeon advised us to return once Isadora turned one and weighed at least eight kilograms. Unwilling to wait, we continued searching until we found CAIFE (Craniofacial Center), which referred us to Campinas. At that point, I had never met another child with Apert Syndrome.

Dr. Renato Freitas at CAIFE introduced us to Dr. Cássio in Campinas. Although relocation was daunting, we received unexpected help, my husband’s employer offered financial support, making the move possible.

In Campinas, Isadora began an intensive treatment schedule, undergoing surgeries every two months. By the time she was one year old, she had already received hand, foot, and cranial surgeries. Along this journey, we encountered painful experiences, including insensitive remarks from healthcare professionals, such as “you’ll save on manicures” and claims that “nothing could be done” about her hands.

The Founding of the Brazilian Association for Apert Syndrome

These experiences made it clear that we needed to build a support system for other families. In 2018, we founded the Brazilian Association for Apert Syndrome, dedicated to providing assistance, sharing information, and connecting families with qualified specialists.

The Role of Parent Associations in Public Health Policy

Parent-led organizations have played an increasingly important role in influencing public health policies, especially in the context of rare diseases. Our initiatives include:Data Collection and Statistics: We developed a nationwide questionnaire to document Apert Syndrome cases in Brazil, compiling data on comorbidities, access to care, and quality of life.Support Networks: We organize meetings between families and medical professionals to promote shared experiences, emotional support, and community solidarity.Advocacy and Public Engagement: We engage with policymakers, public officials, and healthcare workers to emphasize the importance of early diagnosis and coordinated multidisciplinary care.Knowledge Dissemination: We use social media and public platforms to raise awareness about Apert Syndrome and its specific treatment needs.

Data Collection and Statistics: We developed a nationwide questionnaire to document Apert Syndrome cases in Brazil, compiling data on comorbidities, access to care, and quality of life.

Data Collection and Statistics: We developed a nationwide questionnaire to document Apert Syndrome cases in Brazil, compiling data on comorbidities, access to care, and quality of life.

Support Networks: We organize meetings between families and medical professionals to promote shared experiences, emotional support, and community solidarity.

Support Networks: We organize meetings between families and medical professionals to promote shared experiences, emotional support, and community solidarity.

Advocacy and Public Engagement: We engage with policymakers, public officials, and healthcare workers to emphasize the importance of early diagnosis and coordinated multidisciplinary care.

Advocacy and Public Engagement: We engage with policymakers, public officials, and healthcare workers to emphasize the importance of early diagnosis and coordinated multidisciplinary care.

Knowledge Dissemination: We use social media and public platforms to raise awareness about Apert Syndrome and its specific treatment needs.

Knowledge Dissemination: We use social media and public platforms to raise awareness about Apert Syndrome and its specific treatment needs.

Conclusion

Parent organizations play a fundamental role in shaping more equitable and effective public health systems. Through advocacy, education, and collaboration, we can improve the lives of children with rare diseases and their families across Brazil.

I leave you with a personal reflection: special children are not born to special parents. They enter our lives to make us stronger, more resilient, and more committed to changing realities.

Acknowledgments

My deepest gratitude to every person who contributes to this mission, to the healthcare professionals who offer their expertise and to the families who share their stories and strengthen this network of care.

12.3 The journey from necessity to effective change in public changes

Denise Rodrigues Eloi

Instituto Coalizão Saúde, ICOS, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):12.3

This symposium itself is already a significant leap forward in promoting better care for craniosynostosis patients. However, our efforts do not end here; this is just the first step in a long path toward improving the quality of care. From the rich content produced during our discussions, we plan to advance public policy conversations and propose innovative enhancements to the lines of care for patients with craniofacial anomalies.

Beyond continuing what we have already set in motion, raising awareness, sharing knowledge, and providing visibility, we will mobilize resources so that patients can receive the right treatment at the right time, whether in the public or private system. We hope that this initiative contributes to democratizing healthcare access in our country, and we encourage everyone to remain engaged in this ongoing cause.

13 Registration of patients with syndromic craniosynostosis: how it is done and how to improve

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):13

Moderators: Dr. Eduardo Jucá and Dr. Claudia Rodriguez

13.1 Mapping to know! How is syndromic craniosynostosis registered? What statistics do we have?

Miyuki Goto

Brazilian Medical Association, Brazil

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):13.1

Introduction

Accurate data collection and early detection are essential for improving outcomes in craniosynostosis care. Currently, data on craniosynostosis in Brazil is limited by underreporting, misclassification, and fragmented health information systems. A stronger emphasis on multidisciplinary training, standardized coding, and integrated surveillance systems is required to better identify, track, and support affected patients—starting from the prenatal period.

Limitations of Current Classification Systems

Craniosynostosis is currently coded using ICD-10 (CID-10) in Brazil’s national health database (DATASUS). However, the World Health Organization (WHO) introduced ICD-11 in 2022, and Brazil plans to adopt it by 2025. This transition is expected to improve diagnostic specificity, particularly for syndromic craniosynostosis, which is often underclassified under ICD-10.

In the current system, lack of granularity in diagnostic codes leads to:→Underreporting of complex or syndromic cases→Inaccurate estimates of disease burden→Challenges in identifying patients in administrative and epidemiological datasets

→Underreporting of complex or syndromic cases

Underreporting of complex or syndromic cases

→Inaccurate estimates of disease burden

Inaccurate estimates of disease burden

→Challenges in identifying patients in administrative and epidemiological datasets

Challenges in identifying patients in administrative and epidemiological datasets

These limitations hinder policymaking, funding allocation, and service planning.

Plataforma IVISQ and New Data Approaches

To address these gaps, we developed the Plataforma IVISQ—a surveillance and research tool designed to integrate data on:Prenatal recordsMaternal educationBirth outcomes

Prenatal records

Prenatal records

Maternal education

Maternal education

Birth outcomes

Birth outcomes

Using IBGE population estimates, we can analyze the proportion of recorded craniosynostosis cases among total births. However, subnotification remains a major barrier. Many children are not diagnosed early or never reach specialized surgical services, leading to an incomplete national picture.

DATASUS and Private Sector Data Gaps

DATASUS captures public healthcare procedures and diagnoses, but code incompatibility between diagnostic and procedural entries requires reconciliation by epidemiologists and health data analysts. In the private healthcare sector, data from the National Supplementary Health Agency (ANS) face similar limitations in terms of rare disease classification, making it equally difficult to assess national prevalence comprehensively.

Newborn Screening and Early Detection

Current newborn screening programs in Brazil include the heel prick test, red reflex test (olhinho), hearing test (orelhinha), and tongue-tie screening (linguinha). However, none currently screen for craniosynostosis or related syndromic conditions. As a result, many cases go unnoticed in the critical early months.

Future proposals should include:➢Expanding neonatal screening programs➢Incorporating craniofacial markers and genetic testing for early diagnosis➢Training healthcare professionals to recognize early signs prenatally and postnatally

➢Expanding neonatal screening programs

Expanding neonatal screening programs

➢Incorporating craniofacial markers and genetic testing for early diagnosis

Incorporating craniofacial markers and genetic testing for early diagnosis

➢Training healthcare professionals to recognize early signs prenatally and postnatally

Training healthcare professionals to recognize early signs prenatally and postnatally

Conclusion

The upcoming transition to ICD-11, combined with greater awareness across prenatal and neonatal care teams, presents an opportunity to strengthen Brazil’s national data systems. A more robust, integrated registry, spanning both public and private sectors, is critical for accurately tracking the craniosynostosis population. Efforts to improve diagnosis, data interoperability, and early access to care, including the potential introduction of targeted genetic testing, will be key to achieving equitable and timely treatment for affected children.

13.2 Model of a platform for registering rare diseases applied to syndromic craniosynostosis

Sandra C Facincone1, Claudia Verde Rodriguez2, Alana Moura1

1Clinical Development, RARAS CRO; 2RARAS CRO; 3Patient Advocacy, RARAS CRO

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):13.2

Patient registries play a crucial role in overcoming research limitations inherent in the study of rare diseases, where patient numbers are typically small. A patient registry is an organized system that uses observational study methods to collect uniform and structured data (clinical and other) to evaluate specified outcomes for a population defined by a particular disease, condition, or exposure and that serves one or more objectives (scientific, clinical or policy purposes).1

In this summary, we explore registry platform models and how to anticipate the challenges related to the design, data collection, quality management, and long-term maintenance of rare disease registries.

Introduction

More than 7.000 rare diseases are identified, more than 90% with no approved treatment. Furthermore, many rare conditions still lack clinical knowledge in the medical literature and present a delay until the correct diagnosis.2 Our focus is on addressing the challenges rare disease registries face to build a meaningful registry platform capturing data that most impacts the patient´s life. Especially for rare diseases where clinical trials are often of small size and data are subject to high variability, registries provide a valuable data source to help understand the course of a disease and improve patient care.3

In this context, it is crucial to emphasize that the history of each family is very important and must be recognized as unique in their peculiarities.

The construction of a patient registry follows fundamental points such as establishing the registry goals (short and long term), determining the target population (one specific disease or a group of disorders), and the specifics of each disease. Furthermore, it is essential to have the registry protocol, policies, a well-crafted data management plan, and to determine the budget and mechanisms for sustainability. It is essential to decide which platform to use and the format and design of the data collection tools.4

Key Points

Design and Setup to build a successful platform.

Design and Setup to build a successful platform.

Design and Setup to build a successful platform.

We should consider the data quality, privacy, ownership, registry management, and coordination. Few reports described details regarding quality management (51%) and maintenance (46%) and focused on data confidentiality and ethical use.4 Most registries recruit clinical patients (92%) with informed consent (81%) and protect the collected data (76%). Prepare in conjunction with a board of specialists and the patient´s advocacy groups (PAG) a robust plan to manage which data can be shared and with whom. The guidance from an “Advisory board” along the process (before, during, and after) the process: 1) Specialists in the field trained in building and validating survey methods to be utilized; 2) Ensuring the balance between registration and data collection completeness and the goals set forth; 3) Prioritize data collection – which data to collect at each stage of maturation. It is needed to ponderate existing tools and resources already available for standardizing data collection.

2.Sustainability and Management

2.Sustainability and Management

Sustainability and Management

To achieve the objectives, it is necessary to create mechanisms for sustainability using different stakeholders as long-term funding is crucial to maintain a registry running.

Funding and partnerships: Grants (private, government); Donors, fundraising; Partnering with industry/academia; Choose if fees may be applicable to have access to registry data.

Discussion

Different types of rare registry platforms are currently active covering a wide type of diseases. The United States of America (USA) and the European Union (EU) are leading entities with several platforms available for use, each with different strengths, limitations, and costs.

In Brazil, we also have some examples, such as the Brazilian Rare Diseases Network (40 participating institutions), SIDORA (Paraná), RevELA Project: National Registry of Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis, among other platforms. Most of these Brazilian projects are in partnership with the government and researchers’ institutions, and some of them are focused on providing prevalence and incidence data and demographic factors. However, a quick search in the literature can show us that the current registries are still largely behind in complying with the 2013 guidelines on patient registration and data collection, and the field of rare disease registries has made limited improvements in the past decade.5

To tackle this unmet need, we created a platform where the patient associations for rare diseases participate in their registries. The goal is to be patient-centered and inclusive of families and different conditions. The structure of this platform was based on three pillars: Infrastructure, Data Collection and Question Library. The platform is cloud-based, has Security and Privacy policies, and was translated to multiple languages.

The data collection is structured around a question library that prioritizes what affects the most patients with rare diseases. Through data curation, these questions can be adapted according to usability feedback from associations.

The value of rare disease data can only be maximized if it is not stored in siloed repositories. Exclusive data ownership (industry, government, or private institutions, for example) is not possible anymore since the focus of registries should not be solely on clinical trials but on the journey and necessities of these patients.

Furthermore, it is imperative to create a system that can return the results/data to families immediately (without waiting for data to be published and charging fees for access).

Conclusion

The value of building a centralized registry in the patients is based on returning the data to the families, which is essential for improving the care and safety of the patients. Participating in the registry empowers parents/families to inform their physicians about the disease/syndrome and the specific conditions that might be affecting the patient and others. Collaborative efforts, data sharing, and patient engagement are critical to meaningful results.

Beardmore Division of Pediatric Surgery, The Montreal Children’s Hospital, McGill University Health Centre, Montreal, Quebec, Canada; 10Postgraduate Program in Rehabilitation Sciences, Hospital de Reabilitação de Anomalias Craniofaciais, Universidade de São Paulo, Bauru, SP, Brazil; 11Centro Universitário São Camilo, São Paulo, SP, Brazil; 12Instituto de Educação Médica (IDOMED/Estácio), Vista Carioca Campus, Rio de Janeiro, RJ, Brazil; 13Kursk State Medical University, Kursk, Kurskaya Oblast, Russia; 14Universidade São Francisco, Bragança Paulista, SP, Brazil; 15Department of Neurosurgery, Boston Children’s Hospital, Boston, MA, USA

BMC Proceedings 2026, 20(18):

Syndromic craniosynostosis is a rare disease with challenging manifestations and complex treatment requirements, where timing plays a crucial role. However, treatment management varies based on resources and income, affecting surgical outcomes and ultimately impacting the care quality of the patient and their families. The “Bauru International Craniofacial Symposium: Comprehensive Care, Policy, and Advocacy” fostered a powerful and much-needed discussion, highlighting fundamental strategies to address syndromic craniosynostosis and advance surgical innovation nationally and globally.

This event, led by family patient partners and held in collaboration with institutions in Brazil, the United States, and France, involved patients, policymakers, researchers, and interdisciplinary healthcare professionals. It provided an opportunity to advocate for this rare disease as well as to inform, educate, and exchange knowledge. This symposium marks the beginning of potential changes in healthcare delivery for syndromic craniosynostosis and opens the door for similar discussions, education, and policy-making on other rare diseases. Promoting such events and sharing content and gaps is a path toward improving healthcare delivery, strengthening surgical systems, and increasing the quality of care for our patients.
